title: "The Uncharted Waystation"

# Book One: Beyond the Studio Rift

## Chapter 1: Two Names on One Studio Lease

### Part 1: Aisle Four, Cutback Transfers, and Ready for Everything

**Date:** 1999-10-23 at 12:15 America/New_York

![At a sunlit corner table along the brass-and-glass mezzanine railing of the upper-level Food Court inside Emerald Creek Mall beneath its white peaked fabric tent canopy and skylights, twenty-four-year-old Tammy (five-foot-nothing, straight jet-black hair swept behind her shoulders, pastel baby-blue zip-up fleece cardigan pulled open off her shoulders over a modest white ribbed camisole) perches forward on her chair, arches her back, and puffs her chest out across the table as she smiles boldly at twenty-one-year-old Nolan Lennox (five-foot-seven, wearing his royal-blue Blue Tech polo shirt after his morning shift), who grins back innocently over a French fry without looking down from her face while his two single-tube matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches lean cleanly beside his chair on the warm terracotta quarry tile floor.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p001_emerald_creek_food_court_day1.jpg)

#### 12:15 PM — Seventy-Five Ohms and Coaxial Peg Hooks

Fifteen minutes before the end of my Saturday morning shift in Aisle Four at the Emerald Creek Blue Tech, every blister pack of seventy-five-ohm coaxial cable splitters hung flush with the front plastic lip of its peg hook, sorted in ascending order from three-foot patch leads on the left to twenty-five-foot weather-booted runs on the right.

Home-theater cables were my favorite aisle in the whole superstore because hardware specifications never said one thing while secretly meaning the opposite. A threaded F-connector either mated cleanly onto a television tuner jack or it did not. Nobody in Aisle Four dropped unannounced hints, rolled their eyes when I took a literal question at face value, or got impatient if it took my brain two extra seconds to translate noisy retail small talk into plain English. After turning twenty-one that spring and filling out paper applications at eleven different storefronts within crutching distance of Grandpa Walter's two-story house so I wouldn't even need a bus pass to get to work, Blue Tech had been the one place whose floor manager listened to me rattle off memory-bus speeds and dual-deck VCR hookups without caring that my hazel eyes tracked slightly past his ear or that I walked on matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches.

Shifting my weight onto my left silver forearm cuff, I straightened one crooked yellow price tag beside the indoor dipole antennas just as a soft, polite voice spoke behind my elbow.

"Excuse me—sorry to bother you, sir, do you know if the cheap rabbit ears actually pick up anything inside a metal trailer?"

Turning smoothly on my rubber crutch tips, I looked forward at chest height first—and then had to angle my chin noticeably downward.

Even standing in thick, chunky white late-nineties platform sneakers designed to boost her off the linoleum, the young woman smiling up at me couldn't have stood an inch over five feet flat. At five-foot-seven, I was almost never the tall person in a room—my kid sister Gwen had caught me eye-to-eye back in middle school and towered over me at six-foot-one now—yet I stood a good seven inches taller than this customer. Glossy, ruler-straight jet-black hair fell past the shoulders of her soft pastel baby-blue zip-up fleece cardigan, framing a round, rosy-cheeked baby face that made me figure she was twenty or twenty-one like me. Beneath dark lashes, big warm doe-brown eyes met mine with a gentle, unhurried smile, and she didn't flick her gaze down toward the gray open forearm cuffs around my sleeves for even half a second.

"No bother at all—that's actually a really good physics question," I said earnestly, tapping the nine-dollar cardboard box on the bottom peg instead of the forty-dollar amplified dish corporate wanted us pushing on endcaps. "Corrugated aluminum siding on a mobile home acts almost like a Faraday cage against UHF and VHF broadcast waves. Even an expensive amplified antenna won't pull a clean picture if you set it on top of a television against an interior wall. What actually works is buying this plain nine-dollar passive dipole set plus a twelve-foot RG-six coaxial jumper cable for four bucks so you can set the antenna base right on your windowsill against the glass."

Her warm brown eyes widened as if I had just handed her a magic trick instead of saved her twenty-seven dollars.

"Really? You're not even gonna try talking me into the expensive box?" she asked softly, clasping both small hands around the nine-dollar carton. "Thank you so much. Honestly, half the time clerks talk to me like I'm twelve just because I'm short, and I rode the fifteen bus all the way over from Robert Hall Boulevard this morning just to get my TV working."

My whole face lit up at the route number. Bus schedules were the second half of how my brain mapped Tidewater Virginia.

"Wait—from Robert Hall Boulevard to Emerald Creek?" I said, grinning before I could stop myself. "The fifteen doesn't even run single-seat between Robert Hall and here! You had to ride the northbound fifteen toward Naval Station Norfolk up to Military Highway and Old Emerald Creek Road and do the cutback transfer onto the southbound fifteen turning left toward the mall!"

She let out a delighted, musical laugh that crinkled the corners of her round cheeks. "Oh my gosh, yes! Stand out by eight lanes of highway traffic watching cars fly past just to hop on another bus going two minutes down the road! Nobody ever knows how annoying that transfer is unless they ride the bus themselves."

"I walk four blocks from my grandpa's house right behind Emerald Creek Mall," I explained happily as we headed up the center racetrack toward Register Three, my silver forearm crutches clicking a steady, comfortable rhythm beside her platform sneakers, "though anytime my sister Gwen and I head up toward Glenrock Mall or Norfolk with our wheelchairs, we ride the fifteen constantly. I'm Nolan, by the way."

"I'm Tammy," she said warmly, setting the antenna box and twelve-foot coaxial coil gently onto my checkout belt as my twelve-thirty relief cashier stepped up beside the register drawer to take over my lane. Ringing up her thirteen-dollar total and slipping the receipt neatly inside her plastic bag, I glanced down at my digital wristwatch: twelve-twenty-nine. Right on schedule. Four blocks home put me on Grandpa Walter's porch by twelve-forty-five, though Gwen was working through astronomy problem sets at the kitchen table until two and Grandpa didn't take his afternoon heart pills until one-thirty.

Tammy didn't turn toward the automatic exit doors when I handed her the bag. Shifting the plastic handles into her left hand, she tilted her chin up with the most polite, inviting smile I had ever had aimed at me in twenty-one years.

"Are you getting off work right now, Nolan?" Tammy asked gently.

"Yes, ma'am—just finished my morning four-hour shift," I said, unclipping my laminated Blue Tech name badge from my royal-blue polo collar and slipping it into my jeans pocket. "Walking home right past the mall across the street."

"Would you maybe want to grab lunch with me upstairs at the Emerald Creek Mall food court on your way home?" Tammy asked softly, cheeks pinking in a way that looked almost shy. "You just saved me twenty-seven dollars on my antenna—let me at least buy you some fries."

For two full seconds my brain stalled flat between gears while I ran the math. Nobody outside Grandpa Walter or Gwen had ever invited me to eat lunch with them—not in high school, and definitely not a pretty girl with warm brown eyes and polite manners. Checking my mental clock twice—forty-five minutes across Old Emerald Creek Road still got me up Grandpa's front steps by quarter past one, fifteen minutes ahead of Grandpa's medicine routine—I nodded with a slightly crooked, genuine grin.

"Sure," I said. "I'd really like that, Tammy."

***

#### 12:50 PM — Terracotta Tile and Missing Every Hint

Ten minutes later, after crossing the crosswalk from the Blue Tech lot into Emerald Creek Mall and riding the freestanding cream-and-brass scenic glass elevator opposite Wellington's narrow recessed portal flush up to the second-floor terracotta-tile landing, we carried two red plastic trays from Crown Burger over to a quiet square two-top table tucked along a diagonal side wing of the polished brass-and-glass mezzanine railing.

Overhead, bright October daylight poured down through perimeter skylights beneath the high white peaked fabric tent canopy of the food court, warming the eight-by-eight reddish-brown quarry tiles under our shoes while the smell of salted fries and cinnamon sugar from Annie's Pretzels drifted past. Propping both of my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches neatly parallel against the right side of my chair frame where nobody walking along the railing could trip over the gray rubber tips, I sat down across from Tammy, lined my paper napkin parallel to the edge of my tray, and unwrapped my burger.

Tammy proved to be the easiest person to talk to I had ever met. Whenever most neurotypical people made conversation, it felt like trying to catch tennis balls thrown two at a time in the dark—jokes that weren't actually jokes, questions where the honest answer turned out to be rude. Tammy didn't rush me once. She rested her chin lightly on her palm, sipped from her plain yellow fountain cup, and listened with rapt, polite attention while I told her how Grandpa Walter—seventy-seven and still healthy as an ox, retired Navy machinist, sharpest neurotypical common sense in Chesapeake—had stepped into Juvenile and Domestic Relations Court back in the eighties to take full custody of my sister Gwen and me when we were kids, and how Gwen was razor-sharp at math and orbital astronomy and basically my best friend in the whole world.

"That's really sweet, Nolan," Tammy said warmly, smiling across the laminate tabletop without me ever thinking to mention Gwen's age or how tall she stood. "Not a lot of guys talk that nicely about their family."

Setting her cup aside, Tammy reached up with both hands and swept all of her straight, glossy jet-black hair cleanly back behind her shoulders. Next she slid her pastel baby-blue zip-up fleece cardigan wide open, tugging both blue fleece sleeves partway back behind her elbows as she scooted forward onto the very front edge of her chair. Planting both small hands back against the curves of her jeans hips, she arched her lower spine sharply inward, drew her shoulders tight behind her ribcage, and puffed her chest prominently out over the edge of the table inside her fitted white ribbed cotton camisole.

Locking her big, warm doe-brown eyes straight onto mine across our red plastic trays, the very first thing Tammy said was low, steady, and deliberate.

"Just so you know, Nolan... I'm ready for everything."

I didn't glance down at her chest—not once. For one thing, Grandpa Walter had raised me since elementary school to look people respectfully in the face when they spoke to me; for another, my brain had zero wiring for invisible body-language codes, so I didn't have the faintest clue a guy sitting across a mall table was *supposed* to notice why she had arched her back and puffed her chest out like that. If I registered her posture at all, I figured the curved metal rungs on Emerald Creek Mall's food-court chairs were digging into her spine.

Better yet, inside my literal-minded twenty-one-year-old head, her words made complete, wonderful sense right on top of what I had just finished explaining thirty seconds earlier.

*Ready for everything.* She meant my crutches. She meant my manual wheelchair sitting folded in Grandpa's hallway for long outings, waiting thirty seconds on hydraulic bus lifts, riding CVTA transfers instead of owning a car, and knowing Gwen and Grandpa Walter came packaged with my life.

Warm, uncomplicated gratitude washed straight through my chest. Still holding my French fry halfway to my mouth, keeping my gaze politely on her smiling eyes, I grinned from ear to ear.

"Honestly, Tammy, thank you for saying that," I said earnestly. "You have no idea how rare that is. Usually the second somebody realizes crutches aren't for a sprained ankle that heals in two weeks—or that going anywhere far means planning around wheelchair-lift buses—they get real polite and suddenly remember somewhere else they need to be. Knowing none of that bothers you takes a huge weight off."

Across the narrow wood-grain tabletop, Tammy stayed frozen in that arched, shoulders-back pose for two full heartbeats, blinking once as if waiting for me to wink or laugh—before realizing I was one hundred percent serious and had no earthly idea she had just offered to ride back to her trailer off Route 13 and climb into bed with me that very afternoon.

Slowly, an amused, almost fascinated softness curved the corners of her mouth. Letting her shoulders relax an inch forward without losing a drop of that warm, inviting glow in her doe-brown eyes, Tammy pulled a ballpoint pen out of her purse and wrote seven neat digits across the corner of a clean paper napkin.

"You really are the sweetest guy in Chesapeake, Nolan Lennox," Tammy murmured, sliding the folded napkin right beside my red tray. "What evening are you off in the next couple days? Come over to my place off Robert Hall—I'll cook you dinner and show you how well my new antenna works."

"I'm off Monday afternoon," I said happily, tucking the napkin safely into my pocket beside my Blue Tech badge. "Grandpa and Gwen can swing by in Grandpa's car after dinner to pick me up."

Walking the four quiet blocks home from Emerald Creek Mall at one-fifteen in the afternoon—fifteen minutes ahead of Grandpa Walter's pills, right on routine, sun warm on my royal-blue polo sleeves and silver crutches clicking steady down the sidewalk—I couldn't stop smiling to myself, certain I had just met the gentlest, most polite girl in the world.

### Part 2: Four Wooden Steps, Sofa Cuddles, and Your Older Sister?

**Date:** 1999-10-25 at 16:30 America/New_York

![At golden October dusk on the gravel driveway outside Tammy's beige-and-white single-wide mobile home with four wooden steps, taller sixteen-year-old Gwen Lennox (six-foot-one, in a modest burgundy pullover and jeans) wraps her shorter twenty-one-year-old brother Nolan Lennox (five-foot-seven, in a blue plaid flannel shirt and jeans) in their trademark warm greeting hug beside all four of their single-tube matte-silver and matte-charcoal Lofstrand forearm crutches leaning against their healthy seventy-six-year-old grandfather Walter Lennox's parked sedan, while five-foot-nothing Tammy stands at the foot of the wooden steps in her baby-blue cardigan and platform sneakers tilting her head way up in wide-eyed surprise to ask if Gwen is Nolan's older sister, as Grandpa Walter smiles warmly beside the car.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p002_older_sister_trailer_pickup.jpg)

#### 4:30 PM — Four Steep Steps and What Cuddling Literally Means

Four steep pressure-treated wooden steps led up from the rutted gravel lane of Pinecrest Court to the front-middle aluminum storm door of Tammy's rusty old single-wide trailer, which was the exact reason my manual wheelchair was sitting folded back in Grandpa Walter's front foyer near Emerald Creek Mall while I walked in from Robert Hall Boulevard on my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches.

Tammy had warned me politely about those steps on the phone Sunday night so I wouldn't get surprised at the curb—a small, thoughtful detail my rule-oriented brain appreciated more than flowers. Riding CVTA Route 15 out from Emerald Creek on a mild October Monday afternoon had taken the full cutback transfer in reverse: catching the northbound fifteen outside the mall up Old Emerald Creek Road to eight-lane Military Highway, crossing carefully on the walk signal, and boarding the southbound fifteen all the way down to Robert Hall Boulevard before crutching five minutes past gravel lots and weathered mobile-home skirting.

Before I even got my right knuckles raised to knock, the aluminum screen door swung inward and Tammy smiled down at me from the doorway.

Without her chunky white platform sneakers, standing completely barefoot on the patterned beige linoleum in medium-wash jeans and a simple white ribbed cotton spaghetti-strap camisole, Tammy looked even smaller than she had at Blue Tech—five feet flat to the millimeter, the crown of her glossy, straight jet-black hair barely clearing my collarbone as I climbed the four wooden treads onto her threshold and clicked my silver crutches neatly inside.

"You made it!" Tammy beamed, stepping back between the living room on the left and the kitchenette on the right so I had plenty of room to plant both gray rubber tips on the flat linoleum without bumping the doorframe. It was unmistakably a weathered trailer-park rental—rusty exterior seams, yellowed acoustic ceiling panels bearing overlapping rings where the park landlord had patched roof leaks over and over again, and the faint ghost of stale cigarette smoke left behind in the dark wood paneling by whatever tenant had lived here before Tammy moved in recently, since Tammy didn't smoke and nobody in my family, not even Grandpa Walter, had ever touched a cigarette—yet Tammy had scrubbed every counter spotless. "Look over by the front window first—tell me if I hooked it up right!"

Right on the narrow wood-paneled living-room windowsill to the left of the front door, across the room from a white twenty-inch plastic box fan humming on low speed in the rear dinette window because the trailer had no air-conditioning, sat the nine-dollar passive rabbit-ear antenna and twelve-foot coaxial jumper cable from Aisle Four. Over against the far left end wall on a small laminate stand, her thirteen-inch television was pulling in afternoon local news without a single bar of static snow.

"Zero ghosting on channel thirteen," I said happily, inspecting the threaded F-connector finger-tight on the tuner jack. "You routed the coax clean clear of the fan motor too so sixty-hertz interference wouldn't bleed into the picture."

Tammy laughed softly, big warm doe-brown eyes crinkling in her round baby face, and handed me a tall plastic tumbler of sweet iced tea beaded cold with condensation.

"Come sit on the couch and cuddle with me while we talk, Nolan," Tammy said gently, nodding toward her soft floral thrift-store sofa against the wood-paneled wall.

If she had used some vague romantic hint, it would have sailed clean past my ears just like Saturday's lunch line had. *Sit on the couch and cuddle while we talk*, though, was plain, literal English—and cuddling on a couch was something my autistic brain already knew and trusted completely from a lifetime of curling up shoulder-to-shoulder under wool blankets with Gwen on Grandpa's queen-sized pull-out sofa bed downstairs at home, ever since we were little kids visiting Grandpa's un-adapted two-story house from our parents' one-story place and still sharing that sofa bed whenever our legs weren't feeling up to climbing the stairs. Propping both of my matte-silver forearm crutches parallel against the end table within easy reach, I lowered myself onto the left cushion, set my iced tea on a coaster, and lifted my right arm openly so Tammy could settle in beside me.

Tammy didn't push a single thing beyond those exact words.

Tucking both bare feet comfortably underneath her on the center cushion, she leaned gently against my blue plaid flannel shirt, rested her cheek right against my shoulder, and just talked with me like we had all afternoon in the world. Whenever my voice launched into a ten-minute tangent about motherboard bus speeds or why CVTA timed the Glenrock Mall pulse transfers at fifteen and forty-five past the hour, she didn't check the clock, didn't tease me for sounding like an instruction manual, and didn't interrupt once. In quiet, matter-of-fact sentences of her own, she told me she was twenty-four—three years older than me, which startled me because her round face looked barely twenty—and explained how she got by carefully month to month on SSI, Food Stamps, and Medicaid, keeping her bills tiny in this trailer because clocking too many payroll hours anywhere could make the government yank her medical card out from under her overnight.

Around quarter to six, Tammy padded barefoot three paces into the narrow wood-paneled kitchenette, unfolded a small plastic step-stool so her five-foot-nothing reach could pull down a box of spaghetti and a jar of marinara from the upper cabinet above the sink, and boiled dinner right there on her narrow four-burner stove while garlic butter toasted in the oven. Eating across her tiny two-chair formica dinette with the window fan humming softly beside us and zero raised voices anywhere in the room, my shoulders felt looser than they had ever felt inside a stranger's house.

***

#### 7:10 PM — One Greeting Hug and Two Approvals

At ten minutes past seven, twin yellow headlights swept unhurriedly down the gravel lane of Pinecrest Court and eased to a stop beside Tammy's yard, tires crunching quiet over fallen autumn oak leaves.

Right on the dot—because Grandpa Walter respected schedules almost as religiously as I did—Grandpa's clean, well-kept silver-tan four-door sedan idled smooth in park. At seventy-seven years old, Grandpa Walter didn't have an ounce of quit or frailty in him: sharp behind the wheel after dark, steady on his feet in his tan canvas windbreaker and wire-rimmed glasses, still changing his own oil in the two-car garage and fixing hinges around our spacious two-story Emerald Creek house every Saturday.

Slipping into her pastel baby-blue zip-up fleece cardigan against the October dusk breeze and stepping into her chunky white platform sneakers by the door, Tammy followed me politely out onto the top landing while I navigated the four wooden steps one steady tread at a time on my silver forearm crutches—never grabbing my elbow without asking, just standing ready until both of my sneakers met level gravel.

It was Tammy's very first time laying eyes on either Grandpa Walter or Gwen.

Before Grandpa had even finished rounding the back bumper with a friendly wave, the front passenger door swung wide and Gwen stepped effortlessly out onto the gravel with her two tall matte-charcoal Lofstrand forearm crutches.

Seeing my sister after even half a day apart triggered the exact same automatic reflex it had since she was in kindergarten: Nolan and Gwen Lennox *always* greeted each other with a hug. Stepping three strides forward to meet her beside the rear fender, Gwen and I propped all four of our forearm crutches—my shorter matte-silver pair on the left, her taller matte-charcoal pair on the right—cleanly side by side against the sedan's quarter panel. Opening both arms wide, I pulled my sister straight into a tight, warm bear hug, my five-foot-seven chin tucking right into its familiar spot below her collarbone while six-foot-one Gwen wrapped both arms snugly around my shoulders and rested her cheek happily on top of my tousled brown hair.

"Hey, big head," Gwen murmured warmly against my hair, giving me an extra squeeze before we stepped half a pace apart, still smiling. "Have a good dinner?"

"Really good—spaghetti and garlic toast," I grinned, turning halfway toward the wooden trailer steps to introduce them. "Grandpa, Gwenny, this is Tammy."

At the bottom of the four wooden steps, Tammy had stopped completely still on the gravel with both small hands clasped in front of her baby-blue cardigan.

Even standing in her thick white platform soles, five-foot-nothing Tammy had to tilt her round face sharply upward—past my five-foot-seven shoulder, and then another full six inches higher—just to meet Gwen's green-hazel eyes beneath wavy honey-brown curls that towered thirteen inches above Tammy's crown. Blinking twice in genuine, wide-eyed astonishment at the height difference she had just watched in that hug, Tammy smiled up politely.

"Hi! Oh—wait," Tammy asked gently, looking back and forth from my chin to the top of Gwen's head. "Is Gwen your older sister?"

Gwen let out a bright, easy laugh without a trace of self-consciousness, while a proud grin split my face from ear to ear.

"Nope," I said happily. "She's my younger sister. Gwenny's sixteen—five years behind me."

For half a heartbeat Tammy looked almost dizzied craning her neck that far up at a high-school freshman—realizing on the spot that Nolan's *kid* sister stood over a full foot taller than a twenty-four-year-old grown woman—yet she smoothed it seamlessly into the sweetest, most respectful manners imaginable. Stepping briskly across the gravel, Tammy shook Grandpa Walter's calloused machinist hand with both of hers, thanked him politely for driving all the way out toward Robert Hall Boulevard after dark, and told Gwen warmly how proudly I had bragged about her astronomy math at the mall food court on Saturday.

"Pleasure meeting you, Tammy," Grandpa Walter said in his steady baritone, giving a small, unmistakable nod as his sharp neurotypical eyes took in the swept porch steps, the working porch light she had switched on so Gwen and I could see our footing on the gravel, and how unhurriedly she stood talking with us by the car. "Thank you for feeding this boy something besides mall french fries."

"Anytime at all, Mr. Lennox—drive safe getting home," Tammy called softly from the foot of her steps, waving until I had settled into the front passenger seat, Gwen had stretched her long legs comfortably across the back bench behind me, and all four of our Lofstrand forearm crutches lay parallel across the rear floorboard.

Backing smoothly out onto the paved lane toward Robert Hall Boulevard with warm dashboard amber glowing over the steering column, Grandpa Walter glanced once into the rearview mirror and nodded to himself.

"Polite young woman, Nolan," Grandpa said plainly. "Good manners, clean yard, and she waited till you were steady on the gravel before saying a word. Seems like a nice kid."

My shoulders warmed—yet before letting myself celebrate, I turned halfway around over the cloth headrest toward the back seat. As much as I trusted Grandpa Walter, in my mind Gwen's verdict mattered more than anybody else's on Earth. If neurotypical Gwen heard one false note behind someone's voice, I didn't want anything to do with them.

In the glow of a passing streetlight, sixteen-year-old Gwen met my eyes and broke into a genuinely happy, unmistakable smile.

"I really like her, Nolan," Gwen said warmly, reaching forward to squeeze my shoulder once over the seatback. "Even when she thought I was twenty-five, she was super sweet about it, and I haven't seen you look that relaxed around somebody new in forever."

Turning back toward the windshield as Grandpa steered us up toward Military Highway and home to Emerald Creek, my chest felt lighter than air. Grandpa Walter approved, Gwen approved, and nobody had yelled, rushed, or expected me to read minds all evening long.

### Part 3: Booth Fries, First Night Away From Home, and Connecting Day One

**Date:** 1999-10-30 at 13:45 America/New_York

![Inside a sunlit afternoon window booth with burgundy vinyl seats and warm wood-grain trim at the freestanding Crown Burger on South Military Highway in Chesapeake, Tammy (five-foot-nothing, straight jet-black hair past her shoulders, pastel baby-blue zip-up fleece cardigan over a white ribbed camisole) leans warmly on her elbows across plain off-white fountain cups and burger-and-fries baskets smiling at Nolan Lennox (five-foot-seven, wearing his royal-blue Blue Tech polo shirt after his Saturday morning shift), while both of Nolan's single-tube matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches lean cleanly against the wooden booth frame beside him.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p003_crown_burger_south_military.jpg)

#### 1:45 PM — South Military Highway, Booth Fries, and Uncharted Territory

Afternoon October sunlight slanted warm across burgundy vinyl bench cushions and wood-grain laminate inside the freestanding Crown Burger on South Military Highway, glinting off the matte-silver aluminum shafts of my two Lofstrand forearm crutches propped neatly against the end of our window booth.

Exactly one week had passed since Tammy walked up behind my elbow in Aisle Four at Blue Tech asking about rabbit-ear antennas—and five days since Grandpa Walter and Gwen drove out to pick me up after Monday night's spaghetti dinner. Clocking out of my Saturday morning shift at twelve-thirty, I had kept my royal-blue work polo on, caught the northbound CVTA Route 15 outside Emerald Creek Mall up to eight-lane Military Highway, crossed on the pedestrian signal, and ridden the southbound fifteen all the way down toward Robert Hall Boulevard to meet Tammy on her side of Chesapeake.

Across our two plain off-white fountain cups and plastic baskets of crinkle-cut fries, Tammy rested both forearms on the table inside her pastel baby-blue zip-up fleece cardigan, straight jet-black hair framing her round cheeks while her warm doe-brown eyes crinkled at me like I was the best company in Tidewater.

"I still can't get over Monday night," Tammy laughed softly, dipping a fry into ketchup. "Here I am squinting out into my driveway expecting some little kid in braces, and out steps your *younger* sister towering a whole head over both of us like a college volleyball star. Your grandpa is such a gentleman, too."

"Grandpa and Gwenny both really liked you," I said earnestly, warming right up at getting to deliver a one-hundred-percent literal, verified report. "In the car on the way back to Emerald Creek, Grandpa said you had good manners and waited till my crutches were steady on the gravel, and Gwenny said she hadn't seen me look that relaxed around somebody new in forever."

Tammy's smile softened in a way that made my ears feel warm. Taking a slow sip from her straw, she tilted her head slightly across the booth.

"Are you scheduled to open tomorrow morning at Blue Tech, Nolan, or did they give you Sunday off?"

"Off all day Sunday," I said happily. "Don't go back till Monday afternoon."

"Then walk back over to my place with me after we finish eating," Tammy said gently, reaching halfway across the laminate tabletop to brush her fingertips lightly against my knuckles. "We can put a movie tape in the VCR, order a pizza later, and instead of making your seventy-seven-year-old grandpa drive all the way down Military Highway in the dark a second time this week... you could just spend the night at my place and catch the Sunday bus home whenever you wake up."

For three full seconds my chewing stopped dead while every scheduling gear inside my head spun at double speed.

At twenty-one years old, I had never spent a single night anywhere on Earth that wasn't with my family.

Not once. Back when Gwen and I were small and still living with our parents in a one-story house, an overnight trip meant visiting Grandpa Walter's big two-story house near Emerald Creek Mall—where Grandpa unfolded the queen-sized pull-out sofa bed in the downstairs family room near the first-floor full bathroom and tucked Gwen and me in side by side under his wool blanket so our legs wouldn't have to climb the stairs at bedtime. After Grandpa stepped into court to take custody of us, every single night since had been spent either up in my own full-sized bed across the upstairs hall from Gwen and Grandpa, or right back down on that familiar queen sofa bed beside Gwen whenever our legs ached. Other kids went to noisy neighborhood sleepovers or Boy Scout tents; between forearm crutches, wheelchair logistics, and how quickly unfamiliar houses drained my autistic battery, home had been my sole overnight coordinates for twenty-one straight years.

Naturally, my literal brain jumped straight to practical checklist items instead of anything unspoken.

"Oh—uh, I didn't pack a toothbrush or sleep clothes," I blurted honestly, glancing down at my royal-blue Blue Tech polo and jeans. "And if I don't walk through the front door before supper, Grandpa and Gwenny are gonna think a car clipped me crossing Military Highway."

Tammy didn't laugh at me or act like calling home at twenty-one was childish. Instead, she smiled warmly as she stacked our empty fry baskets onto one tray.

"I keep unopened spare toothbrushes under my bathroom sink, a clean t-shirt and gym shorts aren't a problem if you just sleep in your undershirt, and my corded phone hangs right on the kitchen wall," Tammy said smoothly. "You can call your grandpa the minute we get inside."

Checking my mental rulebook twice—Sunday off work, zero night driving for Grandpa, clear phone check-in, first-run Sunday fifteen bus timetable already memorized inside my head—I nodded and reached for my silver forearm crutches beside the booth.

"Okay," I smiled, pulse beating a little faster under my collar. "Let's do that."

***

#### 4:15 PM — Wall-Phone Check-In, Clinic Paperwork, and Turning Out the Lamp

Walking the quiet stretch from South Military Highway into Pinecrest Court took ten unhurried minutes in the mild October sun, my rubber crutch tips clicking steady along the asphalt shoulder beside Tammy's chunky white platform sneakers before I climbed the four steep wooden steps up to her front-middle aluminum storm door.

Stepping inside between the living room on the left and the kitchenette on the right—where yellowed ceiling panels still showed faint rings from leaks the trailer-park landlord had tar-patched over and over again, and a ghost of the previous tenant's old cigarette smoke lingered softly beneath Tammy's lemon pine cleaner—Tammy slipped out of her platform sneakers and blue cardigan barefoot on the patterned beige linoleum and pointed straight to the beige corded phone mounted on the wood-paneled partition wall.

"Go ahead and call Emerald Creek first so nobody worries," she said kindly, filling two glasses of ice water at the sink while the twenty-inch white plastic box fan hummed gently in the rear dinette window.

Grandpa Walter picked up on the second ring back in our sunlit eat-in kitchen. Behind his steady voice, I could hear Gwen clattering measuring spoons across the island—probably baking cinnamon apples—and calling out a cheerful *Tell Tammy hi!* across the room. When I explained plainly that Tammy and I were going to watch a movie and I was going to stay over tonight and ride the Sunday fifteen bus home in the morning so Grandpa wouldn't have to make the night drive down Route 13, Grandpa paused for barely one second—weighing Monday evening's good impression against the plain calendar fact that his grandson had turned twenty-one in the spring—before giving me a calm, trusting *All right, Nolan. Call us in the morning before you head for the bus stop, son.*

Hanging the heavy beige receiver back onto its metal wall cradle left a strange, weightless flutter right underneath my breastbone. I was officially staying the night somewhere on my own.

Through dusk and early evening—with zero trick-or-treaters ever venturing down Pinecrest Court's unlit gravel lane off Route 13—everything felt as easy and unhurried as Monday had been: watching an action cassette on her thirteen-inch living-room television with zero broadcast static, splitting a small cheese pizza across the two-chair Formica dinette, and brushing my teeth with the new wrapped toothbrush she handed me inside the small bathroom off the narrow thirty-two-inch rear hallway.

Around nine-thirty, though, instead of pulling an extra blanket out onto the floral living-room sofa the way my literal brain had quietly half-expected, Tammy switched off the living-room lamp, caught my hand lightly in her small fingers, and led me past the corded wall phone down that narrow wood-paneled rear hallway straight into her single bedroom at the far right end of the trailer.

Without a window air-conditioner anywhere in the mobile home, the small back bedroom felt close and warm even in October, lit only by a tiny amber shade on the nightstand beside Tammy's bed.

Standing just inside the doorway on my matte-silver forearm crutches with nowhere else in the nine-by-twelve room to look except the turned-down mattress, every circuit in my head caught up all at once. My palms went damp around my gray contoured handgrips, my heartbeat thudded straight up into my throat, and honest panic tried to tangle my tongue.

"Tammy—um," I stammered, ears burning hot in the dim lamp glow because pretending to know unspoken scripts was impossible for me. "I like you a whole lot, I just—I want to be honest so I don't mess anything up. I've never actually... been with anybody before."

Tammy didn't tease me, didn't sigh, and didn't look surprised for even half a heartbeat. Stepping barefoot across the carpet until she stood right in front of me—tilting her round face seven inches up to meet my eyes with that calm, patient warmth—she reached over to the top drawer of her dresser and lifted out a neat folded sheaf of printed clinic papers.

"You don't have to be nervous around me, Nolan," Tammy said softly, unfolding the pages under the bedside lamp so my literal, print-trusting eyes could read every header and lab line in plain black ink. "Look right here so you don't have to wonder about anything. These are my long-term birth-control visit notes from my Virginia Medicaid OB/GYN, and right underneath is my full lab panel from the clinic showing I'm completely clean of every STI."

I read both pages word for word because printed documentation calmed static faster than anything on Earth. Tammy folded the papers neatly back into the drawer—never once bringing up a condom or even thinking to ask about my past history, which only worked out safely for both of us because she really was my very first partner and I was completely clean too.

Gently taking my left and right matte-silver Lofstrand crutches one at a time, Tammy propped both shafts parallel and upright within easy arm's reach beside the dresser, helped me sit comfortably on the edge of the mattress so I never had to balance awkwardly on one foot, reached down to the hem of her white ribbed spaghetti-strap camisole, and clicked off the bedside lamp.

[Note to the AI: Fade to black here for physical intimacy between Tammy and Nolan during Nolan's second visit to her trailer and his first night ever spent away from family. For safety compliance, write strictly around the sex scene with zero explicit description and do not generate any artwork depicting nudity or sexual activity; the narrative resumes afterward in quiet post-scene conversation.]

***

#### 10:35 PM — Connecting Day One and Unfamiliar Ceilings

Forty minutes later, in the quiet dark of the back bedroom with a thin cotton sheet pulled up across us and October crickets chirping steadily outside the rear screen window, the distant hum of the twenty-inch box fan down the narrow hallway sounded like gentle white noise.

Staring straight up at the shadowy seams of Tammy's water-patched acoustic ceiling tiles while my pulse finally slowed back toward its normal resting rate, my rule-following brain ran the calendar math from Saturday to Saturday—and blinked twice in the dark.

"Hey, Tammy?" I murmured sheepishly beside her on the pillow.

"Hmm?" Tammy whispered warmly, resting her bare arm across my chest and tucking her chin comfortable against my shoulder.

"Was... one week maybe kind of fast?" I asked honestly, genuinely unsure what the normal neurotypical timetable was supposed to be when two people met in an electronics aisle. "From ringing up a nine-dollar antenna on Saturday to this?"

Beside my ear, Tammy let out a soft, genuinely amused laugh that shook her shoulders against mine.

"Nope," Tammy said without a trace of hesitation. "Not fast at all, Nolan. Honestly? I wanted to bring you back here and sleep with you the very day we met."

In the warm dark, my eyes went wide open against the ceiling.

Like two loose coaxial connectors clicking flush onto a splitter jack, an entire seven-day-old memory snapped together inside my head in high definition: upstairs under the white peaked tent canopy of the Emerald Creek Mall food court, Tammy sweeping her glossy jet-black hair behind both shoulders, sliding her baby-blue cardigan open off her arms, scooting onto the front edge of her chair, arching her back, puffing her chest out across our red plastic Crown Burger trays—a signal I hadn't glanced down at once—and looking me dead in the eyes to say, *Just so you know, Nolan... I'm ready for everything.*

"Oh!" slipped right out of my mouth before I could stop it, heat rushing all the way to the tips of my ears in the dark. "At the mall food court—when you said you were ready for everything... you weren't talking about waiting on bus wheelchair lifts at all, were you?"

Tammy giggled softly against my collarbone, patting my chest once before letting her eyes drift shut. "Told you that you were the sweetest guy in Chesapeake, Nolan. Go to sleep."

Within five minutes, Tammy's breathing slowed into deep, even sleep beside me.

For another half hour, though, I lay wide awake under the unfamiliar sheet, staring up at ceiling panels that weren't mine. Out past the thin corrugated aluminum siding, an eighteen-wheeler geared down along Route 13 where quiet maple branches usually brushed the second-story eaves at home; metal heating ducts ticked strange little cooling pops along the narrow hallway; and for the first midnight in twenty-one years, neither Grandpa Walter nor Gwen was breathing anywhere under the same roof. Even with my twenty-one-year-old chest glowing proud and dizzy that a pretty, soft-spoken woman had chosen me, my fingers lightly tapped out tomorrow morning's southbound-to-northbound Route 15 cutback transfer times against the edge of the mattress—already looking forward to climbing Grandpa's three wooden porch steps before noon and pulling my little sister into a hug in our sunny kitchen.

### Part 4: Sunday Morning Glow, Big-Brother Boundaries, and Straight-A Homework

**Date:** 1999-10-31 at 10:50 America/New_York

#### 10:50 AM — Three Porch Steps and Zero Poker Face

Crisp late-October maple leaves crunched dry and bright beneath the gray rubber tips of my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches as I turned onto our quiet street behind Emerald Creek Mall at ten minutes to eleven, still wearing yesterday's wrinkled royal-blue Blue Tech work polo under my flannel jacket and feeling roughly ten feet tall.

Catching the sparse Sunday-schedule CVTA Route 15 cutback transfer back from Robert Hall Boulevard had meant an extra twenty-minute wait on the concrete bench at Military Highway and Old Emerald Creek Road, and I hadn't minded a single minute of it. Sunlight sparkled off every parked windshield, cool autumn air smelled like woodsmoke and damp oak bark, and my brain kept replaying two brand-new facts that still felt slightly unreal: I had spent an entire night under a roof away from my family without anything going sideways, and a pretty, polite woman with warm brown eyes genuinely liked me.

Because my autistic wiring came from the factory with zero poker face whatsoever, it never crossed my mind what my expression looked like from the outside as I climbed Grandpa Walter's three un-adapted wooden porch steps, pushed open the front door into the foyer, and clicked down the hardwood hall toward the smell of Sunday coffee and cinnamon toast in our eat-in kitchen.

Out at the sunlit breakfast table, surrounded by open three-ring binders, green engineering graph paper, and a graphing calculator, Gwen glanced up from her chair—and broke into an immediate smile.

Reaching for her two tall matte-charcoal forearm crutches leaning against her chair back, my sister stood up to meet me halfway across the linoleum just like we did every time either of us walked through the door. Propping all four of our crutches neatly side by side against the kitchen doorframe right beneath the faint pencil marks where Grandpa had measured our heights growing up, I opened both arms and pulled Gwen straight into our automatic greeting hug—my chin tucking into its familiar spot below her collarbone while she wrapped both long arms warmly around my shoulders and rested her cheek on top of my tousled hair.

"Morning, stranger," Gwen said happily, giving me an extra squeeze before stepping half a pace back to look down at my face. "Survive your big night out in the wild?"

Then Gwen stopped mid-blink.

Her green-hazel eyes narrowed half a millimeter—taking in my loose, unhurried shoulders, the goofy, unstoppable grin practically stretching my cheeks to my ears, and whatever floating, unmistakable glow was radiating straight off my face in the morning sun.

Right in front of me, Gwen's eyebrows arched high toward her honey-brown curls, and a delighted, knowing grin tugged at the corners of her mouth.

"Whoa," Gwen teased softly, tilting her head down at me with pure sisterly amusement. "Hold everything—look at your face, Nolan Lennox. Somebody had a *really* good movie night."

Heat rushed up my neck so fast the tips of my ears felt like toaster coils.

Even though Gwen stood six inches taller than me, read neurotypical subtext ten times faster than my brain ever would, and could spot a half-truth from across a crowded mall, inside my head one rule sat carved in solid granite: she was still my younger sister, five years behind me, and a big brother's job was to protect his kid sister. Grown-up bedroom details belonged strictly behind a closed door where my little sister never had to hear about them.

At the same time, lying outright went completely against how my brain worked—so I rubbed the back of my warm neck, stared down at the toaster for half a second while that hopeless grin refused to leave my mouth, and stuck strictly to verified PG facts.

"It was a really good evening," I said earnestly. "We had cheese pizza at her little dinette table, channel thirteen came in super sharp on the nine-dollar antenna with zero ghosting, and Tammy even keeps spare wrapped toothbrushes under her sink so I didn't have to skip brushing my teeth before bed. She's really nice to me, Gwenny."

Gwen didn't poke, didn't tease me harder to make me squirm, and didn't ask a single prying question.

Instead, every trace of playful mischief in my sister's eyes melted into pure, uncomplicated warmth. Growing up right beside me, Gwen knew better than anybody on Earth how many classmates in middle school and high school had looked straight past the quiet guy on forearm crutches who talked like a computer manual—and seeing me walk into our kitchen standing tall and glowing because someone was treating me gently made her whole face shine.

"I'm really, really happy for you, Nolan," Gwen said sincerely, reaching out to pat my cheek once before picking up her charcoal crutches to settle back into her chair. "You deserve somebody who treats you well."

Right on cue, the interior fire door from our two-car garage clicked open past the mudroom, and Grandpa Walter strolled into the kitchen wiping socket-wrench grease off his knuckles with a red shop rag. Spot-checking the wall clock above the double sink—ten-fifty-four, right ahead of the eleven o'clock arrival I had promised when I called from Tammy's wall phone at nine—Grandpa gave me one approving nod, poured a tall glass of orange juice out of the refrigerator, and set it beside the island.

"Bus run on time off Military Highway, boy?" Grandpa asked pleasantly.

"Right on the dot, Grandpa," I beamed, gulping half the cold glass while feeling mighty glad to be standing back on familiar kitchen linoleum. "Left socket drawer need sorting after lunch?"

"Already sorted," Grandpa chuckled, ruffling my hair once on his way toward the coffee pot. "Eat yourself two eggs and go keep an eye on that scholar at the table so she doesn't daydream out the backyard window all afternoon."

***

#### 1:15 PM — Protecting Every Straight A at the Kitchen Table

By quarter past one—after I ran upstairs to shower in our hallway bath, changed out of yesterday's wrinkled work polo into a clean navy cotton t-shirt, and downed scrambled eggs and toast while Grandpa settled into his leather recliner in the family room with the Sunday Tidewater newspaper—it was time for my non-negotiable Sunday afternoon job.

Pulling out the oak kitchen chair directly beside Gwen's elbow, I propped my matte-silver crutches next to hers, slid her assignment planner square in front of me, and uncapped a blue pen.

On paper, anybody looking at Gwen's report card would have laughed at the idea that she needed her older brother sitting beside her homework stack. Even though the Chesapeake school district had stubbornly held Gwen back a grade in early elementary school over hospital-cast attendance days after a childhood leg surgery—an administrative decision she still despised the district for with every bone in her body—she was already taking upper-level math a year ahead of her class and pulling straight A's across every single period on her freshman schedule at Emerald Creek High: Honors Algebra II and Trigonometry, Honors Biology, World History, and Freshman Honors English. Half the time, once she finished her actual math worksheet, she filled the margins of her green engineering pad with orbital-mechanics equations that already went clean over my head.

Knowing my little sister's brain, though, meant knowing the exact trap waiting inside classes she resented having to sit through a year older than her classmates: whenever routine busywork felt insultingly slow for her—twenty-five repetitive World History vocabulary definitions or writing out four lines of intermediate algebra steps when she could already see the final sine ratio in her head—she got tempted to skip steps or leave the dull worksheet for Monday morning.

And in my rulebook, girlfriend or no girlfriend, nothing in Chesapeake came ahead of making sure that school district never got another excuse to mark Gwen down—making sure her straight A's stayed straight A's.

Working hourly cash registers and cable aisles over at Blue Tech was an honest living that suited my routine-loving brain fine, yet I knew down to my bones that Gwen's mind belonged inside university lecture halls and astronomy labs—places where scholarships didn't forgive sloppy homework shortcuts and where the outside world already looked twice at anyone walking in on forearm crutches before reading their transcript.

"Let's see the trig sheet first," I said, running my index finger down numbers one through twenty-four against her textbook page. "Wait—hold up, Gwenny. On fourteen, seventeen, and nineteen you jumped straight from the secant identity to the final fraction without writing the common-denominator line in the middle."

Gwen let out a theatrical groan across the tabletop, flopping her chin onto her palm. "Nolan, it's literally just multiplying top and bottom by cosine! Anybody with eyeballs knows how I got there!"

"Mr. Callahan docks two points per problem if every intermediate substitution isn't written in pencil," I reminded her calmly, sliding the worksheet and her mechanical pencil right back across the wood grain. "Six points turns a hundred into a ninety-four, and two ninety-fours in a grading period puts a ninety-three A-minus in the gradebook if you catch one hard quiz. Write the middle lines out cleanly while I pull your Biology flash cards."

Rolling her eyes toward the ceiling, Gwen couldn't even hide the fond smile tugging at her mouth as she picked her pencil back up and neatly wrote out all three missing middle steps.

For the next two and a half hours, afternoon sunlight crept slowly across the kitchen island while we worked through every single folder side by side. Once her trig sheet was ironclad, I held up her stack of white index cards one by one—quizzing her aloud on cellular respiration stages, mitosis phases, and sixteenth-century trade routes, setting any card she hesitated two seconds on into a repeat pile until she rattled the whole deck off without a single stumble, and double-checking the back side of her English essay rubric so no hidden prompt got missed.

At quarter to four, when the very last World History definition was underlined neat and dry and every binder ring snapped shut inside her backpack for Monday morning, Gwen stretched both long arms high overhead, let them drop down, and leaned her shoulder warmly against mine right there at the kitchen table.

"Thanks, slave driver," Gwen murmured affectionately, bumping my arm with hers.

"Hey—straight A's across the board," I said proudly, tapping her closed backpack. "Nobody at that school gets an excuse to mark my sister down."

Gwen looked sideways at me for a quiet second—noticing how, even on the Sunday after my very first night away from home with a brand-new girlfriend, I hadn't spent the afternoon hovering by the kitchen wall phone or rushing back out the door to Route 15. I was right here at our table where I always was. Smiling softly to herself, my little sister rested her head briefly against the top of my hair before rolling her chair toward the counter to put the kettle on for hot chocolate, both of us completely content in our quiet Sunday routine.

### Part 5: Two Direct-Deposit Stubs, One Wobbly Wooden Ramp, and Moving Off Route 13

**Date:** 1999-11-20 at 10:15 America/New_York

![Exterior view of Tammy's weathered, rust-streaked single-wide mobile home at Pinecrest Court on an overcast late-November afternoon in Chesapeake, showing dark roof-tar leak patches overhead, the shuttered living-room window on the left, the center scroll-grille storm door behind four weathered wooden steps and a flat wooden top landing, and the landlord's steep, uninspected unpainted plywood-and-two-by-four wheelchair ramp resting on crooked stacks of unmortared cinder blocks and wooden shims over the loose gravel driveway along the windowless right wall.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p005_bodged_wooden_trailer_ramp.jpg)

#### 10:15 AM — Two Direct-Deposit Stubs and the Fourteen-Day Rule

Burnt black coffee steamed out of a chipped ceramic mug beside a dented metal cashbox as Mr. Pruitt, the landlord over Pinecrest Court, pinched his reading glasses onto the end of his nose and squinted down at my two perforated Blue Tech direct-deposit paystubs.

Inside the cramped wood-paneled office trailer near the front gate off Route 13, an electric baseboard heater ticked dry and hot against the scuffed linoleum while a ring of labeled brass master keys jangled from a pegboard behind his desk. Standing right beside my manual wheelchair in her chunky white platform sneakers, flared jeans, and pastel baby-blue fleece cardigan, Tammy rested one warm hand lightly on my shoulder—giving me an encouraging squeeze every time Mr. Pruitt's ballpoint pen hovered over another line of my gross year-to-date earnings.

Over the three weeks since Halloween weekend, everything between Tammy and me had settled into a steady, comforting rhythm that made twenty-one feel like real adulthood. Whenever I wrapped up an afternoon cash-register shift at Blue Tech or finished my Sunday homework check at Grandpa Walter's kitchen table with Gwen, I had ridden the CVTA Route 15 cutback transfer down here to eat supper at Tammy's little two-chair dinette and watch broadcast movies on channel thirteen. Then, earlier this week, Tammy had sat down across from me with a yellow legal pad and walked my literal, rule-following brain through two plain pieces of math.

First, Pinecrest Court enforced a strict fourteen-day guest rule printed right on page two of every lot agreement: any adult staying overnight more than twice a week had to pass an income check and sign onto the lease as an approved occupant, or risk getting the tenant written up. Second, Tammy lived carefully on her monthly SSI check, Food Stamps, and Virginia Medicaid, and working regular retail payroll hours risked knocking her over the income cliff and costing her medical coverage—whereas splitting her modest trailer and lot rent straight down the middle with my steady Blue Tech direct deposits meant both of us came out ahead without breaking a single rule.

To my twenty-one-year-old mind, following the printed park rulebook while helping out a girlfriend who treated me gently made total, airtight sense.

"Retail electronics up by Emerald Creek Mall, huh?" Mr. Pruitt muttered, tapping the top paystub against my laminated Virginia identification card before sliding both back across the counter. "Steady hours. Beats chasing folks down on the fifth of the month."

"I haven't missed a scheduled shift since orientation, sir," I answered earnestly, sitting up straighter in my wheelchair. "Direct deposit posts every other Friday at six in the morning."

Tammy let out a soft, pleased laugh beside my ear, looking proud of me as Mr. Pruitt flipped open a battered manila folder and turned a carbon-copy lease addendum toward my side of the counter.

"Month-to-month co-tenant addendum, effective November twentieth," Mr. Pruitt said, pointing with the cap of his blue pen. "Initial by the fourteen-day guest clause, initial by lot upkeep, sign and print at the bottom. Both of you."

Checking every single line from left margin to right margin first—because Grandpa Walter had raised Gwen and me never to sign a piece of paper we hadn't read—I printed my initials neatly inside both boxes, signed my full name along the bottom line, and passed the pen up to Tammy, who signed right underneath mine with a round, looping flourish.

Mr. Pruitt tore off the canary-yellow carbon sheet, folded it once, and dropped a freshly cut brass trailer key right onto the paper with a bright metallic clink. Wiping a streak of pine sawdust off his flannel cuff, he jerked a thumb toward the gravel lane outside the window.

"Finished knocking together that wheelchair ramp over by your front steps about nine-thirty, Tammy," Mr. Pruitt added casually, taking a sip of his black coffee. "Used what lumber I had stacked behind the maintenance shed, mind you, so it ain't the Taj Mahal. Just take 'er easy coming off the top turn and keep both tires centered."

"Thank you so much, Mr. Pruitt," Tammy said in her sweetest, politest voice, scooping up the yellow lease copy while I slipped my very first set of non-family house keys into my jeans pocket. "We really appreciate you putting that up so fast."

***

#### 11:20 AM — Leftover Plywood, Loose Cinder Blocks, and Sort of Working

Five minutes later, rolling down the rutted gravel lane past rows of weathered single-wides toward Tammy's lot—where dark-silver patches of roof tar striped the rusty aluminum seam overhead from all the old ceiling leaks Mr. Pruitt had patched over the years—I spotted Grandpa Walter's silver-tan four-door sedan already backed squarely beside the gravel pad.

Its trunk stood propped wide open around my scuffed oak bedroom dresser tied snug with hemp rope, my nineteen-inch CRT television cushioned inside a folded quilt, and two liquor-store cardboard boxes packed neat with my Blue Tech polos, socks, and jeans. Right in front of the trailer's center entry door, seventy-seven-year-old Grandpa Walter stood with both hands planted on the hips of his khaki work pants beside Gwen, who was balanced tall on her matte-charcoal Lofstrand forearm crutches in her burgundy sweater—both of them staring in dead, fascinated silence at the brand-new wooden contraption bolted beside Tammy's four front steps.

Stopping my manual wheelchair next to the rear bumper of Grandpa's sedan, I blinked twice at what Mr. Pruitt had built.

Starting next to the small square landing outside the center storm door and angling down parallel to the dented aluminum skirting, the ramp was cobbled together out of raw, unpainted pine two-by-sixes and two mismatched sheets of half-inch exterior plywood that didn't quite meet flush at the middle seam. Instead of concrete footings sunk into the dirt, the whole frame rested on four stacks of dry, unmortared gray cinder blocks wedged directly onto the loose driveway gravel with scrap cedar shims. There were zero raised wooden curb edges along the bottom sides to keep a front wheelchair caster from wandering off into thin air, two galvanized nail heads sat slightly proud near the center joint, a curled strip of green indoor-outdoor porch carpet had been stapled crooked across the top lip, and the waist-high handrails consisted of raw, unsanded two-by-fours nailed flat against skinny upright posts.

Grandpa Walter reached out two calloused fingers and nudged the nearest upright post. The entire left handrail swayed a full inch sideways and back again with an audible dry squeak.

Stepping forward, Grandpa planted one heavy leather work boot dead center on the unpainted plywood span and pressed half his weight down. Beneath his sole, the thin half-inch plywood bowed visibly inward with a hollow, echoing *creak-pop*, while a dry trickle of crushed limestone shifted underneath the middle cinder block.

Grandpa Walter let out a long, low whistle through his teeth, squinting down the crooked lumber line with the practiced eye of a man who kept every wrench in his two-car garage sorted to the sixteenth of an inch.

"Good Lord Almighty," Grandpa murmured dryly, careful to keep his voice pleasant with Tammy standing right beside my chair. "If a Chesapeake city building inspector ever turned down this lane with a clipboard, he'd run clean out of red ink before noon."

Beside him, six-foot-one Gwen peered down over her charcoal forearm cuffs at the steep pitch and crooked green carpet strip, one honey-brown eyebrow arching high.

"Are we positive that's a wheelchair ramp," Gwen murmured just loud enough for me and Grandpa to hear, "and not a ski jump?"

Tammy shifted half an inch in her chunky platform sneakers, glancing quickly from Grandpa's face up to Gwen's—still visibly thrown off whenever she had to tilt her five-foot-nothing chin more than a foot upward to meet my sixteen-year-old little sister's eyes. "Mr. Pruitt didn't charge us extra for the wood," Tammy offered quickly, keeping her tone bright and polite. "He said once the gravel packs down under the blocks after a rain or two, it'll sit plenty flat."

Looking at the angle with my own ground-scanning eyes, I could see every single flaw Grandpa saw: the slope ran closer to two inches per foot than the gentle one-in-twelve concrete ramps over by Emerald Creek Mall, and nobody on Earth would ever stamp a city inspection sticker onto dry-stacked cinder blocks and half-inch plywood. At the same time, my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches were already strapped snug in the vertical holder tubes behind my wheelchair backrest, my brass key was sitting warm in my front pocket, and there was only one honest way to find out whether my chair could actually climb into my new home.

"Let me test the center line," I said, unlocking my brakes and rolling up to the splintery bottom lip where plywood met gravel.

Grandpa stepped immediately within arm's reach of the lower open edge—not touching my push-handles unless I asked, just stationing his steady frame right beside the drop-off the way he had spotted Gwen and me since childhood—while I leaned my chest well forward over my knees so my front casters wouldn't pop skyward on the steep incline, gripped both steel hand-rims hard, and drove upward in three strong, even strokes.

Halfway up, the plywood belly flexed noticeably underneath my rear tires with a loud wooden groan, one cedar shim gave a tiny crunch against gray concrete block, and when I brushed my left knuckle against the upright rail at the top turn to line up with the storm door, the raw two-by-four wobbled like a loose fencepost.

Yet a second later, my front casters bumped over the crooked strip of green carpet onto the small top landing, and both rear tires cleared the threshold right behind them without tipping backward an inch.

Turning my chair ninety degrees on the cramped square landing outside Tammy's center door, I looked back down past the bowed plywood seam springing slowly flat again and broke into an honest grin.

"Hey—it works!" I called down, before glancing once more at the swaying two-by-four rail beside my elbow and adding strictly for accuracy, "Sort of."

Down on the gravel, Gwen couldn't hold back a fond snort of laughter, shaking her head up at me while Grandpa Walter rubbed his chin and gave one reluctant, half-amused nod.

"Keep both tires dead down the middle stringer where the two-by-sixes sit, boy, and stay off it when frost gets on that bare plywood," Grandpa advised, turning back toward the open trunk of his sedan to untie the hemp rope around my oak dresser. "Come on—let's get this chest of drawers inside before suppertime."

***

#### 4:15 PM — Leaving My Childhood Bed Made Up and Learning a New Bus Math

By quarter past four, pale late-November daylight was already slanting low and gold through the bare pines behind Pinecrest Court, and my few belongings were tucked into Tammy's narrow twelve-by-forty-six-foot home.

Back at the far right end of the thirty-two-inch hallway—just past the corded beige wall phone and the tiny bathroom—Grandpa Walter had wrestled my scuffed oak dresser neatly against the bedroom wall while I walked behind him on my matte-silver crutches carrying boxes of folded polos and socks. Back near Emerald Creek Mall, Grandpa had flatly refused to let me take apart the childhood full-sized bed upstairs in my second-floor bedroom—reminding me that Tammy already had a bed back here, and insisting my upstairs room at home stayed made up with fresh sheets anytime my legs felt tired, a late bus stranded me near the mall, or Gwen and I wanted a weekend movie night downstairs on the queen pull-out sofa bed. Out in Tammy's front living area—where a faint trace of the previous tenant's old cigarette habit still clung inside the thin wood-grain paneling despite Tammy scrubbing the walls with pine cleaner—my nineteen-inch CRT television sat wired cleanly to her nine-dollar rabbit ears while Tammy poured tall glasses of sweet iced tea at the two-chair dinette, thanking Grandpa Walter twice for doing the heavy lifting.

Walking out beside Grandpa and Gwen to see them off at the sedan—palming both steel push-rims firmly to brake my wheelchair inch by inch down the steep, creaking wooden ramp until my casters touched solid gravel again—a sudden, unexpected tightness tugged behind my breastbone.

All twenty-one years of my life, first in our parents' old one-story house when we were little and then every single year since Grandpa Walter took custody of us in his big two-story Colonial behind Emerald Creek Mall, going to sleep had meant Gwen's bedroom right across the upstairs hall and Grandpa's steady footsteps checking the front deadbolt downstairs.

Reaching back to pull my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches out of the backrest tubes, I clicked the gray cuffs around my forearms, stood up from my wheelchair beside the open passenger door of Grandpa's silver-tan sedan, and opened my arms to Gwen.

My little sister didn't hesitate half a heartbeat. Balancing one hand on the sedan roof while propping her tall charcoal crutches against the rear quarter-panel, Gwen wrapped both long arms tight around my shoulders and tucked my chin right into its lifelong spot beneath her collarbone, resting her cheek firmly on top of my tousled brown hair.

"Don't you dare forget tomorrow afternoon, big brother," Gwen murmured warmly into my hair, squeezing me once more before letting go just enough to look down into my eyes. "Mr. Callahan assigned twenty-eight inverse-trig identities over Thanksgiving week, and if nobody sits at the kitchen table making me write out every middle step, my brain's gonna rebel."

"Every Sunday right after lunch, Gwenny," I promised immediately, patting her burgundy sleeve with total certainty. "Route 15 cutback gets me to Old Emerald Creek Road by ten-forty-five. Straight A's stay straight A's."

Grandpa Walter stepped around the trunk, clapped one broad, steady hand onto my shoulder, and tucked a crisp folded twenty-dollar bill straight into my flannel shirt pocket before I could protest.

"Keep that in your wallet for milk and bread before Friday's check clears," Grandpa said, his calm eyes holding mine for a second longer than usual—making sure I knew without a big speech that the front porch light near Emerald Creek Mall never turned off on us. "Call the house once you're settled tonight, Nolan."

"Yes, sir. Nine o'clock sharp," I said, giving him a quick, tight hug around his flannel ribs before helping Gwen stow her charcoal crutches across the back floorboard.

Tammy stepped lightly down the four wooden front steps in her platform sneakers to stand beside my elbow, waving politely with both hands tucked into her baby-blue cardigan pockets as Grandpa backed the silver-tan sedan smoothly out of the narrow slot and rolled down the rutted gravel lane toward Route 13.

Watching Grandpa's red taillights blink once at the gate and disappear behind the overgrown pines, my schedule-loving brain automatically ran Monday morning's brand-new math: instead of locking Grandpa's front door twenty minutes before my shift and strolling four flat blocks across to Blue Tech, getting to work from Pinecrest Court meant rolling down Mr. Pruitt's bouncy, creaking plywood ramp ninety minutes early to catch CVTA Route 15 off Robert Hall Boulevard and make the Military Highway cutback transfer both ways every single workday.

Then Tammy slipped her warm small fingers around my wrist, leaned her cheek briefly against my upper arm with a quiet sigh, and tugged gently toward the four steps and the wobbly unpainted wooden ramp waiting beside them. At twenty-one, with a brass key of my own in my pocket and someone gentle smiling up at me in the cool November dusk, two buses each way and a ramp that *sort of* worked felt like the luckiest bargain in Chesapeake.

### Part 6: Two-Digit Clocks, Floppy-Disk Backups, and Turning Everything Off Before Midnight

**Date:** 1999-12-31 at 23:35 America/New_York

#### 11:35 PM — Surge Strips, CMOS Batteries, and Gwen's Floppy Disk

Twenty-five minutes before the year two thousand arrived on the East Coast, every endcap back at the Emerald Creek Blue Tech looked like a hurricane had blown straight through the computer department.

All five hours of my Friday morning-to-afternoon shift, panicked Chesapeake shoppers had lined up twelve deep at my register clutching six-outlet beige surge protectors, bricks of alkaline flashlight batteries, shrink-wrapped floppy-disk diagnostic boxes promising to rescue home computers from the Millennium Bug, and hand-cranked weather radios—half of them convinced that the exact second eleven-fifty-nine rolled over to twelve-zero-zero, two-digit calendar clocks were going to shut down the Virginia power grid, scramble bank accounts, and drop airliners out of the sky.

Standing on the patterned beige linoleum beside Tammy's kitchenette partition in my flannel lounge shirt with both of my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches leaned neatly against the counter, I knew perfectly well that thirty-two-bit Quantum OS 98 already tracked dates cleanly past the year twenty-thirty-eight as long as a motherboard's internal CMOS clock knew nineteen-ninety-nine turned into two thousand instead of nineteen hundred.

At the same time, my rule-following brain believed in zero unnecessary risks when sixteen-year-old Gwen's astronomy spreadsheets were sitting on an older gray laptop back home near Emerald Creek Mall.

Over at the two-chair Formica dinette, Tammy stirred miniature marshmallows into two ceramic mugs of hot cocoa in thick fuzzy winter socks and an oversized cream cable-knit sweater. Six weeks into living together at Pinecrest Court, everything under our narrow roof felt calm, polite, and genuinely happy. Tammy never minded my ninety-minute two-bus CVTA Route 15 commute, never complained when I spent Sunday afternoons at Grandpa Walter's kitchen table checking Gwen's straight-A freshman folders, and right now—twenty-five minutes before our very first New Year's Eve midnight together—she simply smiled warmly across the counter as I lifted the beige corded wall receiver off its hook.

"Making sure your sister shuts down the spaceship math?" Tammy asked fondly, carrying both steaming mugs over to the floral living-room sofa where my nineteen-inch television showed crowds packed into coats up in Times Square.

"Can't trust a ninety-seven laptop BIOS until we see how the rollover behaves," I explained earnestly, dialing Grandpa Walter's seven digits by muscle memory.

Grandpa picked up on the second ring, his steady voice sounding amused over the murmur of his own living-room television.

"Evening, Nolan," Grandpa chuckled. "Got three gallons of tap water in milk jugs on the counter just to keep the neighbors happy, and your sister's still hunched over that gray contraption at the dining table."

"Could you put Gwenny on for sixty seconds, Grandpa?"

A wooden chair scraped, tall matte-charcoal forearm crutches clicked three brisk strides across linoleum, and Gwen came onto the line laughing before I even said a word.

"Yes, Nolan, I already saved a backup copy onto a fresh three-and-a-half-inch floppy disk and slid the little plastic write-protect tab open so nothing can overwrite it!" Gwen announced triumphantly.

"Did you verify the file opens off the A-drive before ejecting the disk?" I double-checked, because unverified backups didn't count as backups.

"Opened it twice, closed the program, and put the floppy inside my Latin binder," Gwen promised warmly. "Can I keep plotting until eleven-fifty-eight?"

"Nope—shut it all the way down right now," I instructed firmly. "Click Start, click Shut Down, wait until the screen actually gives you the amber message saying it's safe to turn off your computer, press the power button all the way out, and then flip the red rocker switch on the floor surge strip to off—better yet, pull the three-prong wall plug clean out of the baseboard outlet until twelve-fifteen just in case the electric substation hiccups at midnight."

Through the receiver I heard my little sister let out a fond, dramatic sigh—followed two seconds later by the unmistakable chime of Quantum OS 98 logging off.

"Shutting down right now, Chief Engineer," Gwen murmured affectionately. "Wait—did you turn your own tower off back in Tammy's bedroom?"

"Shut it down and unplugged the surge strip at eleven-fifteen," I reported proudly. "Happy New Year, Gwenny. Tell Grandpa I'll be home by quarter to eleven Sunday morning to check your biology packet."

"Happy New Year, big brother. Give Tammy a hug for us."

Hanging the beige receiver back onto its wall cradle, I double-checked that the electric baseboard heater was clicking steady against the December chill outside Mr. Pruitt's frosty plywood ramp, picked up my matte-silver crutches, and walked five paces into the living room to settle onto the floral sofa right beside Tammy.

Tucking both socked feet underneath her cushion, Tammy handed me my warm cocoa mug, leaned her glossy dark hair comfortably against my shoulder, and watched the glowing numbers on the bottom corner of channel ten count down the final three minutes of nineteen-ninety-nine.

"Think the streetlights out on Route 13 are actually gonna blink out?" Tammy asked softly against my flannel sleeve.

"Substation relays don't care what year the calendar says," I answered calmly, wrapping my free arm gently around her shoulders the way couples in movies did. "Still—having every hard drive parked with the power off before midnight just makes good engineering sense."

Ten, nine, eight ticked down across the nineteen-inch glass; confetti burst white across Times Square; Tammy tilted her chin up with a bright, gentle smile to kiss my cheek; and out past our frosted living-room window and nine-dollar rabbit ears, the amber sodium streetlight out by the Pinecrest Court gate kept right on shining into the year two thousand without flickering once. At twenty-one years old, holding a warm mug beside someone sweet with my family safe across town and every floppy backup verified, I figured grown-up life didn't get much steadier than this.

### Part 7: Unspoken Rules, Four Nights Back Upstairs, and Clean Work Polos

**Date:** 2000-09-23 at 13:20 America/New_York

#### 1:20 PM — Rehearsing Four Sentences on the Southbound Fifteen

All the way down eight-lane Military Highway aboard the southbound CVTA Route 15, clutching an empty canvas gym bag across my knees in my royal-blue Blue Tech work polo while my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches rattled softly beside my leg, I whispered the same four-sentence apology under my breath for the nineteenth straight time.

*Tammy, I'm really sorry about Wednesday night. When you said you were fine eating leftovers by yourself and not to rush back if Grandpa needed help in the garage, I took the exact words literally instead of realizing you wanted me to come straight home. Next time I'll ask directly whether you want company before I make a stop. I only swung by today to grab clean work shirts out of my dresser if you still want space.*

Ten months of smooth, predictable peace inside Pinecrest Court—ten months where I had split every lot-rent payment right on the first, saved up my biweekly direct deposits to buy a sturdy oak full-sized bedframe for Tammy's back bedroom plus a brushed-silver dual-deck VCR and DVD combo for our movies, and thought I had finally figured out how being a boyfriend worked—had blown apart in ninety seconds flat four nights ago.

Looking back at Wednesday afternoon, my literal twenty-two-year-old brain still ached trying to trace the invisible tripwire I had stepped on.

Right before I rolled down Mr. Pruitt's creaking wooden ramp at two-thirty to catch the bus for my four-to-eight evening shift at Blue Tech, I had mentioned plainly that seventy-eight-year-old Grandpa Walter was planning to clean out the high shelves in his two-car garage before Friday's rain, and asked if Tammy minded me stopping by Grandpa's house for forty minutes after work before catching the last outbound bus home. Standing barefoot by the kitchenette sink, Tammy hadn't frowned once. In a flat, breezy voice, she had waved a dish towel and said, *Oh, don't rush back on my account—go sort wrenches with your grandpa and Gwen, I'm totally fine eating cold macaroni by myself.*

Inside my head, spoken English wasn't a riddle; *I'm totally fine* meant she was fine. So after clocking out at eight, I had walked four blocks behind Emerald Creek Mall, steadied Grandpa's step-stool while he handed down paint cans, quizzed sophomore Gwen for ten minutes on her Latin II second-conjugation verbs, and caught the final Route 15 cutback transfer back to Robert Hall Boulevard—walking in Tammy's center door at nine-forty-five expecting a quiet goodnight.

Instead, I had walked straight into a hurricane.
