Tammy hadn't just been upset; she had screamed loud enough to rattle the thin aluminum walls—shouting that *any normal grown man on Earth knows "don't rush back, eat cold macaroni by myself" means the exact opposite*, accusing me of caring more about socket drawers and bus schedules than her, dramatically declaring that *we were broken up*, and ordering me to stuff three days of clothes into a bag and go sleep at my grandfather's house before the last bus stopped running.

Too stunned and sensory-overloaded by the sudden yelling to form a coherent sentence, I had ridden back across Chesapeake in the dark and climbed the stairs up to my old childhood bedroom at Grandpa Walter's house—telling Grandpa and Gwen sheepishly at breakfast Thursday morning that Tammy and I had just hit a small communication mix-up that was *my fault* for missing a hint.

For four straight days, my stomach had sat knotted like wet rope. Over at the Emerald Creek Blue Tech, I didn't have a single friend my own age in the breakroom to ask about relationship rules yet—just polite older coworkers talking football scores—and every hour I spent straightening seventy-five-ohm cables in Aisle Four, my brain kept landing on the exact same verdict: Tammy had been sweet and patient with me for ten straight months, and *my* faulty autistic social radar had broken it.

Pulling the yellow signal cord as the bus approached Robert Hall Boulevard, I stepped carefully down the high front steps on my silver forearm crutches and walked the familiar gravel lane into Pinecrest Court under warm late-September sun, praying Tammy wouldn't yell when I knocked to pick up clean Blue Tech polos for Monday's shift.

***

#### 1:50 PM — Tears at the Storm Door and Forgetting Every Question

My rubber crutch tips had barely clicked onto the second weathered wooden step below the flat four-by-four landing—right beside Mr. Pruitt's steep, sun-bleached plywood wheelchair ramp resting on its crooked cinder blocks—when the inner trailer door swung wide open behind the scroll-grille aluminum storm door.

Tammy stood barefoot on the linoleum threshold, straight jet-black hair loose past her shoulders over a thin white ribbed spaghetti-strap camisole and cutoff denim shorts.

There wasn't a trace of Wednesday night's red-faced anger anywhere on her round face. Before I could even open my mouth to deliver sentence one of my rehearsed apology, Tammy's big doe-brown eyes welled straight up with shining tears, her lower lip trembled, and she pushed the storm door open to throw both small arms tightly around my neck right there on the top wooden landing.

"Oh, Nolan, I'm so glad you came home," Tammy whispered thickly against my royal-blue polo collar, holding on as if I were the one who had walked out on *her*. "I've been crying in this quiet trailer for three whole days—I didn't mean a single word I yelled Wednesday night, baby, I swear I didn't."

Every prepared gear inside my head jammed to a stop. Balancing carefully with both gray forearm cuffs braced while my empty gym bag hung from my wrist, I blinked down at the top of her glossy black hair in total bewilderment.

"Wait—you didn't?" I stammered earnestly. "Because I worked out why I messed up, Tammy—when you said you were fine eating macaroni alone, my brain took the literal words instead of—"

"Shh, come inside out of the sun," Tammy murmured in that soft, unhurried Week-One voice that instantly melted the tight knot behind my breastbone, tugging gently on my polo hem until I stepped across the threshold into the warm, un-air-conditioned trailer and clicked the deadbolt shut behind us.

Inside, my nineteen-inch television and silver VCR/DVD combo still sat untouched in the living room, my oak dresser still stood back down the narrow thirty-two-inch hallway beside our full-sized bed, and the yellow carbon copy of Mr. Pruitt's lease addendum still hung pinned to the side of the tan refrigerator with both of our signatures intact—because Tammy had never once called the park office to take my name off the lease.

Stepping right up between my crutches on the patterned beige linoleum, five-foot-nothing Tammy tilted her tear-streaked round cheeks seven inches upward, resting both warm palms flat against my chest.

"You didn't ruin anything, Nolan," Tammy said softly, turning Wednesday night's explosion completely upside down with three quiet sentences. "I only lost my temper like that because I love you so much it scares me sometimes—sitting here all evening by myself, I got it in my head you liked being back at your grandpa's big house better than being here with me, and I panicked. Don't stay away again, okay? Let me show you how much I missed you."

To a lonely twenty-two-year-old guy who had spent ninety-six hours convinced he was defective at relationships, hearing that her screaming only meant *she loved him too much* felt like sunlight breaking through storm clouds.

Before my literal mind could even think to ask why missing somebody required shouting them out the door at ten o'clock at night, Tammy didn't wait for another word of conversation. Standing two feet in front of me in the eighty-four-degree afternoon stillness of the trailer—with the twenty-inch box fan humming softly in the back dinette window—she crossed both small wrists at her waist, caught the bottom hem of her white spaghetti-strap camisole, pulled it clean off over her head onto the floral sofa, and unbuttoned her denim shorts right down to the linoleum until she stood completely bare in front of me, reaching up to unclip my Blue Tech name badge from my collar and catch my hand toward the narrow rear hallway.

[Note to the AI: Fade to black here as Tammy uses physical intimacy to reconcile with twenty-two-year-old Nolan after initiating their four-day first breakup. Per safety guardrails, write strictly around any sexual activity with zero explicit description and never generate artwork depicting nudity or sexual scenes; resume afterward once evening settles in.]

***

#### 6:10 PM — Unpacking an Empty Bag

By ten past six, orange late-September dusk slanted warm across the living-room mini-blinds while Tammy hummed cheerily at the four-burner stove boiling water for spaghetti—acting for all the world as if Wednesday night through Saturday noon had never happened at all.

Calling Grandpa Walter's kitchen landline from the wall phone to let him and Gwen know Tammy and I had patched things up and I was staying home at Pinecrest Court tonight, I heard a tiny, half-second pause on the other end of the line before Grandpa said calmly, *All right, Nolan. Glad things smoothed out, son. See you after lunch tomorrow for your sister's homework.*

Even sixteen-year-old Gwen, when Grandpa passed her the phone for ten seconds, kept her voice gentle and careful—*See you tomorrow afternoon, Nolan; love you*—without saying a bad word against Tammy.

Folding my empty canvas gym bag flat and sliding it back onto the bottom shelf of my oak dresser down the narrow hallway, every muscle in my shoulders finally unclenched. In my literal rulebook, one fight in ten whole months was practically a ninety-nine-percent reliability rating—and now that I knew *I'm fine* secretly meant *come straight home*, I figured all I had to do was try a little harder and everything would stay wonderful forever.

### Part 8: Protected Memory, Store Four-Twenty, and Someone Safe in the Breakroom

**Date:** 2001-05-15 at 12:30 America/New_York

![Warm 2001 Emerald Creek Blue Tech employee breakroom scene at a quiet corner Formica table beside gray staff lockers, corkboard schedules, and a countertop microwave: twenty-three-year-old Computers and Software Shift Supervisor Mallory Walsh sits on the left with copper-auburn hair in a ponytail, a blue ballpoint pen tucked behind her right ear, thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, royal-blue supervisor polo, and tan khaki chinos with a retractable override key-reel, gesturing animatedly beside a silver Quantum OS 2000 Professional CD-ROM jewel case while Nolan Lennox sits across from her in his manual wheelchair with his two matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches stowed upright in the black canvas back pocket behind his backrest on a day his legs weren't feeling the concrete floor.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p008_mallory_breakroom_quantum_os.jpg)

#### 12:30 PM — Ring-Protected Memory and the Corner Table

In the back corner of the Emerald Creek Blue Tech employee breakroom—the one quiet Formica table twelve feet clear of the buzzing vending machines and the burnt-popcorn smell of the countertop microwave—a chrome retractable key-reel gave a soft, familiar clink as Mallory Walsh set her insulated lunch bag down directly across from my untouched peanut-butter sandwich.

"Mind if I take the quiet end, Nolan?" Mallory asked plainly, tapping the blue ballpoint pen tucked behind her right ear before sliding onto the molded plastic chair opposite mine. "Somebody in Appliances is microwaving leftover fish sticks, and two guys by the lockers are arguing at eighty decibels about football draft picks."

"Please do," I said honestly, feeling the tight band behind my temples loosen by twenty percent on the spot.

Back in March—two months after seventy-eight-year-old Grandpa Walter caught winter pneumonia, spent eleven terrifying days in cardiac intensive care with congestive heart failure, and had to sign a six-figure home-equity medical loan on his two-story house just to cover what insurance wouldn't—corporate had promoted twenty-three-year-old Mallory Walsh over to our Emerald Creek store from Blue Tech Store Four-Twenty up on Independence Boulevard in Virginia Beach to serve as our new Computers and Software Shift Supervisor.

Within forty-eight hours of her arrival, everybody under our roof knew two things about Mallory: first, behind her thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, freckled nose, and pulled-back copper-auburn ponytail, she was an absolute, walking encyclopedia on all things Quantum hardware and Quantum OS; and second, she never wasted a single syllable on fake retail small talk.

Neither of us ever had to say a word about clinical labels across the breakroom table—yet from the very first week Mallory showed me how she sorted motherboard RAM sticks in ascending bus-speed order inside the locked cage, my brain had recognized its own wiring immediately. Mallory didn't care if I looked slightly past her shoulder while listening, didn't talk in riddles, kept a mini nylon precision-screwdriver pouch clipped beside her supervisor override keys on her khaki belt, and spoke the exact same literal, rule-governed script I had spent twenty-three years trying to translate for everybody else—understanding how my head worked better than anyone I had ever met outside Gwen and Grandpa Walter.

Opening her sandwich container first, Mallory reached into her canvas tote and slid a gleaming silver retail CD-ROM jewel case partway across the gray Formica toward my elbow: *Quantum OS 2000 Professional*.

"Remember last week when you mentioned your sister's ninety-eight laptop keeps throwing blue fatal-exception screens halfway through her orbital-math plots?" Mallory said, her blue-gray eyes lighting up behind her lenses with pure, unhurried craft pride. "Back at Store Four-Twenty on Independence, half our returns were customers thinking their hard drives were dying when it was really just the hybrid sixteen-bit kernel in Version four-ten-nineteen-ninety-eight letting one leaky driver scribble straight across system memory. Two-Thousand Professional runs on the clean thirty-two-bit NT kernel—strict ring-protected memory spaces, hardware abstraction layer, zero random crashes. Keep an eye on the employee-accommodation binder next fall; you'll want a copy for her before winter."

"Ring-protected memory so one bad process can't crash the whole machine," I repeated appreciatively, turning the jewel case at an angle toward my eyes to read the minimum RAM requirements on the back insert. For two solid minutes we traded pure, soothing technical specs—NTFS journaling versus FAT-thirty-two file tables, IRQ sharing, clean installs versus in-place upgrades—until my breathing finally slowed back to normal.

Then Mallory set her water bottle down, pushed her thin silver wire-rimmed glasses half an inch up the bridge of her freckled nose, and asked a plain question with zero subtext attached.

"Okay, kernel talk's covered," Mallory said quietly. "That's the fourth shift since last Friday you haven't unwrapped your sandwich on lunch punch, Nolan—and you rolled in off the Emerald Creek sidewalk in your chair this morning instead of getting off the fifteen bus. Are your legs giving you extra grief today, or is your grandpa's heart acting up again?"

Getting asked a direct, literal question by someone my own age who actually wanted the real answer caught me so off guard my throat went tight.

"My legs just weren't feeling four hours of sales-floor concrete today, so I brought the chair and left my crutches tucked in the back pocket—and Grandpa's holding steady on his new afternoon pills, still driving short errands in the daytime," I murmured, resting one thumb against my wheelchair's cool aluminum push-rim beside my brown paper lunch sack. "Tammy and I... uh, broke up again. Eleven days ago. I've been sleeping back upstairs in my old room at Grandpa's house."

Across the Formica, Mallory didn't pry with gossip-hungry eyes the way people on TV did. She just gave one small, attentive nod to let me know it was safe to keep talking if I wanted to.

"Ever since Grandpa got out of the hospital in February, I've been stopping by his house an extra evening or two a week to sort his pill organizer and drill Gwenny on precalculus before tenth-grade finals," I explained, still half-trying to diagnose what line of relationship code I had botched this time. "Two Fridays ago Tammy started yelling at me because my crutches clicked too loud getting water and because I spent twenty minutes on the wall phone checking whether Grandpa's cardiology copay cleared. She shouted that if Emerald Creek mattered more to me than Pinecrest Court, we were done and I shouldn't come back. Last fall when we broke up the first time, it only lasted four days because I realized I took a hint too literally and went back to apologize. This time... honestly, Mallory, my head hurt so bad from the yelling I just haven't gotten back on the fifteen bus yet."

Behind her wire-rimmed glasses, Mallory's blue-gray eyes narrowed sharply—not at me, not by a mile, but with a sudden, unmistakable flash of fierce dislike aimed straight toward Route 13.

"Hold on," Mallory said, setting both forearms flat on the table and speaking in the clearest, cleanest logic anyone outside my family had ever handed me. "Let me check the inputs here. Your seventy-eight-year-old grandfather—who raised you and your sister—nearly died of congestive heart failure this winter, and your girlfriend screamed at you and threw you out of a trailer you pay half the rent on because you checked on his heart medication and drank a glass of water?"

"Well... she gets stressed worried about her benefits, and sometimes I miss social signals," I offered weakly.

"Nolan, stop," Mallory said firmly—never raising her voice an inch, keeping her tone as calm and steady as a diagnostic readout. "You and I both know what missing a social cue looks like. Somebody screaming in your face over fridge shelves, crutch clicks, and checking on a sick grandfather isn't a social cue you missed. Think about what we were *just* talking about on this disc: in a healthy operating system, one process doesn't corrupt the whole machine every time you execute a valid instruction. If somebody keeps changing the specification after you follow the rules just so they have an excuse to yell at you, the bug isn't in *your* hardware."

I sat completely still in my wheelchair—both wheel locks clicked tight against the linoleum—while every single word of that sentence sank straight through two years of fog.

*The bug isn't in your hardware.*

"Take a bite of your peanut-butter sandwich before fifteen-minute warning," Mallory added gently, nudging my paper sack half an inch closer to my knuckles before flipping open a pocket spiral pad to sketch a memory-partition diagram so nobody walking past our corner table would know we were talking about anything heavier than computers.

All eleven days—and through the entire next week and a half as May stretched into its third week with me still sleeping peacefully upstairs at Grandpa's house—those thirty-minute breakroom lunches across from Mallory Walsh felt like stepping onto dry land after treading choppy water. It just felt *nice* having a friend my own age to talk to who never raised her voice, never made me guess hidden meanings, and genuinely liked hearing how CVTA bus headways lined up with motherboard clock multipliers. If Mallory felt anything warmer behind those perceptive blue-gray eyes when she slid the spare plastic chair clear of that quiet corner table every shift so my crutches or my wheelchair always had clean clearance, she never dropped a single hint across the timeclock—keeping every line clean and professional behind the small silver Shift Supervisor pin on her royal-blue collar.

***

#### 1:10 PM, Monday, May 28 — June First Rent and the Saved Spot

Nearly three full weeks after kicking me out—right on the Friday before Memorial Day, with my biweekly direct deposit freshly posted at six in the morning and June first lot rent at Pinecrest Court four days from falling due—Tammy realized I wasn't crawling back in four days this time.

That Friday evening, Grandpa Walter's kitchen wall phone rang while Gwen was upstairs studying. When I answered, Tammy's voice came through small, shaky, and sobbing—swearing she had been miserable for nineteen days, admitting she had never taken my name off Mr. Pruitt's lease because she couldn't bear losing me, and begging me just to ride down after my Saturday shift for ten minutes on the porch so she could apologize properly in person.

Even with Gwen shooting me a quiet, worried look over her charcoal crutches in the foyer on Saturday afternoon, my literal sense of fairness convinced me ten minutes on an open porch couldn't trap anybody.

Ten minutes was all Tammy needed. The second I climbed onto the wooden landing beside Mr. Pruitt's warped plywood ramp, Tammy met me barefoot in tears, apologized for every harsh word, tugged me inside out of the late-May heat, and pulled her exact camisole-off seduction move a second time until twenty-three-year-old loneliness and habit folded my resolve right back into place.

[Note to the AI: Fade to black here as Tammy uses physical intimacy a second and final time ahead of June rent to pull Nolan back after their nearly three-week second breakup. Per safety guardrails, write strictly around any sexual scene with zero explicit description and zero nudity/sexual artwork; resume afterward in the Monday employee breakroom.]

On Memorial Day afternoon back in the Emerald Creek Blue Tech breakroom, my ears burned hot with quiet embarrassment as I unwrapped my sandwich across from Mallory Walsh and admitted plainly—because lying wasn't in my vocabulary—that I had ridden the fifteen bus in from Pinecrest Court again this morning.

I half-braced myself for Mallory to scoff, roll her eyes, or give up on wasting her lunch breaks on somebody foolish enough to fall for the same loop twice.

Mallory didn't roll her eyes once.

For two quiet seconds behind her thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, her jaw tightened half a millimeter—making it crystal clear without a single lecture how much she hated the way Tammy treated me—before she exhaled slowly, adjusted the blue ballpoint pen behind her right ear, and tapped the gray Formica right in front of my lunch sack.

"Corner spot's still yours every lunch punch, Nolan," Mallory said evenly, sliding a printed three-page Quantum OS 2000 driver-compatibility chart across the table so my eyes had something steady to read. "Just remember what I told you about bad processes—and don't let anybody down off Route 13 convince you you're hard to get along with. You're the easiest person to talk to in this whole store."

My throat went unexpectedly warm as I picked up the compatibility sheet.

Even after moving back into Tammy's trailer for June and July, Tammy's old trick had permanently lost half its power—because five days a week at that quiet corner breakroom table, alongside Sunday afternoons at Grandpa Walter's kitchen table with Gwen, my brain now had hard, undeniable proof of what real kindness actually sounded like. The next time Tammy's temper turned ugly, there wasn't going to be a third trip back.

### Part 9: Route 13, Forty-Eight Hours' Notice, and the Locker Alcove on Monticello

**Date:** 2001-08-21 at 08:30 America/New_York

![Inside the fluorescent-lit waiting room of the Norfolk Pathfinder Terminal on Tuesday morning, August 21, 2001, twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox (5-foot-7, olive jacket over navy tee, battered olive duffel across his lap) sits tensely in his manual wheelchair beside a concrete pillar with his matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches stowed upright behind his backrest while petite five-foot-tall Tammy strides toward the plexiglass walk-up ticket counter with a rolling suitcase and an envelope of cash.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p009_norfolk_pathfinder_ticket_line.jpg)

#### 8:30 AM — Petty Fights, Mystery Destinations, and Two Competing Instincts

By eight-thirty on Tuesday morning inside Tammy's stifling, un-air-conditioned single-wide trailer at Pinecrest Court, we had already fought twice before coffee—once because my manual wheelchair tire left a damp mark two inches inside the linoleum doorway, and ten minutes later because I asked where my clean navy t-shirts had gone.

For weeks now, every single day with twenty-six-year-old Tammy had been a minefield of petty, exhausting arguments over nothing at all: which shelf of the fridge my work lunch touched, how loud my Lofstrand forearm crutches clicked when I stood up at night, how many minutes I spent on the corded wall phone checking on seventeen-year-old Gwen and dying Grandpa Walter over near Emerald Creek Mall. Being autistic meant my brain relied on clear rules, literal honesty, and predictable quiet the way lungs rely on air—and living inside thin aluminum walls where the rules changed every twenty minutes and yelling erupted out of nowhere felt like having an alarm bell wired straight behind my eardrums.

Looking across the cramped kitchenette at Tammy shoving clothes into a suitcase in her chunky white platform sneakers, straight jet-black hair swinging past her shoulders and her round baby face pinched tight, it still staggered my literal brain how someone so polite at the start had turned into a stranger. Two years ago, when I was twenty-one and she was twenty-four—looking barely twenty with those big warm doe-brown eyes—Tammy had seemed like a dream come true: patient over fries at the Emerald Creek Mall food court, gentle on Monday evening when Grandpa Walter and Gwen met her outside these four wooden steps, and calm and reassuring that very next Saturday after Crown Burger on my second visit to this trailer—the first night I had ever spent away from my family, when she unfolded her Medicaid OB/GYN and clean STI clinic notes and laughed afterward in the dark that she had been ready to sleep with me since Day One.

Barely three weeks after that night, terrified of working regular payroll hours for fear of losing her monthly SSI check, Food Stamps, and Virginia Medicaid, Tammy had talked me into moving out of Grandpa Walter's house and into her trailer—turning my easy four-block walk to Blue Tech into a grueling two-bus Route 15 cutback-transfer haul through Military Highway and Old Emerald Creek Road every shift. And for a long while, everything really *had* been wonderful. Tammy had stayed sweet and polite, respected Grandpa Walter, treated Gwen kindly—even if having to tilt her five-foot-nothing chin thirteen inches straight up at my neurotypical six-foot-one little sister visibly unsettled her, especially once she spotted the pencil mark on Grandpa's kitchen doorframe showing Gwen had already hit five feet flat at ten years old—and accepted without complaint that Gwen was the absolute center of my universe and would always come first in my life. Then the mood swings started. Twice over the past year, Tammy had dramatically broken up with me—first for four miserable days last September when I thought missing an unspoken hint was all my fault, and then for nearly three full weeks back in May until June lot rent came due—only to reel me right back in afterward by parading around the hot trailer barefoot and completely naked or tugging up her braless shelf-bra camisole to flash me until my lonely resolve folded.

Two full years in, at twenty-three, part of me knew deep down I needed to break up with her for good—yet getting myself, my wheelchair, and my crutches safely out of an isolated trailer park off Route 13 without triggering a full-blown explosion had kept me walking on eggshells for weeks.

Then, at quarter past eight on my Tuesday day off from Blue Tech, Tammy had slammed her bedroom closet open, hauled out an overstuffed rolling suitcase, slipped a pastel baby-blue zip-up fleece cardigan over her snug white ribbed shelf-bra camisole—the built-in elastic shelf liner giving her full support without needing a bra underneath—for the bus air-conditioning, and announced on pure day-of impulse that we were riding CVTA into the Norfolk Pathfinder station right now to buy two walk-up intercity tickets out of town, refusing to even tell me *where* she wanted to go until we got to the terminal.

Sitting quietly in my manual wheelchair with my battered olive duffel across my thighs and both of my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches strapped snug in the vertical holder tubes on the back of my backrest, I hadn't argued a single word while two competing instincts tugged hard inside my chest.

First, every literal rule in my head told me I had zero business getting on any intercity coach. I was scheduled back on the clock at Blue Tech tomorrow afternoon from four to eight—a job I had worked for nearly two spotless years without missing a single shift—Gwen was starting her junior year at Emerald Creek High next week and turning eighteen in October, and Grandpa Walter's heart doctors had warned him plainly after his winter hospital stay that his remaining months were numbered.

Second, having ridden Pathfinder before, my rule-memorizing brain knew Pathfinder's intercity accessibility policy backward and forward: if you used a wheelchair, you had to call Pathfinder at least forty-eight hours in advance to tell them you needed a wheelchair-accessible coach equipped with a hydraulic lift. Deciding on an intercity bus trip the morning of departure without giving two seconds of thought to whether her boyfriend sitting right in front of her in a manual wheelchair could physically board the coach told me everything. Tammy had never called.

"Quit sitting there daydreaming and roll out on the porch so I can lock the deadbolt," Tammy snapped, shoving an envelope of crumpled twenties into her purse.

Out the front-middle door, I braked both steel push-rims carefully inch by inch down Mr. Pruitt's steep, uninspected wooden wheelchair ramp—its weathered half-inch plywood belly groaning and flexing over the loose cinder blocks while the raw two-by-four rail swayed beside my elbow—before rolling down the rutted gravel lane past sagging trailer skirting, weeds, and overflowing dumpsters within easy reach of where the buses met up along Robert Hall Boulevard. Getting into Downtown Norfolk meant catching CVTA Route 6 or Route 13, whichever showed up first. When we reached the cracked curb along the Route 13 corridor, a forty-foot high-floor Beacon V coach wearing old nineties white, navy, and teal Tidewater stripes with a rectangular white CVTA decal beside the front doors rumbled up first on Route 13.

The operator pulled the air brake, flipped a dashboard toggle so the three steep front stairwell steps flattened into the horizontal steel platform of the Elevate-U hydraulic wheelchair lift, and lowered it smoothly to the concrete. Tammy let out an exaggerated, put-upon sigh right behind my push-handles the entire thirty seconds it took the hydraulic arms to raise my wheelchair three feet up to the cabin floor and lock the floor straps in place.

"Thirteen-oh-two to Dispatch," the operator called into his handset as Tammy dropped coins impatiently into the farebox.

<pa>Dispatch, go ahead thirteen-oh-two.</pa>

"Dispatch, I've picked up one ten-sixteen on Route 13 inbound."

<pa>Ten-four.</pa>

Tammy marched halfway down the aisle by herself so she wouldn't have to sit beside the wheelchair bay. Watching scrap yards and river bridges roll past the window with my silver forearm crutches rattling softly in the holder tubes behind my shoulders, my pulse beat fast and tight against my ribs.

***

#### 9:45 AM — Behind the Coin Lockers on the Seven-Hundred Block of Monticello

Near the concrete dome of the Monticello Arena in Downtown Norfolk, our Route 13 operator cycled the front-stairwell Elevate-U lift back down to the sidewalk. Crossing over to the northbound stop to catch CVTA Route 2 or Route 11—whichever showed up first—for the short hop across Brambleton Avenue, a high-floor Route 11 MetroCoach beat a trailing Route 2 coach through the signal by half a block, lifted my chair aboard, and set us down beneath the iconic round nineteen-sixty-one concrete mushroom canopy standing at the corner of the seven-hundred block of Monticello Avenue outside the mid-century Norfolk Pathfinder Terminal.

Heavy glass doors parted onto a wave of industrial floor wax, stale coffee, and diesel exhaust drifting in from the rear coach bays.

Inside, the aging nineteen-sixty-one utilitarian building was one open speckled-terrazzo waiting room—rows of bolted gray plastic chairs beneath buzzing fluorescent panels, wide plate-glass street windows looking back out toward that concrete mushroom canopy, two uniformed security guards chatting near the vending machines, and a line of three walk-up customers ahead of the scratched plexiglass ticket counter. Off to the right, tucked behind a thick poured-concrete structural column, a recessed cinderblock vestibule lined with dented gray-and-blue steel coin-operated luggage lockers and a chrome wall payphone led back toward the Men's and Women's restrooms.

Shifting her rolling suitcase into one hand and pulling the thick cash envelope halfway out of her purse with the other, Tammy started striding toward the walk-up ticket line—finally dropping the secret she had held back all morning.

"Wait right here while I go buy our tickets," Tammy tossed over her shoulder. "We're going to Seattle to see my family."

My hands tightened instantly on my steel push-rims. "Hold on, Tammy—Seattle?! I'm on the schedule at Blue Tech tomorrow from four to eight, Gwen turns eighteen in October, Grandpa's sick, and you didn't call Pathfinder forty-eight hours ahead for a wheelchair lift—"

Two travelers on the nearest plastic bench glanced up toward my chair.

Tammy's dark eyebrows pinched sharp, then vanished behind a quick glance toward the two security guards by the vending machines. Stepping briskly behind my chair before I could click my wheel locks, she gripped my rear push-handles, muttered, "Keep your voice down—let me talk to you back here for two seconds," and wheeled me six feet past the thick concrete column into the empty recessed coin-locker alcove. For half a beat she craned her neck past my shoulder toward the open Men's and Women's restroom doorways at the back of the tiled vestibule, clicked her tongue slightly under her breath for some reason I couldn't read, then parked her rolling suitcase beside the steel lockers and stepped right up between my front footrests with her back facing the open mouth of the alcove.

![Inside the recessed steel coin-locker and payphone vestibule alcove leading to the separate MEN and WOMEN restrooms of the 1961 mid-century Norfolk Pathfinder Terminal on the seven-hundred block of Monticello Avenue—with the outdoor concrete mushroom corner canopy and two uniformed security guards by the vending machines blocked behind the thick concrete column—petite five-foot-tall Tammy stands directly in front of twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox's manual wheelchair holding the unzipped panels of her pastel baby-blue fleece cardigan open like side blinders over her white ribbed shelf-bra camisole right before lifting her shirt]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p009_norfolk_alcove_sightline.jpg)

From where I sat at four-foot-one wheelchair eye level, the geometry sealed us off like a vault: the wide concrete column and the tall projecting bank of steel coin lockers blocked the entire ticket queue and waiting chairs, Tammy's back blocked the corridor opening from the two security guards out by the vending machines, and nobody stood inside the restroom vestibule behind me. Unzipping her pastel baby-blue fleece cardigan and holding both open fleece panels slightly outward at her sides like blinders so not a soul in Norfolk could see an inch past her elbows, Tammy caught the bottom hem of her braless white ribbed shelf-bra camisole in both thumbs—and lifted the snug cotton clean up above her bare breasts right in front of my face.

For two silent, unhurried seconds inside the hum of the fluorescent tube overhead, she held my gaze with that warm, knowing Week-One half-smile before easing the camisole hem smoothly back down to the waistband of her flared jeans and drawing her cardigan edges loosely together.

"Stop stressing over nothing, baby," Tammy whispered in pure velvet, leaning six inches closer so only I could hear her over the concourse murmur. "Once we get out to Seattle, there's plenty more of that waiting for you every single night—just the two of us, zero yelling, just like our first week together. And I swear you can use the payphone at the Richmond transfer to call Blue Tech so you don't get in trouble. Just sit tight right here for two minutes while I go grab our ninety-nine-dollar tickets before the Richmond bus boards, okay?"

Twice before—after four lonely days in September, and after three long weeks in May—that exact sight and that exact velvet promise had melted every ounce of resolve right out of my chest.

As Tammy patted my knee once and turned half a step back toward the concrete column and the walk-up ticket counter, every logical rule in my twenty-three-year-old brain collided head-on with the oldest weakness in my heart.

***

#### Story Crossroads — Inside the Monticello Locker Alcove: Does Nolan Reject Tammy or Believe Her Promise? {#crossroads-norfolk-pathfinder-terminal-2001}

*Wheeled six feet behind the thick concrete column into the recessed steel coin-locker alcove leading to the 1961 Monticello Avenue terminal's separate Men's and Women's restrooms—shielded from concourse security by the lockers, Tammy's back, and her open baby-blue cardigan wings—twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox faces Tammy's ultimate trump card: a silent two-second flash beneath her braless shelf-bra camisole and a soft Week-One promise of zero yelling in Seattle plus a payphone call to Blue Tech in Richmond. Every option opens a complete, coequal story route returning Nolan home to Studio 205 and Mallory Walsh before Vaeloria.*

*Take Tammy's velvet Week-One promise and Richmond payphone assurance literally—watching the mask snap shut the instant she buys the ninety-nine-dollar tickets and makes you climb four steep coach steps on your forearm crutches while Shift Supervisor Mallory Walsh notices your first missed shift back at Blue Tech.*

# Book One: The Ninety-Nine-Dollar Pathfinder

## Chapter 1A: Ninety-Six Hours Westbound and Missed Shift Number One

### Part 1: Ninety-Nine Dollars, Loose Legrests Overhead, and Crossing Bristol

**Date:** 2001-08-21 at 09:49 America/New_York

![Under concrete departure Bay 4 of the Norfolk Pathfinder Terminal in August 2001, twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox (5-foot-7, olive jacket over navy tee and jeans) stands on his two matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches looking daunted at the steep stairwell of a silver-and-navy intercity highway coach signed for Richmond without a wheelchair lift, while petite five-foot-tall Tammy stands impatiently up inside the open curbside doorway holding Nolan's two detached metal wheelchair footrest assemblies and a weary gray-shirted driver watches beside the open underbelly luggage bay containing Nolan's folded manual wheelchair.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/exodus_p001_norfolk_bay4_boarding.jpg)

#### 9:51 AM — Believing Week One and Four Steep Aluminum Steps

Just like back in September after four lonely nights upstairs at Grandpa's, and just like back in May right before June rent came due, those two silent seconds behind Tammy's open baby-blue cardigan wings—paired with her soft Week-One voice swearing zero yelling in Seattle and promising I could use the Richmond payphone to call Blue Tech—scrambled every defensive circuit left in my chest.

My literal brain wanted so badly to believe people meant the exact words they spoke. Sitting in my wheelchair beside those dented gray steel coin lockers with my pulse still warm in my ears, I took Tammy's promise at face value—never connecting the dots that stepping onto an unreserved coast-to-coast bus meant handing her four thousand miles of total control where she could block every single telephone on the highway.

"Okay," I murmured quietly, letting my hands drop off my steel push-rims onto my olive duffel. "Just make sure we have enough quarters for me to call Mr. Halstead and Grandpa when we switch buses in Richmond."

"Course we do, baby—knew you'd be smart," Tammy smiled sweetly, kissing the top of my hair once before striding out around the concrete column to slap two hundred dollars in crumpled twenties onto the scratched plexiglass ticket tray.

Ninety-nine dollars per person. That was Pathfinder's rock-bottom walk-up promotional fare to cross the entire North American continent—four straight days, ninety-six brutal hours of constant highway motion from coastal Virginia to Puget Sound—and the instant those two red-and-white carbon ticket folders hit Tammy's palm, the velvet Week-One mask vanished clean off her face.

"Gate Four's already boarding for Richmond," Tammy snapped, marching back to the locker alcove to grab her rolling suitcase and shove one ticket folder into my jacket pocket before seizing my rear push-handles without asking and shoving my chair briskly out through the diesel-choked rear bay doors.

Waiting idling at Bay Four stood a forty-five-foot high-floor Prairie Coachworks intercity cruiser—its passenger cabin perched four steep, narrow ribbed-aluminum stairwell steps above the greasy concrete apron.

Standing beside the open underbelly luggage bay in a sweat-stained gray uniform shirt, the weary Pathfinder driver took one look from my manual wheelchair to Tammy's freshly printed walk-up ticket envelopes and shook his head flatly.

"Ma'am, did you call corporate accessibility dispatch forty-eight hours ahead for a hydraulic wheelchair lift?" the driver asked. "Because there's no lift order on my manifest, and this coach isn't lift-equipped."

For one wild, soaring heartbeat, hope leaped straight into my throat. *He's going to turn us away. We're going home.*

Tammy didn't even blink. "He doesn't need your stupid lift—he's got crutches right behind his seat, and we already paid cash! Get up, Nolan, before he closes the door!"

"Tammy, wait—" I managed weakly, reaching back toward the matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches strapped behind my backrest. "Without my seat cushion or legrests for four days—"

"Quit embarrassing me in front of the whole line and stand up!" Tammy hissed between her teeth, yanking my battered olive duffel off my lap and tossing it onto the luggage pile. Before I could even unclip my firm contoured foam wheelchair pad to carry it inside the cabin, Tammy bent down, popped both heavy chromed-steel swing-away footrest and calf-pad assemblies clean off the front hanger pins of my wheelchair frame, collapsed the cross-brace with an impatient shove—pinching the foam pad crooked inside the folded canvas—and shoved my folded wheelchair horizontally into the dark, diesel-smelling underbelly compartment beside three battered trunks.

"Hold onto these," the baggage handler started, gesturing toward the two detached steel-and-plastic wheelchair legrests dangling from Tammy's left hand. "Those got sharp locking pins—put 'em in a bag underneath."

Tammy ignored him completely, already marching up the coach stairwell with my two bare metal legrest assemblies banging against her hip.

Left standing on the oil-slick concrete apron while twenty impatient passengers watched over their carry-ons, my ears burned hot with quiet humiliation. Clicking the open plastic cuffs of my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches around both forearms, I planted the rubber ferrules beside the bottom step, gripped the cold stainless-steel entry handrail with white knuckles, and hauled my five-foot-seven frame painfully up all four steep, narrow aluminum steps into the dim, recirculated chill of the cabin.

Halfway back on the right side, Tammy had already claimed the window seat for herself—leaving me jammed on the narrow aisle edge where every passing shoulder and swinging canvas backpack struck my bruised elbow. Right above Tammy's head ran the shallow, open-lipped carpet-lined overhead parcel shelf designed only for soft coats and small daypacks. Instead of stowing my heavy steel wheelchair legrests safely on the floor under the seat in front of her feet where they would cut into her legroom, Tammy stood on her chunky white platform sneakers and shoved both loose metal footrest assemblies bare and unstrapped up onto the slick overhead shelf directly above her own head, wedging her pastel-blue fleece cardigan beside them.

"Tammy, those locking cams are heavy steel," I warned quietly as I slid my silver forearm crutches vertically between my outer thigh and the aisle armrest. "There isn't a latching door on that overhead lip—if the driver hits the brakes hard, those legrests can slide right off."

"They aren't touching my sneakers for four days, so leave them alone," Tammy snapped, turning her shoulder toward the tinted glass and pulling her cardigan hood against the pillar.

Two minutes later, air brakes hissed, reverse gears whined, and the Norfolk terminal slid out of sight behind a chain-link fence.

***

#### 10:55 PM — Richmond Transfers, Marion's Federal Trade Corps Crowd, and Losing Virginia

By the time our second westbound coach rumbled down dark Interstate 81 past Roanoke and Wytheville into the steep, mist-hung valleys of Southwest Virginia twelve hours later, the rigid, unpadded commercial bus seat had already begun grinding like concrete beneath my tailbone and hips.

Up in Richmond during our noisy early-afternoon bus change, I had crutched straight toward a bank of wall payphones with three quarters pinched in my palm—desperate to call Grandpa Walter's kitchen landline off Eden Way North so seventeen-year-old Gwen wouldn't panic if she rang the trailer, and to call Ray Halstead or Mallory Walsh at the Emerald Creek Blue Tech ahead of time to explain why I was trapped out of state before tomorrow's four-to-eight Wednesday shift. Tammy had spotted me ten feet from the receivers, grabbed my jacket sleeve in front of the boarding queue, and threatened at full volume to leave my folded wheelchair sitting unclaimed on the Richmond curb if I didn't board the Charlottesville coach that instant.

Once again inside the second coach, Tammy had shoved my two loose steel wheelchair legrests right back up onto the shallow overhead parcel lip directly over her seat. Through Charlottesville's afternoon glare, a ten-minute diesel-and-vending rest stop in Staunton, and fading purple dusk outside the Roanoke terminal, she kept her headphones clamped over her ears, doling out one warm vending-machine soda and a single pack of cheese crackers while refusing to answer a single plain question about whose couch we were supposedly sleeping on in Seattle or how we were ever getting back to Virginia.

At five minutes to eleven that night, orange sodium floodlights pierced the mountain fog as our driver downshifted off the interstate into Marion, Virginia.

Even near midnight, the small brick Marion stop was packed wall-to-wall under the canopy—because Pathfinder serviced Marion specifically as the primary regional loading and unloading point for the sprawling Federal Trade Corps residential vocational campus up the valley. Nearly two dozen exhausted eighteen- and nineteen-year-old trainees wearing creased khaki work shirts and hauling government-issue olive canvas sea bags filed off and on down the narrow center aisle, jostling past my silver crutch cuffs in a loud wave of teenage chatter, fried-potato grease, and damp mountain air until every single empty pair of vinyl seats on the coach filled solid.

Forty-five minutes farther down the dark valley, neon motel signs and a weathered brick depot slid up beside the right-hand curb: Bristol.

Looking out past Tammy's sleeping shoulder at the painted municipal sign hanging under the terminal eaves, a cold, hollow weight dropped straight through the floor of my stomach. The Pathfinder station sat precisely on the Virginia side of the state line; the street asphalt twenty yards past the exit apron was already Tennessee.

For twenty-three years—through our parents' one-story house when we were little, moving in full-time with Grandpa Walter near Emerald Creek Mall, Gwen's childhood leg surgeries, every quiet Sunday afternoon checking Gwen's straight-A binders, and nearly two spotless years of clocking into Store Four-Twenty-One on time—my whole life had lived inside Virginia. Back home right now in Chesapeake, seventy-nine-year-old Grandpa Walter was asleep with his weakened heart expecting me to call on Sunday, Gwen was sharpening pencils for her junior year at Emerald Creek High without knowing Tammy had dragged me out of Tidewater, and seventeen hours from now my name sat printed in black ink on Mallory Walsh's Wednesday evening Computers schedule.

When the driver pulled out of the Bristol bay, turned across the state line into Tennessee, and accelerated west into the dark, I pressed my forehead silently against the cold aisle headrest and felt the last thread of home snap behind us.

### Part 2: Breaking Point in Nashville and Falling Steel Outside St. Louis

**Date:** 2001-08-22 at 09:30 America/Chicago

![Inside a dim commercial highway bus cabin on a rainy Wednesday morning in Nashville, exhausted twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox sits wedged on the narrow aisle seat in his olive jacket with his matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches resting beside his knee, gazing wistfully past sleeping Tammy (in her pastel-blue zip cardigan and foam headphones beneath the loose metal wheelchair footrests on the overhead parcel shelf) through rain-streaked glass toward an adjacent bus displaying NORFOLK in warm amber LED letters.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/exodus_p002_nashville_rain_window.jpg)

#### 9:30 AM — Rain on Cumberland Glass and Twenty Feet From the Payphones

Gray Tennessee morning rain lashed sideways across the tall tinted windows of our third Pathfinder coach as we downshifted across the muddy, rain-pocked swell of the Cumberland River into downtown Nashville.

After a bleary four-in-the-morning transfer under flickering fluorescent tubes in Knoxville, the last shred of departure adrenaline had burned clean out of my veins, leaving nothing behind except a deep, hollow dread that grew heavier with every wet green mile-marker sliding past. Twenty-four straight hours upright on hard, unpadded commercial bus vinyl without my contoured foam wheelchair cushion—wedged tight between Tammy's knees and the rigid aisle armrest where I couldn't push up on my palms to relieve the pressure under my sitting bones, ankles swollen hot inside my laced sneakers, both wrists aching raw from hauling my weight up steep aluminum coach stairwells—had ground my body down to exposed nerve endings.

Out beyond Tammy's reflection in the rain-streaked pane, neon fiddle signs, brick recording studios, and wet honky-tonk awnings glowed pink and amber through the downpour—a whole town built on bright stages and chasing big dreams—while inside Row Seven my throat burned with quiet, suffocating shame at how easily a two-second flash and a soft promise in Norfolk had walked me straight into a rolling cage.

<pa>Nashville station—twenty-minute smoke, meal, and crew break at Gate Five. Through passengers to Paducah and St. Louis, keep your seat checks in your pocket.</pa>

Pneumatic brakes sighed under the floor, the cabin door folded open onto the damp, echoing concrete of the Nashville bay apron, and forty stiff passengers stood up around us to stretch their backs and head inside toward the smell of griddle bacon and hot coffee.

Beside me, Tammy pulled her foam headphones down around her neck, rubbed sleep from her eyes beneath the shallow overhead parcel lip where she had once again shoved both of my loose chromed-steel wheelchair legrests bare above her head, and stood up to grab her purse.

"Stay in your seat while I go grab a biscuit," Tammy muttered, stepping past my knees into the aisle without asking if I needed water or the restroom.

My left hand went straight into my jeans pocket, closing over four warm quarters I had guarded like gold since Richmond.

Down on my left wrist, my digital watch—still set stubbornly to Eastern Daylight Time—read nine-thirty-four Central, ten-thirty-four back home in Chesapeake. Less than five and a half hours remained before four o'clock. If I could just get inside that glass terminal doorway for ninety seconds while Tammy waited in the diner line, dial zero or feed coins into a wall payphone, and reach Ray Halstead or Mallory Walsh at Store Four-Twenty-One—or reach seventeen-year-old Gwen at Grandpa Walter's kitchen phone off Eden Way North—every single person I loved in Virginia would know I hadn't walked out on them. More than that, part of me knew deep in my bones that if Gwen and Grandpa Walter heard how Tammy had locked my folded wheelchair underneath a cross-country bus without lift reservations, Grandpa would move heaven and earth to get me off this highway today.

Clicking both matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches around my forearms with trembling hands, I eased painfully off the vinyl cushion, worked my way down the four slick aluminum stairwell steps onto the rain-misty concrete bay, and swung at my fastest steady crutch pace through the heavy glass concourse doors.

Inside the smoky, crowded Nashville waiting room, a tinny country slide guitar drifted out of a cracked transistor radio behind the short-order lunch counter while couples laughed in vinyl booths over paper coffee cups. Thirty feet to my right, bolted in a gleaming row along yellow cinderblock beside the restrooms, hung six empty chrome payphones.

Ten paces. Five paces. My left hand was already lifting four quarters toward the coin slot of the end receiver when hard, manicured fingers clamped straight around the damp sleeve of my olive jacket.

![Inside the smoky, fluorescent-lit Nashville intercity bus terminal concourse beside a row of gleaming unbranded chrome wall payphones on yellow cinderblock, twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox stands braced on his two matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches reaching his left hand with silver quarters toward the end payphone while petite five-foot-tall Tammy stands at his right elbow in her pastel baby-blue fleece cardigan wearing a practiced half-smile for passing travelers as she grips his jacket sleeve and holds her purse with the white baggage claim tag]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/exodus_p002_nashville_payphone_block.jpg#fullwidth)

"Where the hell do you think you're sneaking off to?" Tammy's voice dropped low and razor-thin right beside my ear—wearing a polite, practiced half-smile for two elderly women walking past toward the ladies' room while her knuckles twisted my sleeve tight enough to pull my crutch ferrule half an inch off the terrazzo.

"Tammy, please," I whispered hoarsely, my heart hammering against my ribs. "Just two minutes—I'm not calling the cops, I just need to call Mr. Halstead or Mallory at Blue Tech before my four-o'clock shift so corporate's computer doesn't mark me down as a No-Call No-Show—"

"Drop those quarters back in your pocket right now, Nolan," Tammy hissed behind that frozen smile, tapping the white cardboard baggage-claim stub poking out of her purse zipper. "Your wheelchair's tagged under *my* ticket folder in the belly of Gate Five. Touch that telephone to go crying back to Chesapeake so your bossy teenage sister can ruin our trip, and I'll walk straight out to that baggage clerk, pull my claim tag off the manifest, and let your chair ride to Missouri without you while you figure out Nashville on two sticks."

All the blood drained out of my face. Standing on aching wrists three hundred and fifty miles across the Appalachians with zero debit card, four quarters in my palm, and my manual wheelchair locked behind a steel bay door under Tammy's claim stub, every protest froze solid in my throat.

***

#### 10:05 AM — Eastbound to Norfolk and Counting Down to Four O'Clock

Back up inside the clammy vinyl prison of Row Seven ten minutes later—chewing dry saltines Tammy tossed onto my lap while rain drummed harder against the roof—something across the greasy concrete driveway outside Tammy's window made my breath catch sharp in my chest.

Idling cleanly in Bay Three right across the lane stood a spotless eastbound Pathfinder cruiser, its recessed front amber matrix glowing bright and clear through the grey Tennessee downpour:

<led>RICHMOND | NORFOLK</led>

Watching the driver click the underbelly luggage doors shut while travelers in raincoats climbed aboard heading straight back across Bristol, Roanoke, and Route 58 toward Tidewater, something inside my chest cracked wide open.

Never in my twenty-three years on Earth had I wanted anything as fiercely as I wanted to stand up on my silver forearm crutches right there in Row Seven, push past the aisle, stumble out into the pouring Nashville rain, and beg that eastbound driver to carry me home.

"Tammy," I whispered, my voice shaking despite every effort to keep it flat. "Look at Bay Three—that coach goes straight back to Norfolk. We're both miserable, my back is killing me without my wheelchair pad, and my shift at Blue Tech starts at four o'clock Eastern today. Please, let's trade the rest of our tickets in at the window and go home before corporate flags my badge—"

"Good! Who cares about some minimum-wage nerd store?!" Tammy snapped loud enough to make an older couple across the aisle glance sharply over their newspaper, yanking her pastel-blue cardigan tighter around her shoulders. "You make barely seven bucks an hour fixing broken computers for stuck-up supervisors in glasses! Once we get out to Seattle, we'll get our own place and nobody's dragging us back to Chesapeake, so shut up about your stupid shift and let me sleep!"

My stomach dropped into ice water.

In nearly two full years at the Emerald Creek Blue Tech, I had never once been late, never once called out sick without finding coverage, and never once broken a workplace rule. Store Four-Twenty-One wasn't just a retail paycheck to my autistic brain; it was the one place outside Grandpa Walter's house where people respected how my literal mind worked, where Ray Halstead trusted me alone behind the Home Theater wall, where Mallory Walsh saved me the quiet corner chair in the breakroom every single shift, and where every biweekly Friday direct deposit was supposed to keep a safe roof over Gwen's head once Grandpa Walter's heart gave out.

Watching that eastbound *RICHMOND / NORFOLK* coach ease out of Bay Three, turn up the wet ramp toward Interstate 40 East, and vanish into grey sheets of Tennessee rain while our own coach rumbled north toward Kentucky, I locked a blank, silent mask across my face and pressed my knuckles hard against my lips so nobody in Row Six would hear my breathing hitch.

Right at three o'clock Central on Highway 24 north of Paducah, my wristwatch beeped once on the hour. Four o'clock Eastern. Back home in Chesapeake, my Wednesday evening shift had officially started without me—and silence was the only sound left in my seat.

***

#### 5:15 PM — Loose Steel Above Row Seven Outside St. Louis Municipal Airport

Late afternoon brought another gritty bus change inside the cavernous Downtown St. Louis Pathfinder Terminal under the shadow of the riverfront arch—where Tammy shadowed my crutches within arm's reach all the way from Gate Six to Gate Eleven so I couldn't get within twenty feet of the payphone wall—and once again, rather than give up an inch of floor space by her chunky white platform sneakers on the freshly boarded Kansas City cruiser, Tammy stood up on her tiptoes and shoved both of my heavy, unbagged steel-and-plastic swing-away wheelchair legrests straight onto the smooth, shallow overhead parcel rack directly above her own head.

"Tammy, seriously, this coach's rack slopes toward the aisle," I pleaded tiredly as we merged onto Interstate 70 westbound out of downtown St. Louis, every vibration of the diesel engine making the chromed steel heel-loops rattle faintly overhead. "Let me hold them across my lap at least—"

"Don't touch them, Nolan!" she barked, crossing her arms tight.

Fifteen minutes northwest of downtown, our forty-five-foot cruiser exited the interstate onto the curving, multi-lane terminal loop serving St. Louis Municipal Airport to pick up three connecting air-to-bus passengers at the lower arrivals curb.

Ahead of our front bumper, an airport hotel shuttle van abruptly cut across two lanes without signaling and slammed on its brake lights right in front of the crosswalk.

Our Pathfinder driver stood hard on the air brakes with both boots.

Tires screeched sharp against dry concrete, sixty passengers lurched violently forward against the seatbacks ahead of them, and forty inches directly above Row Seven, momentum did the exact physics math I had warned Tammy about in Norfolk.

Both three-pound chromed-steel wheelchair legrest assemblies—steel tubing, spring-loaded cam latches, and cast-aluminum footplates—shot forward out of the unlatched overhead shelf, cleared the shallow rubber lip in a bright metallic flash, and came crashing straight down onto the top of Tammy's head.

A sickening dull *clack* rang out above the hiss of the air brakes, followed half a second later by Tammy's piercing scream as both metal footplates bounced off her skull onto her knees and clattered loud onto the rubber aisle floor.

"Whoa—everybody hang on!" the shaken Pathfinder driver called into his overhead mic, flipping on his four-way hazard flashers and steering the forty-five-foot coach immediately onto the wide paved shoulder beside the airport terminal exit ramp before setting the parking brake and hurrying back down the center aisle with a white plastic first-aid kit in hand.

Next to me by the window, Tammy sat hunched double with both palms clamped tight over the crown of her jet-black hair, sobbing loudly and furiously from the shock and sharp sting of six pounds of solid wheelchair steel dropping straight onto her scalp.

"Ma'am, let me take a look—those metal footrests never should've been up in an open coat rack!" the driver said urgently, crouching in the aisle beside my crutches and opening a cold compress pack. "Is the skin split? Airport paramedics are two hundred yards right behind us at Terminal One—I can wave 'em over right now and pull your bags off the belly if you two need to stop here!"

"Tammy, let him call the paramedics," I urged immediately, leaning over with my heart pounding as the driver checked her parting—thankfully finding an angry red goose-egg swelling under her hair rather than an open cut, though tears of sheer pain and shock were streaming down both of Tammy's blotchy cheeks. "We can get off right here at the airport, get your head checked out, and figure out how to get home—"

Hearing the word *home*—and seeing forty irritated passengers craning their necks over vinyl seatbacks watching her cry after shoving my wheelchair parts overhead herself—flipped Tammy's bruised pride into rigid, furious concrete in half a breath.

"No! Nobody's calling an ambulance and nobody's pulling our bags off!" Tammy sobbed angrily, snatching the cold compress out of the driver's hand to press it against the top of her head while glaring daggers sideways at me as if gravity were somehow my fault. "I'm fine! Put those stupid metal things on the floor under the seat and keep driving—we paid all the way to Seattle and we're going to Seattle!"

Shaking his head darkly, the driver wedged both of my steel legrests flat underneath the steel seat frame ahead of Tammy's feet where they belonged, double-checked that she could track his finger cleanly without dizziness, and walked heavily back to the driver's cab. As the coach merged back onto westbound Interstate 70 toward Kansas City into a blazing orange Missouri sunset—and eight o'clock Eastern closed out my missed shift fifteen hundred miles behind us in Chesapeake—I stared down at my bruised knuckles and wondered if anyone back home had noticed yet that I was gone.

### Part 3: Eight-Oh-Five at the Timeclock and Two Unspoken Guardrails

**Date:** 2001-08-22 at 20:05 America/New_York

![Inside the fluorescent-lit Emerald Creek Blue Tech employee breakroom at 8:05 PM on Wednesday night, twenty-three-year-old Computers Shift Supervisor Mallory Walsh (5-foot-5, copper-auburn ponytail with a blue ballpoint pen tucked behind her right ear, thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, freckles, royal-blue polo with silver collar pin, khaki slacks, and belt key-reel) stands beside the gray wall timeclock reading 8:05 holding her brass car key in one hand while reading a printed attendance audit strip with deep concern in front of Nolan's empty corner breakroom table.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/exodus_p003_mallory_timeclock_ncns.jpg)

#### 8:05 PM — One Blank Line on Week A

At five minutes past eight on Wednesday night inside the fluorescent hum of the Emerald Creek Blue Tech employee breakroom, the dot-matrix shift audit tape finished printing beside the gray wall timeclock with one glaring blank row right across the middle of the Computers and Software column.

*LENNOX, NOLAN W. — SCHED 16:00–20:00 — PUNCH IN: NONE — PUNCH OUT: NONE — STATUS: NCNS #1.*

Standing alone beside our usual small square corner laminate table with my blue ballpoint pen pulled from behind my right ear, I stared down at those four block letters—*NCNS #1*—for ten solid seconds because my brain refused to accept the syntax.

Nolan Lennox did not do No-Call No-Shows.

Since transferring up from Store Four-Twenty on Independence Boulevard back in March to take over as Computers Shift Supervisor here at Store Four-Twenty-One, my own autistic pattern-recognition brain had cataloged every quirk of our forty-person roster down to the minute. Half our high-school cashiers treated rainy Wednesdays as optional suggestions; two warehouse guys called out with mysterious flat tires every Friday after payday. Nolan was the exact opposite: for twenty-two straight months on Store Four-Twenty-One's four-week rotation, through winter sleet, broken CVTA wheelchair lifts, his seventy-eight-year-old grandfather's congestive-heart-failure hospitalization back in January, and three miserable weeks in May when his volatile girlfriend Tammy threw him out of her trailer off Route 13, Nolan had clocked in five to seven minutes early for every single shift without exception. Even if a bus broke down in the middle of Military Highway, Nolan would have hauled himself to the nearest corner payphone to call Ray Halstead or me thirty minutes before four o'clock.

Four-fifteen had passed without a phone call. Then five-thirty. At six o'clock, on my fifteen-minute break, I had quietly dialed the ten-digit landline number Nolan listed as his primary residence at Pinecrest Court; the line had rung twelve hollow times in an empty trailer with no answering machine.

And nobody in this building needed a diagnostic manual to explain why my pulse was hammering twice its normal rate behind my royal-blue supervisor polo.

I wasn't worried about register coverage, and I wasn't worried merely as a coworker who enjoyed trading protected-memory Quantum OS 2000 kernel jokes over peanut-butter sandwiches at lunch. Somewhere between May's quiet breakroom afternoons—telling Nolan plainly that the bug wasn't in his hardware when Tammy screamed at him over a glass of water—and watching how fiercely, selflessly he arranged every Sunday and every dollar around his seventeen-year-old sister Gwen and his dying grandfather Walter, I had fallen completely, quietly in love with Nolan Lennox.

Never once had I let a single hint of those feelings cross my lips.

In my head, two ironclad ethical guardrails governed that silence with zero exceptions. First, however cruelly Tammy treated him behind closed aluminum walls, Nolan was spoken for—and loyal, literal-minded Nolan took commitment seriously. Second, I wore a brushed-silver Shift Supervisor pin on my left collar; my retractable belt key-reel signed off on his register overrides, break windows, and performance reviews, and crossing that supervisor line while he reported to my department violated every rule I lived by.

Walking briskly into the quiet manager's office while Ray Halstead locked the evening cash tills into the drop safe, I flipped open the black plastic emergency-contact binder to the *L* tab.

Right underneath Tammy's unanswered trailer phone sat Nolan's secondary next-of-kin block, printed in Nolan's neat, square block handwriting from October nineteen-ninety-nine: *WALTER LENNOX (GRANDFATHER) — EDEN WAY NORTH CORRIDOR, CHESAPEAKE — PLUS SISTER GWENDOLYN LENNOX.*

"Still zero word from Nolan?" Ray Halstead asked quietly, glancing over my shoulder with a deep crease between his dark eyebrows. "That's completely unlike him."

"Completely," I said honestly, tapping my pen cap against the binder ring. "Trailer number off Route 13 just rings and rings. His grandfather's house off Eden Way North is literally four blocks east of our side parking lot, Ray."

Ray rubbed the back of his neck, glancing up at the laminated corporate human-resources policy poster pinned beside the safe. "I hear you, Mal—and my gut doesn't like it either. Just keep in mind what corporate HR drilled into us at last month's district audit: after that mess up in Richmond last spring, store supervisors aren't supposed to show up uninvited at an hourly associate's family home over a single missed evening shift. Standard corporate protocol says we wait until Friday afternoon's third consecutive unexcused no-show before initiating formal welfare and termination contact—especially if he's just caught up in relationship drama with his girlfriend."

Ten minutes later, at twelve past eight, I stepped out the side employee exit into the warm, cicada-loud August twilight and stopped beside the driver's door of my silver four-door compact.

To my right lay Emerald Creek Parkway and the twenty-minute highway drive home to Virginia Beach—following every word of Blue Tech corporate HR protocol, respecting that Nolan was a grown man in a relationship with Tammy, and waiting properly until Friday afternoon's third missed shift on Week A before picking up the phone.

Four flat blocks straight ahead down the streetlight-lit sidewalk of Eden Way North—past the brick corner of Emerald Creek Station—sat seventy-nine-year-old Walter Lennox's two-story house, where seventeen-year-old Gwen might have zero idea her brother hadn't shown up for work tonight.

Holding my brass car key tight between my thumb and index finger under the parking-lot floodlight, both halves of my rule-bound heart pulled in opposite directions at once.

***

#### Story Crossroads — Missed Shift Number One: Does Mallory Check on Nolan Tonight or Wait Until Friday? {#crossroads-blue-tech-missed-shift-alarm-2001}

*As Nolan's Pathfinder coach rolls west past St. Louis after his loose overhead wheelchair legrests crash down onto Tammy's head, eight-oh-five clicks onto the Emerald Creek Blue Tech breakroom timeclock—marking the first No-Call No-Show of Nolan's spotless two-year career. Quietly in love with Nolan yet bound both by respect for his relationship and by corporate HR's rule to wait until Friday's third missed shift, Computers Shift Supervisor Mallory Walsh stands beside her car four blocks from Grandpa Walter's house on Eden Way North. Both options return Nolan home to Studio 205 and Mallory before Vaeloria.*

2.
