# Book One: Intercept Before the Restroom

## Chapter 1B: Eden Way North, US-287, and Five Minutes to Spare in Pasco

### Part 1: Eden Way North at Eight-Eighteen and Two Pre-Dawn Tickets West

**Date:** 2001-08-22 at 20:18 America/New_York

![Around the sunlit oak-and-laminate kitchen table of Grandpa Walter's Chesapeake home in August 2001, seventy-nine-year-old retired Navy machinist Walter Lennox (in wire-rimmed glasses and a beige cardigan over a blue shirt) slides his blue-and-gold credit union card firmly across the tabletop while seventeen-year-old Gwen Lennox (6-foot-1, burgundy top, with her matte-charcoal single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches leaning behind her chair) points out the Pasco arrival window on a printed coast-to-coast bus schedule alongside off-duty Mallory Walsh.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/pasco_p001_walter_kitchen_timetable.jpg)

#### 8:18 PM — Breaking Protocol Four Blocks Down Eden Way North

Instead of turning right onto Emerald Creek Parkway toward Virginia Beach, I turned left out of the Marketcenter apron and drove straight east down Eden Way North past the dark brick storefront of Emerald Creek Station.

Corporate human-resources manuals were written for ordinary flake-outs, not for a verbal autistic twenty-three-year-old who had clocked into Store Four-Twenty-One five minutes early for twenty-two straight months and suddenly vanished off the face of the Earth. If Ray Halstead wrote me up tomorrow morning for checking on an associate's family after one missed evening shift, I would sign the coaching form without blinking.

Three minutes later, I parked beside the curb in front of a quiet, maple-shaded two-story brick-and-siding home with an attached two-car garage, unclipped my royal-blue Blue Tech lanyard so I wouldn't look like somebody serving papers, and climbed the three wooden front porch steps.

Before my knuckles finished the second knock, the porch light clicked on and a tall, curvy seventeen-year-old girl—easily six-foot-one even leaning gracefully on a pair of tall matte-charcoal single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches, with honey-brown wavy hair and sharp green-hazel eyes—pulled the inner door open behind the glass storm screen.

"Hi—sorry to bother you after eight," I said plainly, keeping my hands visible and my voice calm. "Are you Gwen Lennox? My name's Mallory Walsh. I'm Nolan's Computers supervisor four blocks over at Blue Tech."

Every bit of casual evening color vanished from Gwen's face in half a heartbeat.

"Nolan?" Gwen's fingers tightened hard around the storm-door latch as seventy-nine-year-old Walter Lennox rose slowly out of his brown leather recliner inside the family room behind her. "Wait—Nolan worked four to eight tonight. Did something happen to his wheelchair crossing the Parkway?!"

"He never clocked in at four, and he never called," I answered directly, refusing to waste ten seconds on soft corporate hedging. "I tried the trailer landline at Pinecrest Court at six and again at eight—nobody picks up. Has either of you heard from him since Monday?"

"Not a word since Sunday supper," Walter Lennox said, stepping steadily into the foyer in his pressed flannel shirt despite the thin, weathered lines around his mouth from his winter heart hospitalization. Opening the storm door wide so I could step inside, the retired Navy machinist traded one long, grim look with his granddaughter. "That boy hasn't missed a shift or a phone call in his life, Miss Walsh."

"Tammy," Gwen said immediately, jaw setting like iron. "Back in May she threw him out for three weeks until June lot rent came due. Lately she's been picking fights with him every single morning over calling us."

We didn't wait for Friday. At seven-thirty Thursday morning—two hours before Nolan's scheduled nine-to-three-thirty Thursday shift, with Ray Halstead covering my morning department counter after hearing Nolan's family hadn't heard from him either—Gwen rode beside me in my silver compact out to the rutted gravel lanes of Pinecrest Court off Route 13.

Tammy's rusty single-wide sat padlocked behind Mr. Pruitt's sagging plywood ramp, curtains drawn tight. Next door, however, an older retired shipyard pipefitter watering tomato pots in flip-flops took one look at Gwen's charcoal crutches and spilled everything before I finished asking.

"Oh, your brother?" the neighbor called over his chain-link fence. "Watched her march him down that wobbly wood ramp in his wheelchair around eight-forty Tuesday morning with two bags! Bragging loud enough for half the court to hear that she was taking cash down to the Norfolk Pathfinder terminal to buy ninety-nine-dollar walk-up tickets clear out to Seattle to see her kin!"

Gwen stared at me across the hood of my car, eyes wide with horror. "Seattle?! Four days on a bus without calling Pathfinder forty-eight hours ahead for a wheelchair lift?!"

Back around Walter's kitchen table twenty minutes later with Pathfinder's printed coast-to-coast timetable spread open beside my spiral notebook, my pattern-matching brain locked onto the math fast. A Tuesday late-morning departure out of Norfolk ran Richmond, Marion, Knoxville, Nashville, and St. Louis by Wednesday evening, crossed Kansas into Denver and Salt Lake City Thursday into Friday, and pulled into the Pasco Intermodal Terminal in eastern Washington early Saturday morning for a scheduled rest stop before crossing the mountains into Seattle.

"If we wait until he reaches Seattle on Saturday afternoon, Tammy vanishes with him into a city of half a million people where we don't know a single street address," Gwen said fiercely, pointing her index finger straight at *PASCO, WA — ARR 07:40 AM SAT*. "Pasco's one single intermodal building! If we fly out tomorrow afternoon, we beat that bus right to the Pasco platform!"

Across the laminate table, Walter Lennox reached into his wallet without a second's hesitation and slid his blue-and-gold Tidewater Maritime Credit Union Visa card squarely between Gwen's notebook and mine.

"Book two round-trip seats into Pasco on Meridian Airlines for Friday afternoon—plus a third return seat coming home Saturday with wheelchair bulkhead assistance for Nolan," Walter ordered quietly, his gray Navy eyes flint-steady despite the tremor in his thin knuckles. "I'll sign a notarized guardian travel letter at the credit union branch before noon so Gwen clears the gate with her high-school ID, and I'm holding down this house until you bring my grandson through that front door."

Only one gut-punch hit us before we drove to Norfolk International Airport on Friday afternoon.

At quarter past three on Friday—right after Nolan's two-thirty Friday shift window ticked into his third consecutive unexcused absence on Week A—I stopped by Store Four-Twenty-One's back office while Ray Halstead hung up a long, tense call with Blue Tech District Human Resources.

Ray set the receiver down slowly and shook his head with genuine regret in his tired eyes.

"I tried everything I had, Mal," fifty-two-year-old Ray told me quietly, sliding both a signed letter on crisp Blue Tech letterhead and a business card with his personal home and cell numbers handwritten on the back across the desk into my folder. Because Ray's five-year-old granddaughter was autistic, Ray understood how both my brain and Nolan's worked better than any manager in our district. "I know for a fact Nolan didn't walk off his job—when his girlfriend cornered him in that bus station Tuesday morning, a literal guy like Nolan never connected the dots that stepping onto that Pathfinder bus meant getting fired here. He figured he'd just call us from the first rest stop, not knowing she'd block him from every payphone. I explained every bit of that to District HR, plus twenty-two months of spotless attendance. Corporate's automated PeopleSoft attendance engine up at headquarters still won't budge—three consecutive missed shifts without a pre-approved medical leave packet triggers automatic involuntary termination at eight o'clock tonight with a corporate rehire freeze. Store managers can't overrule Corporate's computer."

My chest squeezed hard, knowing Nolan's rule-loving heart better than anyone in that building. Losing Store Four-Twenty-One wasn't going to feel like losing a seven-dollar-an-hour register badge to Nolan; it was going to feel like the floor of his whole orderly world giving way beneath his feet.

"Tell Nolan not to list corporate's eight-hundred verification line on a single job application—tell him to put down my personal number right there on that card," Ray added firmly. "Corporate can lock me out of putting him back on our payroll; they can't stop me from picking up my own phone off the clock and giving him a glowing professional reference to anyone in Hampton Roads who calls. Now go get him home safe."

### Part 2: Bitter Wind in Colby, US-287, and Breaking Skin in Salt Lake

**Date:** 2001-08-24 at 02:40 America/Chicago

![At 2:40 AM outside a lonely, windswept highway travel plaza in Colby, Kansas under green-lit diesel canopies and pitch-black prairie sky, weary twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox (5-foot-7, olive jacket over navy tee and jeans) leans quietly on his two matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches beside a diner walkway pillar looking across at petite five-foot-tall Tammy sitting hunched and regretful on the concrete curb while their unbranded silver-and-blue highway coach refuels in the background.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/seattle_p001_colby_kansas_truckstop.jpg)

#### 2:40 AM — Facing East Outside the Colby Travel Plaza

At twenty minutes to three on Friday morning outside a desolate, wind-scoured highway travel plaza in Colby, Kansas, nothing existed in any direction except flickering green fluorescent diesel canopies, flat black prairie stretching into infinity, and the hot, throbbing ache radiating upward from my compressed tailbone.

After rolling through Kansas City and Topeka in a blur of stale sweat and diesel fumes—while my wristwatch ticked silently past my missed Thursday morning shift back home in Chesapeake without me knowing Mallory Walsh and Gwen were already poring over Pathfinder timetables around Grandpa Walter's kitchen table—our driver had ordered everyone off the coach for fifteen minutes to fuel the tanks. Leaning heavily on both matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches beside a concrete walkway pillar out of the relentless prairie wind, I watched Tammy walk slowly away from the boarding line toward the outer edge of the gravel lot.

She stopped alone beside the low concrete curb, facing straight east—back down dark Interstate 70 toward Missouri, Kentucky, Tennessee, and Virginia.

Under the harsh, humming amber glare of the yard floodlight, Tammy didn't know anyone was watching her. Her straight black hair hung limp and unbrushed around her round face, a tender bruise under her parting from my falling steel wheelchair legrests outside St. Louis Municipal Airport still made her wince whenever the wind caught her hood, her chunky white platform sneakers were coated gray with rest-stop dust, and her cash envelope was down to thin twenties. Watching eighteen-wheelers thunder eastbound toward home across the flat Kansas dark, her shoulders slumped two full inches inside her pastel-blue fleece cardigan.

For ten fleeting, unguarded seconds out on the Great Plains, pure exhaustion stripped every ounce of bravado clean off Tammy's face. I could see plain as daylight that she was having real second thoughts—that the four-day cramped reality of what she had impulsively started in Norfolk was sinking in, and part of her wished desperately she were back inside her Chesapeake trailer right now.

Hope flickered tiny and fragile behind my ribs. Adjusting my grip on my silver crutch handles, I swung quietly halfway across the cracked concrete toward her.

"Tammy," I rasped softly over the prairie wind, testing that single crack of mercy. "There's an eastbound schedule board right inside the diner doors. If we use what's left of your cash before the driver finishes fueling—"

Hearing my voice say it out loud snapped Tammy's pride shut harder than cold iron.

Her spine stiffened straight up, every trace of doubt vanished behind a hard, flat scowl, and she turned her back squarely on the eastbound highway without answering my question.

"Shut up and get back in line, Nolan," Tammy bit out, marching straight past my crutches toward the open aluminum coach stairwell. "Nobody's turning around."

***

#### 1:15 PM — Thin Air in Denver, US-287, and Midnight Fire in Salt Lake

By midday Friday inside the glaring concrete bays of Denver, Colorado—one full mile above the sea-level Tidewater air my lungs had breathed my whole life—every climb up and down those four steep coach steps left my chest tight and dizzy.

My stomach cramped hollow from twenty hours on half a pack of vending crackers and lukewarm water, and a deeper, sharper heat had begun whispering underneath my left hip every time our coach bounced over expansion joints. Yet whenever I glanced sideways at Tammy's tight jaw and angry sighs during our rushed Denver transfer, I bit the inside of my cheek and kept quiet, terrified that asking for food or twenty extra minutes near a clinic would trigger another loud explosion in front of strangers.

An hour north up Interstate 25 past Fort Collins, our relief driver's voice crackled over the overhead PA speaker:

<pa>Folks, for those asking why we didn't head straight west out of Denver on Seventy—our forty-five-foot commercial coaches skip the steep grades up the Continental Divide Tunnel and run northwest up two-lane Highway Two-Eighty-Seven into Laramie to pick up Eighty across Wyoming.</pa>

Cutting diagonally across the high-altitude wind tunnels of northern Colorado and southern Wyoming may have spared the transmission steep mountain grades, yet five straight hours of rough two-lane ranchland frost-heaves along US Highway 287 hammered directly upward through the unpadded commercial bus vinyl into my unshifted sitting bones.

By the time our coach limped down out of the Wasatch mountains for a midnight bus change inside the Salt Lake City terminal—more than eighty hours into constant motion, and four hours after my third missed Week A shift at Blue Tech had quietly triggered corporate's automated eight-o'clock termination computer back in Virginia—my body hit a terrifying physical wall.

Under federal accessibility rules, the Salt Lake driver would gladly have held the Boise coach five extra minutes while a passenger on forearm crutches used the terminal restroom, since the narrow lavatory wedged at the rear of the highway cruiser couldn't fit crutches through its folding door. Tammy didn't even let me ask the driver—snapping under the fluorescent sign that the coach was pulling out any second and I was going to get us stranded in Utah if I took too long.

Bracing one trembling forearm against the cold tile stall wall inside the Salt Lake men's room to check why my left hip felt like an open stove burner instead of dull numbness, my breath caught sharp in my throat.

Sitting wedged upright for more than three days without my contoured foam wheelchair cushion underneath me—unable to do proper thirty-minute weight-shift push-ups jammed beside Tammy in a moving aisle—had broken down the skin over my left sitting bone into an angry, hot, deep-crimson stage-two pressure sore.

Panic turned my palms ice-cold. Every wheelchair user on Earth knew an untreated pressure ulcer could turn infected fast and put you flat in a surgical ward for months. Yet when I crutched back out toward Gate Nine pale and shaking to show Tammy my hands were trembling and tell her my skin was tearing open, she rolled her eyes with a heavy, disgusted sigh, grabbed my jacket wrist, and pulled me straight up the steps of the overnight Idaho and Washington coach before the pneumatic door hissed shut—neither of us having the faintest idea that two Meridian Airlines boarding passes bearing Gwen's name and Mallory Walsh's name were already descending toward eastern Washington ahead of us.

### Part 3: Five Minutes Before the Restroom Door and Crying Into Four Safe Arms in Pasco

**Date:** 2001-08-25 at 07:42 America/Los_Angeles

![On a sunlit wooden concourse bench inside the Pasco Intermodal Terminal in Washington on Saturday morning, August 25, 2001, exhausted twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox cries openly in heartbreak over losing his Blue Tech job and relief at being rescued as tall seventeen-year-old sister Gwen Lennox (6-foot-1, burgundy hoodie) wraps both arms protectively around him from his right and Mallory Walsh (5-foot-5, copper-auburn ponytail, silver wire-rimmed glasses, forest-green cardigan) holds him warmly from his left with Ray Halstead's folded reference letter in hand, beside Nolan's rescued manual wheelchair and all four single-tube matte-silver and matte-charcoal Lofstrand forearm crutches.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/pasco_p002_four_safe_arms_pasco_bench.jpg)

#### 7:42 AM — Five Minutes Before the Restroom Door

Bleary, harsh Pacific Northwest morning sunlight slanted through the dusty high windows of the Pasco Intermodal Terminal in eastern Washington when our coach groaned into Bay Two after a sleepless overnight haul across Boise, Nampa, and the empty high desert.

<pa>Pasco station—twenty-minute rest stop before Yakima, Ellensburg, and Seattle!</pa>

Ninety hours without real sleep, running a low fever from the burning stage-two pressure sore under my jeans, my wrists swollen raw inside my silver Lofstrand cuffs, and my brain swimming through thick gray cotton fog, I barely managed to lower myself down the four aluminum coach steps onto the concrete platform and shuffle inside the quiet tiled concourse.

Tammy walked right on my heels, her eyes dark, glassy, and wired tight after four days of bruised pride, dwindling cash, and watching me flinch every time her shoulder brushed mine. Spotting the quiet, isolated rear hallway leading back toward the terminal restrooms away from the ticket desk and the sparse Saturday-morning benches, Tammy abruptly stepped right in front of my crutches—dropping her voice into that eerie, syrupy Week-One velvet whisper that didn't match the hard grip of her fingers closing around my jacket sleeve.

"Come back here toward the restrooms with me for five minutes before everybody gets off the bus, baby," Tammy murmured, tugging steadily on my elbow while my exhausted, pain-fogged head struggled even to process whole sentences. "You've barely touched me this whole trip, Nolan, and you know how much I've missed being close to you like our first week—"

"Tammy, wait, I hurt all over, I can't—" I mumbled dazedly, my rubber crutch tips dragging half a step across the waxed tile toward that empty restroom corridor because I didn't have the balance or strength left to pull my arm free.

Thirty feet behind us, the heavy automatic glass entrance doors from the curbside taxi drop-off burst wide open.

"NOLAN!" a clear, fierce, unmistakable voice rang straight across the Pasco concourse like a brass bell cutting through fog. "Get your hands off my brother right now!"

Every nerve left in my body jolted wide awake.

Clicking swiftly across the terrazzo on two tall matte-charcoal Lofstrand forearm crutches—six-foot-one in a wrinkled travel hoodie and jeans, flanked shoulder-to-shoulder by a five-foot-five young woman with a copper-auburn ponytail, thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, and a forest-green zip cardigan—was my seventeen-year-old sister Gwen, with Mallory Walsh striding right beside her and a uniformed Pasco transit security officer three paces off their heels.

Tammy froze dead in her chunky platform sneakers outside the restroom hallway, her jaw dropping open as if two ghosts had materialized out of the Washington sagebrush. "What the—how the hell did *you* get out here?!"

"Airplane, Tammy—invented in nineteen-oh-three, and it comes with wheelchair assistance," Gwen snapped coldly, planting herself like a six-foot-one lighthouse squarely between Tammy and my crutches while the transit security officer stepped up with one hand resting calmly on his radio mic.

"Everything alright here, folks?" the officer asked, looking straight from my pale, shaking face to Tammy's hand dropping fast off my sleeve. "These two ladies just showed ticketed ID at the desk looking for Mr. Nolan Lennox—he's free to walk right out that front door with his family."

Outnumbered three to one under a security guard's stare—and spotting the Pathfinder driver already waving Seattle passengers back toward Bay Two—Tammy didn't dare swing or scream. Face burning blotchy purple with humiliation, she spat, "Fine—go crawl home to your sister, you useless baby!" spun on her heel, and marched straight back out the bay doors onto the departing Seattle coach alone while the baggage clerk pulled my folded manual wheelchair, two steel legrests, and battered olive duffel out of the underbelly bay onto the platform for Mallory.

***

#### 7:50 AM — The Greeting My Bones Needed, My Lost Shirt, and One Massively Earned Hug

Five seconds after the glass bay doors clicked shut behind Tammy, Gwen didn't waste half a breath on luggage or paperwork.

Propping both of her tall matte-charcoal Lofstrand forearm crutches cleanly against the arm of the nearest smooth wooden concourse bench, my six-foot-one little sister opened both arms wide right in front of me—and before my trembling knees could buckle inside my jeans, I let go of my own silver crutches against the bench rail and stepped straight into the tightest, most aching greeting hug of my entire life.

Ever since we were little kids, Gwen and I had greeted each other and said goodbye the exact same way every single time without exception, and Tammy dragging me down that plywood ramp on Tuesday morning had stolen four whole days without it. In two thousand and one I didn't have a single clinical phrase or fancy vocabulary word to explain why my autistic wiring needed to hug Gwen tightly every hello and every goodbye—only that tucking my chin into its familiar spot right beneath her collarbone while her long, steady arms locked solid around my shoulders flipped an actual physical switch inside my nervous system, turning down ninety hours of roaring highway static until my heart remembered how to beat in rhythm. Holding onto her wrinkled burgundy travel hoodie like a sailor clinging to a lighthouse, I breathed in airplane soap and home, and Gwen held me back with everything she had, resting her cheek fiercely atop my tousled hair as we eased down together onto the wooden bench so my weight stayed off my sore left hip.

Right as Gwen shifted half an inch to keep one arm anchored warm around my back and I looked straight across into Mallory Walsh's gentle blue-gray eyes behind her thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, the one terrifying math calculation that had eaten me alive since Nashville burst straight out of my raw throat.

"Mallory—my shifts!" I choked out, grabbing the cuff of Mallory's green cardigan with both trembling hands as hot panic flooded my eyes. "Wednesday four-to-eight, and Thursday morning, and yesterday afternoon—Tammy blocked the payphones in Richmond, Nashville, and St. Louis, she wouldn't let me call you or Mr. Halstead! Did corporate fire me?! Please, Mallory—is there *any* way I can get my job back?!"

Kneeling right down on both knees on the terrazzo in front of my bench so her honest eyes met mine on the level without a trace of flinching or false promises, Mallory covered both of my shaking hands with hers.

"Nolan, listen to me—Ray Halstead and I both called District HR on your behalf and told them you hadn't abandoned your post," Mallory said softly, her voice catching slightly with genuine sorrow on my behalf. "Corporate's automated payroll computer up at headquarters still processed Friday evening's third unexcused absence as an automatic termination with a corporate rehire freeze. Neither Ray nor I had override permissions in the system to stop it. I am so, so sorry, Nolan."

Hearing those literal words out loud hit my chest ten times harder than Tammy's palm ever could.

Two whole years of counting out clean change, lining every seventy-five-ohm splitter flush on its peg, memorizing every four-week rotation, never once showing up late, saving every paycheck to keep Gwen safe when Grandpa died—gone. Stolen clean away from me because I froze behind steel lockers in Norfolk.

A jagged, strangled sound tore out of my chest, and right there on a wooden bench in eastern Washington, I broke down crying harder than I had cried since Mom and Dad died.

Gwen pulled me immediately against her right shoulder while right on my left, without a shred of workplace distance left between us, Mallory sat straight up onto the bench beside me and wrapped both of her warm arms around my neck and shoulder too. Held safe inside four fierce arms, I sobbed uncontrollably for five straight minutes—crying for my lost royal-blue polo, crying for ninety hours of terror and aching skin, and crying at the sheer miracle that the two best women on Earth had flown clear across America just to pull me out of a hallway in time.

"You didn't fail a single thing, Nolan Lennox—not one," Mallory whispered fiercely against my hair once my ragged breathing finally slowed, pressing both a signed sheet of crisp Blue Tech letterhead and a white business card into my palm. "Ray Halstead has an autistic granddaughter, Nolan—he understood right away that Tammy cornered you in Norfolk and you never connected stepping onto that bus with losing your job because you thought she'd let you use a rest-stop payphone. Corporate wouldn't let him overrule the computer, so Ray wrote his own personal phone number right on the back of this card and told me anyone you interview with can call him directly for a glowing reference. And losing Store Four-Twenty-One yesterday evening also means there isn't a Shift Supervisor pin standing between you and me anymore."

Wiping my wet cheeks on my sleeve, my literal brain stared from that white business card up to Mallory's freckled, tear-bright face six inches from mine.

For five straight months back in Chesapeake, Mallory had saved me the quiet corner chair in the breakroom, listened to me talk about protected-memory kernels, told me the bug wasn't in my hardware when Tammy screamed at me, and honored every single boundary in the book—and then the very night I vanished off the Week A schedule, she had broken corporate HR protocol, driven to Grandpa's porch, and flown three thousand miles across the country beside my teenage sister to save me.

"Mallory," I whispered hoarsely, looking straight into her eyes, "can I hug you right now—just you and me?"

Mallory's breath caught in the warmest, brightest smile I had ever seen behind her wire-rimmed glasses.

"More than anything, Nolan," she whispered, opening both arms wide.

Turning squarely toward her on the wooden bench while Gwen happily eased her arm back to give us room, I wrapped both of my arms all the way around Mallory Walsh's waist and shoulders, and Mallory locked both of her arms tightly around my neck—burying her face against my olive jacket shoulder as I held her close against my chest in a long, deep, massively earned hug that felt five months and three thousand miles in the making. Right beside my shoulder on the bench, seventeen-year-old Gwen leaned back against her hands beaming from ear to ear with unmistakable, wholehearted sisterly approval.

![On the sunlit wooden concourse bench of the Pasco Intermodal Terminal beside Nolan's rescued manual wheelchair and olive duffel bag, twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox and Mallory Walsh share a deeply emotional, massively earned two-armed hug while tall seventeen-year-old sister Gwen Lennox sits right beside them beaming with proud, wholehearted sisterly approval, with Nolan's shorter matte-silver and Gwen's taller matte-charcoal single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches leaning neatly against the bench arm]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/pasco_p003_mallory_earned_hug_gwen_approving.jpg#fullwidth)

"Come on, you two," Gwen smiled warmly when Mallory and I finally eased half an inch apart with Mallory's fingers still laced tight through mine. "We've got three bulkhead seats waiting over at Pasco Regional Airport, and Grandpa Walter's holding down the fort waiting for us to bring my brother home."

### Part 4: Empty Work Polos, Ray's Personal Number, and Home in Studio 205

**Date:** 2001-12-01 at 15:30 America/New_York

![On the quiet second-floor terrazzo stairwell landing outside white apartment door 205 at Harborview Towers in Norfolk in December 2001, twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox (5-foot-7, heather-navy Henley and jeans) stands beaming on his two matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches while off-duty Mallory Walsh (5-foot-5, copper-auburn ponytail, thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, forest-green zip cardigan over cream tee, canvas tote) rests one warm hand gently over his wrist and smiles up into his eyes.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/pasco_p003_door_205_landing_confession.jpg)

#### 3:30 PM — Autumn Without Blue Tech and Ray Halstead's Personal Line

Flying home to Norfolk out of Pasco Regional Airport that August Saturday evening—after an urgent-care doctor near the airport cleaned and dressed my early stage-two pressure sore before infection could ever take hold—brought me back under Grandpa Walter's roof off Eden Way North into an autumn where my whole daily routine had to be rebuilt from scratch.

That Monday morning, August twenty-seventh, while Danny from Blue Tech's warehouse and a Chesapeake sheriff's deputy stood ten minutes of civil standby outside Pinecrest Court to haul my full-sized bed, oak dresser, nineteen-inch television, silver VCR and DVD combo, and laptop safely under a tarp in Grandpa Walter's garage, I filed my ninety-day written lot non-renewal notice so my name would drop off Mr. Pruitt's lease automatically at midnight on November thirtieth.

Still, for the first three weeks back at Grandpa's house, not having Store Four-Twenty-One four blocks down the street ached every single morning. Out of twenty-two months of muscle memory, I kept waking up on Grandpa's downstairs pull-out sofa bed—where the doctor had ordered me to stay off my left sitting bone while my skin finished closing—and reaching automatically toward the closet for a royal-blue work polo I wasn't allowed to wear anymore, stretching my final Blue Tech vacation-payout check to cover my share of the trailer notice.

Yet being home at Grandpa's house off the clock carried three quiet mercies I couldn't see in August.

First, on Tuesday morning, September eleventh, instead of standing alone surrounded by fifty store televisions when the world fractured on CSN, I was right there in Grandpa's family room when Grandpa drove straight over to Emerald Creek High School to sign frightened seventeen-year-old Gwen out after second-block Latin III—letting all three of us hold tight to one another under Grandpa's dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket all afternoon.

Second, on Friday evening, October nineteenth, Grandpa Walter kept his promise to see Gwen blow out all eighteen candles on her homemade chocolate cake—where Gwen threw both arms around my neck shouting *A million times yes!* when I asked if she would come live with me once my trailer lease cleared—before Grandpa slipped peacefully away in his sleep at six o'clock the next morning. Without retail shifts pulling me away through late October, Gwen and I tackled Chesapeake Circuit Court probate, ten raised-seal death certificates, and selling our childhood two-story house to pay off Grandpa's winter medical home-equity loan one calm checklist line at a time.

Third—and biggest of all—when Gwen spotted a staff posting in early November for a twenty-eight-hour-a-week seated Part-Time Campus Computer Lab and Helpdesk Technician downtown at the Community College of Hampton Roads Norfolk Campus inside the Martin Building, I wrote Ray Halstead's personal phone number right into the supervisor reference box just like he had promised.

True to every word, when CCHR's campus IT director dialed Ray's home line after I fixed a broken TCP/IP gateway and hung print spooler in four minutes flat during my practical interview, fifty-two-year-old Ray spent fifteen full minutes singing my praises—explaining plainly with a grandfather's understanding of autism how I had been tricked onto an out-of-state bus after two spotless years without connecting stepping aboard to losing my job, and vouching that I was the finest Quantum OS 2000 diagnostic technician in Chesapeake.

CCHR hired me that same afternoon at two dollars more an hour than Blue Tech had ever paid—meaning I started my seated campus workbench job in mid-November and never once had to push through winter sleet across Emerald Creek Parkway again.

Those state-college paystubs easily cleared Studio 205 on the second floor of Harborview Towers—a twenty-story nineteen-sixty-five brick-and-concrete high-rise overlooking The Hague inlet off West Brambleton Avenue in Norfolk, six blocks from Ghent High School where eighteen-year-old Gwen transferred to finish eleventh grade, and one six-minute ride down CVTA Route 2 or Route 11 from my CCHR lab bench.

When Mallory Walsh drove up to Norfolk off the clock on Saturday afternoon, December first, to help us carry our rolling microwave-cart pantry inside tiny eleven-by-eighteen-foot Studio 205—taking one look at our single corner full-sized bed draped in Grandpa Walter's plaid blanket and surprising both Gwen and me by saying without a trace of weirdness that brother and sister sharing the one bed that fit so Gwen wasn't out on the street was smart engineering—she walked back out with me onto the second-floor stairwell landing where zero supervisor pin had stood between us since August twenty-fourth.

Adjusting her thin silver wire-rimmed glasses with warm pink dusting her freckles, Mallory told me in crystal-clear syntax that she had been quietly in love with me since Store Four-Twenty-One, and asked me out on our first official Friday-night date up in Ghent—giving us ten peaceful, happy months together before a rainy October Friday night at nine-fifteen when Regional Mobility Supply put Gwen and me on hold for forty-seven minutes.

***

# Story Path: Respect Workplace Boundaries & Wait Until Friday's Third No-Show {#crossroads-path-seattle-full-exodus-rescue}

*Honor corporate protocol and relationship guardrails until Friday evening's automatic third-strike termination—leaving feverish Nolan to endure the full haul to Seattle, Puget Sound General Hospital, grounded post-9/11 skies, and missing Gwen's eighteenth birthday while Grandpa Walter refuses to die until Gwen and Mallory bring Nolan home.*

*[← Return to the Story Crossroads](#crossroads-blue-tech-missed-shift-alarm-2001)*

# Book One: Ninety-Six Hours to Puget Sound

## Chapter 1B: Pasco, Puget Sound General, and Grandpa Walter's Promise

### Part 1: Bitter Wind in Colby, US-287, and Breaking Skin in Salt Lake

**Date:** 2001-08-24 at 02:40 America/Chicago

![At 2:40 AM outside a lonely, windswept highway travel plaza in Colby, Kansas under green-lit diesel canopies and pitch-black prairie sky, weary twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox (5-foot-7, olive jacket over navy tee and jeans) leans quietly on his two matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches beside a diner walkway pillar looking across at petite five-foot-tall Tammy sitting hunched and regretful on the concrete curb while their unbranded silver-and-blue highway coach refuels in the background.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/seattle_p001_colby_kansas_truckstop.jpg)

#### 2:40 AM — Facing East Outside the Colby Travel Plaza

Back in Virginia on Wednesday night, Mallory Walsh had slipped her car key into her ignition, respected corporate human-resources protocol and the boundary around a spoken-for employee, and driven home to wait properly until Friday evening's third unexcused absence before telephoning Grandpa Walter's house off Eden Way North—meaning nobody in Chesapeake even knew Tammy had taken me out of Tidewater while our fourth Pathfinder cruiser pushed deep into the Great Plains.

Out under the flickering green diesel canopy of a windswept highway travel plaza in Colby, Kansas at twenty minutes to three on Friday morning, nothing existed in any direction except flat black prairie, cold grease traps, and the hot, grinding pain radiating up from the base of my spine.

Leaning heavily on both matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches beside a concrete diner pillar while our tanks fueled after Kansas City and Topeka, I watched Tammy walk slowly away from the bus line toward the far edge of the asphalt lot.

She stopped alone beside the low concrete curb, facing straight east—back down dark Interstate 70 toward Missouri, Tennessee, and Virginia.

Under the harsh sodium floodlight, Tammy didn't realize anyone was looking at her. Her straight black hair hung limp and unbrushed over the tender swelling left by my falling steel wheelchair legrests outside St. Louis Municipal Airport, her chunky white platform sneakers were gray with road grit, and as a pair of eastbound eighteen-wheelers thundered past toward home, her posture slumped inside her pastel-blue fleece cardigan.

For ten silent, unguarded seconds in the Kansas wind, sheer exhaustion stripped the mask clean off Tammy's eyes: even Tammy was having second thoughts, realizing the magnitude of this ninety-six-hour nightmare and wishing she were back inside her Chesapeake trailer.

Adjusting my grip on my silver crutch handles, I swung quietly halfway across the concrete toward her. "Tammy," I rasped softly, testing that single crack of doubt. "There's an eastbound schedule board right inside the diner. If we turn around here with what's left of your cash—"

Hearing me say it out loud snapped her pride shut like a steel trap. Straightening her shoulders, Tammy turned her back squarely on the East without another glance at the interstate.

"Shut up and get back in line, Nolan," she snapped, marching straight back toward the aluminum stairwell. "Nobody's turning around."

***

#### 1:20 PM — Thin Air in Denver, US-287, and Midnight Fire in Salt Lake

Midday in Denver brought thin, dizzying mile-high air that left my chest tight every time I hauled myself up and down four steep coach steps on swollen wrists, while hunger twisted my empty stomach and a sharp, secret fire began burning steadily underneath my left sitting bone. Afraid that asking for food or medical help would only set Tammy screaming in another crowded station, I bit my tongue and endured the transfer in silence.

North of Fort Collins up Interstate 25, our driver announced over the PA mic that forty-five-foot commercial Pathfinder coaches bypassed steep Interstate 70 and the high-altitude grades of the Continental Divide Tunnel—cutting northwest up two-lane US Highway 287 across the high windswept basin into Wyoming to rejoin Interstate 80 at Laramie. Five straight hours of rough two-lane ranchland vibration hammered straight through the unpadded vinyl bench into my hips.

By midnight inside the Salt Lake City terminal restroom—more than eighty hours wedged upright without my contoured foam wheelchair cushion or room to shift my weight, while Tammy snapped outside the door that the driver wouldn't wait—my trembling fingers brushed hot, swollen, broken skin over my left sitting bone. A severe pressure ulcer had already torn open beneath my jeans, spiking a low, shivering fever behind my eyes. When I crutched out to Gate Nine pale and begging for an emergency room, Tammy answered with a disgusted roll of her eyes, grabbed my wrist, and pulled me straight onto the overnight Boise and Washington coach.

### Part 2: Fog in Pasco, Across the Cascades, and Abandoned in Seattle

**Date:** 2001-08-25 at 07:45 America/Los_Angeles

![Inside the rainy concourse of the Downtown Seattle Pathfinder Terminal on Saturday afternoon, August 25, 2001, weary, heartbroken twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox stands leaning on his two matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches beside a concrete column and unbranded chrome payphones next to his folded manual wheelchair, two detached metal footrests, and olive duffel bag on the terrazzo floor, while petite five-foot-tall Tammy strides coldly out the rain-streaked glass exit doors pulling her rolling suitcase without looking back.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/seattle_p002_abandoned_seattle_terminal_rain.jpg)

#### 7:45 AM — Fog Behind a Locked Restroom Door in Pasco

By quarter to eight Saturday morning at the Pasco Intermodal Terminal in eastern Washington—after grinding across the Idaho line through Boise, Nampa, and the bleak predawn high desert with nobody from Virginia standing on the platform to stop us—ninety hours without real sleep and a climbing infection fever from my broken sitting-bone skin had turned the tiled concourse into swimming, cotton-stuffed tunnel vision where I could barely string four words together.

Walking right beside my dragging silver forearm crutches toward the quiet rear restroom corridor while the other passengers queued for coffee up front, Tammy didn't yell or shove me at all. Desperate after four miserable days to prove to herself that we were back together and have sex again before reaching Seattle, she slipped her arm softly through mine and dropped into the exact warm, sugary Week-One voice that my exhausted, literal brain had been conditioned for two years to obey whenever my defenses were gone.

"Come inside the single family restroom with me for just a few minutes, baby," Tammy whispered softly against my ear, guiding my trembling elbow toward the heavy steel door while my fever-fogged head struggled even to stay upright on my cuffs. "I haven't gotten to hold you all week, Nolan, and you know how much I've missed being in bed with you... just come inside and let me be close to you like home."

"Tammy, my head's spinning, my hip hurts so bad, I don't think—" I mumbled weakly, unable to think straight or process how out of it I really was as she clicked the heavy privacy deadbolt shut behind us under the buzzing fluorescent tube and reached gently for the hem of my shirt, whispering over and over how much she had missed me until my dazed, exhausted mind gave up trying to sort pain from compliance—wishing only to turn invisible against the cold white tile while the ceiling spun away into gray static.

Only the harsh crackle of the terminal loudspeaker minutes later calling final boarding for Yakima, Ellensburg, and Seattle snapped Tammy abruptly back to the clock, making her straighten her pastel-blue cardigan in a rush and hurry me back up the coach steps two minutes before the bay doors hissed shut.

***

#### 1:10 PM — Ellensburg, Cold Rain in Seattle, and Left Behind at the End of the Line

All five hours northwest through Yakima, windswept Ellensburg, and the dark timbered grades of Snoqualmie Pass, a cold, silent shift burned behind my feverish eyes. Looking sideways at Tammy staring smugly out the window while hot infection throbbed down my left leg with every mile-post, the last thread of confusion in my chest finally burned away: this trip had never been about love, family, or starting fresh. I was just a prop she had dragged across nine states so she wouldn't have to ride alone.

Early afternoon rain was streaking down out of low gray Puget Sound clouds when our coach finally groaned to a halt inside the cavernous, exhaust-choked concrete bays of the Downtown Seattle Pathfinder Terminal.

By then, my teeth were chattering uncontrollably despite sweat soaking clean through my heather-navy t-shirt from a hundred-and-two-degree fever. Dark heat radiated all the way down my left thigh whenever I tried to touch weight to vinyl, and when the baggage clerk slid my folded manual wheelchair onto the greasy concrete without its crushed cushion, my legs shook too violently even to let go of my silver Lofstrand grips without help.

Tammy walked twenty feet away to a row of chrome wall payphones, dropped coins into the slot, and put on a bright, syrupy fake-surprise voice for all of ten seconds—before her cousin on the other end of the line realized Tammy had shown up unannounced in Washington expecting to move an uninvited boyfriend with a wheelchair and an infected wound into a fifth-floor walk-up apartment.

For ninety seconds Tammy argued sharper and louder into the receiver, then slammed the handset down hard enough to crack plastic.

Spinning back toward me where I leaned white-faced and shivering against a concrete bay pillar three thousand miles from Grandpa Walter and Gwen, Tammy didn't even bother putting the mask back on.

"I didn't spend four days getting hit in the head in St. Louis just to play nursemaid in an emergency room!" Tammy snapped coldly, grabbing the telescoping handle of her rolling suitcase and leaving my folded wheelchair, battered duffel, and two loose steel legrests lying abandoned beside my sneakers on the wet concrete. "Call an ambulance on your own dime—we're through!"

Before I could even lift a trembling hand off my crutch grip to answer, Tammy strode straight out the street exit into the Seattle rain and stepped aboard a departing TAPS city bus, vanishing into downtown traffic without looking back once.

### Part 3: Puget Sound General, Grounded Skies, and Holding On Past October Nineteenth

**Date:** 2001-09-11 at 06:03 America/Los_Angeles

#### 6:03 AM — Hospital Television Static and Three Time Zones From Home

At three minutes past six in the morning Pacific Time—nine-oh-three back home in Chesapeake—the muted nineteen-inch television bolted high on the plaster wall of my sixth-floor semiprivate room at Puget Sound General Hospital cut my morning nurse off mid-sentence.

Lying flat on my right side atop a humming alternating-pressure air mattress with an intravenous antibiotic line taped cleanly into the back of my left wrist and both of my matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches standing upright beside the steel closet, I stared up at the glowing glass as dark smoke billowed into a cloudless New York sky three thousand miles east—and felt colder, smaller, and farther from my family than I had ever felt in my twenty-three years on Earth.

Seattle paramedics had pulled me off that Pathfinder bus bay bench seventeen days earlier running a hundred-and-two-point-eight fever with cellulitis spreading dangerously around a deep stage-three sitting-bone pressure ulcer caused by ninety-six hours wedged upright on rigid commercial bus vinyl without a wheelchair pad. Within four hours of admission, wound-care surgeons had debrided the infection and ordered strict zero-sitting bedrest on air-flotation cushions; within twenty-four hours, a soft-spoken hospital clinical social worker named Ms. Rivera had helped me dial Grandpa Walter's kitchen landline off Eden Way North collect so Gwen's terrified sobs of relief could ring right against my ear.

During her second visit three days later—when I stared miserably at my hospital blanket and muttered in literal confusion that I felt sick to my stomach about letting Tammy talk me into that Pasco terminal restroom when my fever was too high to think straight—Ms. Rivera had pulled her rolling stool to eye level and handed me a plain, gentle truth my autistic mind badly needed to hear in black and white. Under Washington law—Revised Code of Washington Section 9A.44.050—when a person is feverish, injured, and cognitively disoriented after four days without sleep, talking them into sexual contact while they lack the mental capacity to consent isn't romance and is never the patient's fault; legally and morally, that responsibility belonged one hundred percent to Tammy, whether I ever chose to speak to police or simply wanted peace. Hearing a licensed professional state that rule plainly lifted a suffocating ton of confused self-blame clean off my chest.

What nothing in Puget Sound General could speed up was human tissue healing—or an empty American sky.

Watching live CSN bulletins cascade across my hospital television over the next two hours—first New York, then Northern Virginia at the Pentagon less than two hundred miles north of Chesapeake, then federal air-traffic control ordering every civilian airliner across the United States onto the ground at once while garbled early wire reports flashed rumors about a car bomb at the State Department and unaccounted-for flights—my heart hammered hard enough to set off the bedside pulse-oximeter alarm twice. Every long-distance circuit into Tidewater barked back a rapid fast-busy tone until four o'clock that afternoon, when Gwen finally got through from Grandpa's kitchen phone to swear through tears that she and Grandpa Walter were safe under his green-and-navy plaid wool blanket at home.

Even after commercial aviation slowly reopened under armed National Guard checkpoints later that month, my wound surgeon shook his head firmly at every discharge request: sitting upright in an economy or bulkhead airline seat for six cross-country hours before fresh granulation tissue closed my left sitting bone would rip the wound straight back open to the bone.

***

#### 3:30 PM, Friday, October 19 — Eighteen Candles Over a Corded Hospital Phone

Five and a half weeks after the skies went quiet, grey October Puget Sound drizzle streaked my sixth-floor hospital window while I cradled the heavy beige bedside telephone receiver against my ear with both hands.

Three thousand miles east in Chesapeake, it was six-thirty in the evening on Friday, October nineteenth.

Gwen's eighteenth birthday.

For seventeen straight years—since the day Grandpa Walter brought me to visit Mom and Dad's hospital room on silver toddler crutches to meet my tiny newborn baby sister—I had never missed a single one of Gwen's birthdays. Missing her eighteenth birthday because I hadn't locked my wheelchair brakes in Norfolk felt like swallowing broken glass.

"Ready on three, Seattle?" Gwen's warm, brave voice rang clear down the long-distance line, trying with everything in her eighteen-year-old heart not to let me hear how badly she wished I were sitting across Grandpa's kitchen table instead of three time zones away in a hospital gown.

"Ready, birthday girl," I whispered thickly, wiping hot tears off my cheek with my hospital wristband sleeve as Grandpa Walter's raspy, oxygen-thinned baritone joined mine from the kitchen speakerphone to sing *Happy Birthday* across the continent—followed by the unmistakable *whoosh* of Gwen blowing all eighteen candles out over the receiver.

Then Grandpa Walter picked up the handset himself.

Back in August, Grandpa's cardiologist had quietly warned us his weakened heart might barely carry him to October twentieth. Even over copper telephone wire, I could hear the soft continuous hiss of his home oxygen concentrator and how shallowly each breath moved inside his seventy-nine-year-old chest.

"Listen to me good, Nolan," Grandpa Walter rasped, every syllable forged out of pure, stubborn Norfolk Navy iron. "Your doctor told Gwen on Tuesday your skin's finally closed enough to clear a cushioned flight next Friday. Don't you fret one minute about the calendar, boy. I don't care what those heart doctors wrote on my chart—I am *not* closing my eyes until your sister and Miss Walsh bring you back through my front door."

"Hold on for me, Grandpa," I choked into the plastic mouthpiece, pressing my forehead against the raised hospital bedrail. "Just one more week—please hold on."

"Count on it, sailor," Grandpa whispered. "See you at home."

### Part 4: Crying Into Four Safe Arms in Seattle, Grandpa's Final Watch, and Studio 205

**Date:** 2001-10-26 at 13:15 America/Los_Angeles

![Inside the sixth-floor Discharge Lounge of Puget Sound General Hospital in Seattle on a rainy October afternoon in 2001, fully dressed twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox (seated in his manual wheelchair with his matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches stowed upright behind his backrest) cries openly inside the warm embrace of newly eighteen-year-old sister Gwen Lennox (6-foot-1, burgundy sweater, matte-charcoal single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches leaning beside her chair) and Mallory Walsh (5-foot-5, copper-auburn ponytail, silver wire-rimmed glasses, forest-green cardigan) as Mallory places Ray Halstead's folded reference letter and personal phone card into Nolan's hand.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/seattle_p003_puget_sound_discharge_reunion.jpg)

#### 1:15 PM — Sixty-Six Days Without Our Greeting Hug, My Lost Shirt, and One Massively Earned Embrace

Seven days after Gwen turned eighteen—carrying her brand-new adult Virginia identification card straight through post-September-eleventh airport security checkpoints beside Mallory Walsh on two Meridian Airlines tickets backed by Grandpa Walter's Tidewater Maritime Credit Union card—the double doors of Puget Sound General's sixth-floor discharge lounge swung open.

Sitting dressed in clean jeans and my heather-navy t-shirt atop a brand-new four-inch medical gel wheelchair cushion inside my rescued manual wheelchair, my breath stopped dead in my throat.

Clicking swiftly across the polished hospital linoleum on her tall matte-charcoal Lofstrand forearm crutches—six-foot-one, eyes shining bright with fierce sisterly joy—came eighteen-year-old Gwen, with five-foot-five Mallory Walsh walking right beside her in a cozy forest-green zip cardigan and thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, carrying a folded fleece travel blanket over one arm.

Neither Gwen nor I said a word before our arms met.

Propping both charcoal crutches cleanly against the discharge counter, Gwen bent straight down over my wheelchair armrests and opened her arms wide, and I locked both of mine around my little sister's waist and shoulders in the deepest, longest, most aching greeting hug of our entire lives. Ever since we were children, hugging Gwen tightly every single time we greeted each other or said goodbye had been woven straight into my autism like breathing. In two thousand and one I didn't have the vocabulary yet to call someone my safe person—I only knew in my bones that sixty-six brutal days without pressing my forehead beneath Gwen's collarbone—across Pasco, surgical needles, watching the country fracture on a muted hospital wall screen, and singing *Happy Birthday* down a three-thousand-mile copper wire—had left every circuit in my chest starving for the one steady anchor on Earth that quieted the noise inside my head.

"Gotcha, shortstop," Gwen whispered thickly into my hair, holding me tight against her burgundy sweater for a full, unhurried minute without letting go until my whole trembling ribcage finally settled into hers. "Never letting you out of reach again."

All two months I had lain on that sixth-floor ward, Gwen and Grandpa Walter had carefully avoided talking about Store Four-Twenty-One over the hospital phone—telling me only to focus one hundred percent on healing my skin so my heart monitor wouldn't spike. The instant Gwen eased half a step back beside my right wheel with one hand still resting warm on my shoulder and I looked straight up into Mallory Walsh's unmistakable blue-gray eyes three thousand miles from Emerald Creek Parkway, that single unanswered question burst straight out of my chest.

"Mallory—you came all the way out here!" My voice cracked wide open with sudden, desperate hope as both of my hands caught hers. "Did Mr. Halstead put me on medical leave once you called Grandpa's house on Friday?! Please tell me—is there *any* way I can get my job at Blue Tech back?!"

Mallory didn't stand above my wheelchair or feed me a gentle lie to save for Virginia. Dropping cleanly onto both knees right on the hospital linoleum beside my front footrests so her eyes met mine dead on the level behind her wire-rimmed glasses, she held both of my hands steady and warm in hers.

"Nolan, I promised myself I would always tell you the exact truth," Mallory said softly, her own voice thick with emotion. "Because I waited until Friday evening's third missed shift on August twenty-fourth to call your grandfather's house, corporate's automated payroll computer up at headquarters had already processed an automatic job-abandonment termination with a corporate rehire freeze before Monday morning. Ray Halstead appealed straight to District HR with your hospital admission fax—and corporate still refused to unlock your employee number. I am so sorry I waited until Friday, Nolan."

Even after sixty-two days of hospital needles, even after Pasco and watching the world fracture on a wall screen and missing Gwen's eighteenth birthday, hearing out loud that I had permanently lost my Blue Tech job stung so brutally hard that all the breath went clean out of my lungs.

I had loved that royal-blue polo. For almost two years I had never missed one single punch on the clock, never misplaced one coaxial adapter, never given anyone a reason to doubt me—counting on every biweekly Friday paycheck to prove an autistic guy on forearm crutches could take care of his little sister when their grandfather died. Losing even that made two months of swallowed terror, homesickness, and grief break wide open at once.

My chin crumpled, a raw, ragged sob tore straight out of my throat, and right there in the middle of the sixth-floor discharge lounge, I buried my face forward and cried my heart out.

Neither Gwen nor Mallory let me cry alone for half a heartbeat.

Right on my right side, Gwen dropped onto the cushioned visitor chair flush against my wheel and pulled me hard against her shoulder; right on my left, Mallory rose off her knees onto the edge of the bench, wrapped both arms tightly around my neck and shoulders, and held my shaking head close against her green cardigan collar while her own tears dampened my hair. Caught safe inside four fierce, unhurried arms—zero yelling, zero shame, zero workplace wall left anywhere between Mallory and me—I cried into both of their arms until two months of swallowed terror finally broke open.

"Let the brave mask go, Nolan—just lean on the two of us," Gwen whispered fiercely against my temple, rocking my shoulders gently while grey Seattle rain drummed against the sixth-floor hospital glass three thousand miles from Virginia. "What Tammy did to you in Pasco wasn't your fault, and losing that blue shirt wasn't your fault either. Mallory and I are holding the line right beside you from here on out. Your broken heart will mend with time—I promise you it will—and we're getting you out of this hospital and all the way home."

"Every single mile of the way," Mallory whispered steadily against my other ear once my ragged breathing slowed, pressing both a crisp letter on Blue Tech letterhead and a white business card into my palm. "Ray Halstead has a five-year-old autistic granddaughter, Nolan—the minute he heard Tammy dragged you to Norfolk, he understood your literal brain didn't connect boarding that bus with getting fired here because you assumed she'd let you call us from a rest stop. Ray couldn't overrule Corporate's computer, so he wrote his own personal phone number on the back of this card and promised that any employer you apply to can call him directly off the clock for a glowing professional reference. And losing Blue Tech also means as of August twenty-fourth, I stopped being your Shift Supervisor."

Wiping my wet cheeks on my wrist, I looked straight across two inches of air into Mallory's honest, freckled face—letting the whole picture sink into my literal brain at once.

For two straight months while skies reopened and my wound closed, Mallory hadn't moved on or forgotten the quiet guy from Computers; she had checked on Grandpa Walter and Gwen off Eden Way North week after week, booked the very first flight Gwen turned eighteen to board, and crossed an entire continent just to bring me back.

"Mallory," I whispered thickly from my wheelchair, reaching both hands slightly toward her green cardigan lapels, "can I hug you right now—just for you?"

Tears shone bright behind Mallory's silver wire-rimmed glasses as the warmest, most relieved smile broke across her face.

"Come here, Nolan," Mallory whispered, leaning right down over my left armrest to wrap both arms all the way around my shoulders while I locked both of mine tightly around her waist and back—burying my cheek against the soft wool of her collar in a long, quiet, massively earned hug that neither of us rushed by a single second. Right beside my right wheel, newly eighteen-year-old Gwen leaned back in the visitor chair watching the two of us with shining green-hazel eyes and the happiest, most wholeheartedly approving grin on Earth.

![Inside the rainy sixth-floor Discharge Lounge of Puget Sound General Hospital in Seattle, fully dressed twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox sits in his manual wheelchair with his two matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches stowed upright behind his backrest, locked in a deeply emotional, massively earned two-armed hug with Mallory Walsh kneeling warmly beside him while newly eighteen-year-old sister Gwen Lennox sits right beside Nolan's wheel with her matte-charcoal single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches leaning beside her chair, beaming with proud, wholehearted sisterly approval]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/paths/seattle_p004_mallory_earned_hug_gwen_approving.jpg#fullwidth)

"Okay, you two," Gwen smiled gently when Mallory finally eased back just enough to lace her fingers warmly over my hand on my wheelchair armrest. "We aren't home in Tidewater yet—we still have Nolan's discharge signatures, post-September-eleventh airport checkpoints, and two flights ahead of us—so let's get my brother out of Seattle while Grandpa Walter's holding watch for his face."

***

#### 8:10 PM, Saturday, October 27 — Keeping Watch Until Morning, Ray's Reference, and Studio 205

True to every word of his sailor's vow, seventy-nine-year-old Grandpa Walter was still awake inside his downstairs bedroom off Eden Way North when Mallory's silver compact rolled into our Chesapeake driveway Saturday night from Norfolk International Airport.

Propped against two pillows beneath his dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket with nasal oxygen tubing tucked behind his ears, Grandpa Walter opened his tired gray eyes the instant I rolled my manual wheelchair right up beside his mattress—with newly eighteen-year-old Gwen standing tall on her charcoal crutches behind my right shoulder and Mallory standing quietly in the doorway. Reaching out one thin, paper-light machinist hand to cover mine and Gwen's together, Grandpa looked straight at my healed, rested face and smiled the most peaceful smile I had ever seen.

"Made it home, boy," Grandpa whispered softly. "Both of you... look after each other now."

Sometime before six o'clock the next morning—with both of his grandchildren finally safe under one roof and Gwen legally eighteen beyond any social-services claim—Walter Lennox kept watch one last quiet night and slipped away peacefully in his sleep.

Everything Grandpa, Gwen, Ray Halstead, and Mallory had protected carried us straight forward through November without me ever needing a retail register badge again. Back at the end of August—the Monday after Mallory first called our house—Danny from Blue Tech's warehouse and a Chesapeake sheriff's deputy had already cleared my full-sized bed, oak dresser, nineteen-inch television, silver VCR and DVD combo, and laptop out of Tammy's Pinecrest Court trailer into Grandpa's garage, while Grandpa mailed my certified ninety-day lot non-renewal notice so my name dropped off Tammy's lease automatically at midnight on November thirtieth despite Tammy's stubborn refusal to sign an early release.

Better yet, without retail shifts pulling me away from Grandpa's house while Gwen and I navigated Chesapeake probate and put our two-story childhood house up for sale to pay off Grandpa's winter medical home-equity loan, Gwen spotted a November staff opening downtown at the Community College of Hampton Roads Norfolk Campus inside the Martin Building for a twenty-eight-hour-a-week seated Part-Time Campus Computer Lab and Helpdesk Technician. When CCHR's campus IT director called the personal home number Ray Halstead had written on his card after my practical interview, fifty-two-year-old Ray gave me a glowing fifteen-minute reference—explaining plainly that I had been tricked onto an out-of-state bus without phone access after twenty-two spotless months and calling me the sharpest Quantum OS 2000 technician he had ever supervised—landing me the seated campus workbench job two weeks before moving day at two dollars more an hour than retail ever paid.

On December first, Gwen and I unlocked Studio 205 on the second floor of Harborview Towers—a twenty-story nineteen-sixty-five high-rise overlooking The Hague inlet off West Brambleton Avenue in Norfolk, six blocks from Ghent High School where Gwen transferred to finish eleventh grade, and one six-minute ride down CVTA Route 2 or Route 11 from my CCHR lab bench. When Mallory carried our microwave cart inside, took one look at our single corner full-sized bed draped in Grandpa Walter's plaid blanket, and surprised both Gwen and me by saying plainly that sharing the one bed that fit so Gwen wasn't out on the street made complete engineering sense—before confessing out on the stairwell landing that she had been in love with me since Store Four-Twenty-One and becoming my girlfriend—every broken piece of two thousand and one finally clicked whole.

Ten quiet, happy months later—with me twenty-four, Gwen newly nineteen in her senior year at Ghent High, Saturday morning open-lab shifts at CCHR followed by Ghent lunches with Mallory, and both of us curled up barefoot on our shared corner bed on a rainy October Friday night—Regional Mobility Supply's hold music warbled past forty-seven minutes as the air beside our dresser shimmered warm amber.

***

# Story Path: Reject Tammy's Promise & Roll Out Into the Concourse {#crossroads-path-lock-wheelchair-brakes-emerald-creek}

*Realize peaceful, yell-free hugs with Gwen beat empty promises every time—rolling straight out from behind the lockers into plain view of thirty witnesses to lock your wheelchair brakes, end things for good, and ride three CVTA buses safely home to Emerald Creek.*

*[← Return to the Story Crossroads](#crossroads-norfolk-pathfinder-terminal-2001)*

# Book One: Beyond the Studio Rift

## Chapter 1: Two Names on One Studio Lease

### Part 10: Rolling Out Past the Lockers, Fellow Passengers, and Safe Home to Emerald Creek

**Date:** 2001-08-21 at 09:51 America/New_York

![On the cozy covered front porch of Grandpa Walter's two-story brick-and-siding home near Emerald Creek Mall in Chesapeake on an overcast late-summer afternoon, taller seventeen-year-old Gwen Lennox (6-foot-1, in a modest burgundy plaid flannel shirt and jeans) wraps both arms joyfully and protectively around her shorter twenty-three-year-old brother Nolan Lennox (5-foot-7, in a navy tee and olive jacket with his battered duffel bag at his feet) after he breaks free from his abusive girlfriend at the Norfolk bus station, while all four of their matte-silver and matte-charcoal single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches lean cleanly against the wooden porch railing beside them.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p001_safe_home_emerald_creek_porch.jpg)

#### 9:51 AM — Out From Behind the Lockers Into Plain Sight

Twice before inside Pinecrest Court, having Tammy lift her shirt and whisper in her Week-One voice had scrambled every circuit in my lonely head and pulled me straight back under her thumb.

Sitting in my manual wheelchair between the dented gray steel coin lockers and the tiled restroom doors two heartbeats after she dropped her shelf-bra camisole hem back down, something quiet, clear, and unshakable clicked into place inside my chest instead.

I didn't need sex to be happy.

All those months of walking on eggshells, flinching at slammed cabinets, and getting screamed at over nothing had never been a fair trade for two seconds of bare skin behind a sweater—or four days trapped on an unreserved cross-country bus built on a promise she had already broken twice. What my autistic heart actually needed to feel safe and whole wasn't sex at all; it was simply a female I could quietly hug and cuddle with in peace. And I *already* had someone I could hug and cuddle with who loved me unconditionally, spoke plain honest truth, and *never* yelled at me: my sister Gwen, who was waiting back home in Emerald Creek.

Better still, I wasn't staying tucked inside a blind locker alcove where nobody in the station could see us.

Gripping both steel push-rims firmly, I rolled straight out from behind the concrete column right into the open center of the speckled-terrazzo waiting room in front of thirty witnesses—and snapped both steel wheel locks down hard on my tires before Tammy got halfway to the ticket queue.

"Save your money, Tammy," I said clearly.

She stopped mid-stride in her chunky white platform sneakers and spun around, dark eyebrows snapping together over her round face in disbelief. "What did you just say?"

My heart hammered fast against my breastbone, yet looking up from my locked wheelchair in plain public view instead of a hidden corner, the literal truth came out steady and final.

"Don't buy a ticket for me," I said calmly, meeting her eyes without blinking. "What you just pulled back by the lockers isn't gonna work on me anymore, and I don't believe a word about zero yelling in Seattle. You never called Pathfinder forty-eight hours ahead for a wheelchair lift, I have work at Blue Tech tomorrow, Gwen turns eighteen in October, and Grandpa Walter's heart doesn't have many months left. The first two times we broke up, Tammy, you were the one who did it. This third time, it's me. We're breaking up for good."

Having her ultimate, never-fail trump card *actually played* two seconds earlier—and watching me roll straight out into the concourse and turn her down flat anyway—blew every fuse behind Tammy's eyes into ugly, blotchy red humiliation and fury.

"You sneaky little bastard!" Tammy shrieked so loud heads whipped around in every bolted row of plastic seats. "You let me haul my suitcase all the way down to Monticello Avenue just to dump me in a bus station?! Nobody cares about your forty-eight-hour cripple excuses or your six-foot freak of a sister—you're getting on that bus with me right now!"

"No, Tammy," I said, holding both locked wheel rims steady while my throat knotted up against the sudden shouting. "Go see your family by yourself. I'm going home."

Tammy didn't say another word. Standing nine inches above my seated frame in her platform sneakers right in the middle of the open waiting room, she swung her right arm back and violently smacked me flat across the left side of my face with all her weight.

The crack rang off the tile walls like a board snapping in half.

My head jerked hard against my right shoulder, knocking my neck stiff while blinding white heat and a high-pitched ringing bloomed across my cheek and ear. Sitting locked in my manual wheelchair with fluorescent lights buzzing overhead, shouting echoing off terrazzo, nowhere to back up, and two years of sensory and emotional exhaustion colliding with sudden physical pain in front of forty strangers, my nervous system overloaded all at once and the dam inside my chest broke completely apart.

My breath hitched in a ragged, broken gasp, and I started crying right there in my wheelchair in the middle of the waiting room—heavy, chest-shaking sobs pouring out of me before I could stop them, hot tears spilling down my stinging face onto my jacket collar while I pressed both palms over my eyes.

Tammy raised her hand again, teeth bared over my chair. "Stop crying like a pathetic little—"

Fellow passengers moved faster than she could swing.

Plastic chairs scraped loud across the terrazzo as a broad-shouldered shipyard worker in a navy canvas coat lunged straight between Tammy and my footrests, catching her forearm and shoving her firmly backward toward the locker alcove where her suitcase sat while a middle-aged woman in teal hospital scrubs and a young guy in a culinary jacket stepped in shoulder-to-shoulder around my wheelchair.

"Get back off him right now—don't you dare touch him again!" the shipyard worker barked, pushing Tammy back another full pace when she tried to claw past his chest while two more passengers yelled for the uniformed guards by the vending bank. "Security! Right here—this woman just hauled off and smacked a man sitting in a wheelchair!"

"Stay out of my business!" Tammy screamed, face purple, clutching her unspent cash envelope as the two security officers jogged up fast to flank her elbows.

"Out the front doors right now, ma'am, or Norfolk PD puts cuffs on you," the older guard ordered flatly while the culinary student rolled her suitcase out from the locker bank behind her heels so she couldn't step back within ten feet of my chair.

My whole chest kept heaving in jagged, humiliating sobs I couldn't turn off. Kneeling down to eye level beside my right wheel so she wasn't towering over me, the woman in teal scrubs pressed a clean stack of folded paper napkins gently into my hand and spoke in the kindest, steadying voice imaginable.

"You're completely safe now, sweetheart—she's out under the corner canopy with security and she can't get back in," she said softly. "Just breathe. Where's home for you? Have you got family waiting?"

Wiping furiously at my wet eyes and burning left cheek with the napkins, choking through heavy sobs that broke every sentence in half, I managed to get the words out.

"I just—I just want to go home," I sobbed, gripping my push-rims with shaking hands. "I just want to go home to the Emerald Creek area of Chesapeake... my little sister turns eighteen in October and my grandpa's dying, and I just want to go home to them."

Nobody looked down on me for crying. An older gray-haired retired chief carrying a canvas tool bag stepped up beside the nurse, made sure the straps holding my silver forearm crutches on the back of my wheelchair hadn't loosened, and nodded calmly toward the side exit away from Monticello Avenue's front curb.

"Easy three-bus hop gets you straight back to Emerald Creek Mall, son," the retired chief said warmly. "Take the two or the eleven—whichever rolls by first—back down into Downtown Norfolk, catch the twenty out to Glenrock Mall, and hop the fifteen right into Emerald Creek. Come out the side doors with me—I'm heading downtown myself, so I'll ride beside your chair till you're safely on that twenty bus."

***

#### 12:15 PM — Stopping Short of the Grassy Island at Emerald Creek Mall

True to his word, the retired chief waited with me a block off the terminal until a southbound Route 2 Beacon V pulled up ahead of the eleven, rode all the way back into Downtown Norfolk beside my wheelchair, and didn't wave goodbye until the front-stairwell Elevate-U lift on an eastbound CVTA Route 20 coach had raised me safely aboard for the run out Virginia Beach Boulevard.

By the time the salt tracks on my cheeks dried in the overhead air vent and our Route 20 bus approached the wide intersection of Virginia Beach Boulevard and Military Highway just outside Glenrock Mall, the operator unhooked his radio handset to call ahead for my pulse transfer.

"Outbound twenty to the outbound fifteen at Glenrock Mall, I have one ten-sixteen for you," he said into the mic.

<pa>Ten-four. Is he heading towards Emerald Creek Mall?</pa>

The operator glanced up into his wide interior mirror toward my wheelchair bay. "Heading to Emerald Creek Mall?"

"Yes, sir," I managed, my voice still hoarse yet steady.

"Ten-four, he says he's heading to Emerald Creek Mall," the operator confirmed into the handset.

<pa>Ten-four, Route 15 copies.</pa>

At Glenrock Mall, the waiting southbound Route 15 Beacon V held its front doors open while its Elevate-U platform lifted my chair aboard, then rumbled south down all eight lanes of Military Highway—gliding over the low, flat highway bridge across the Eastern Branch of the Elizabeth River and branching off at Old Emerald Creek Road toward the mall.

Anyone who rode a wheelchair to Emerald Creek Mall knew the catch waiting out on Mall Ring Road. Instead of a paved transit shelter or a sidewalk landing pad, CVTA's official Emerald Creek Mall bus stop was nothing more than a metal bus-stop sign and schedule placard driven into the middle of a narrow, oval grassy parking-lot island ringed on all sides by an unbroken six-inch concrete curb—zero pavement and zero curb cuts anywhere on it. If an operator pulled up flush to the sign and lowered the wheelchair lift onto that grassy island, your casters were trapped inside six inches of concrete curbing.

As our forty-foot coach banked onto Mall Ring Road across the sea of parked cars from the department store entrance and eased toward that curbed strip of dry grass, I unlocked my vocal cords and called forward clearly.

"Driver, I'm getting off here at the mall—could you stop short of the island and put me off on the roadway?"

"Sure thing, man—know that curb well," the operator called back with an understanding nod in his mirror. Flipping on his four-way hazard flashers in the wide parking-lot drive lane a full bus length short of the grassy island and the bus-stop post, he pulled the air brake and cycled the front stairwell flat into the Elevate-U platform, lowering my manual wheelchair smoothly onto the level gray asphalt of the parking lot roadway.

Grandpa Walter's house wasn't far from the outer ring road at all. Rolling clear of the mall lot into the quiet maple-lined neighborhood blocks, I locked my wheelchair brakes at the foot of Grandpa's three wooden front steps, unstrapped my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches from the back of my chair, hoisted my olive duffel onto the boards, and climbed up onto the covered front porch just as seventeen-year-old Gwen pushed open the storm door on her tall matte-charcoal crutches.

Six-foot-one Gwen took one look at my red-rimmed hazel eyes, the faint pink mark fading on my left cheek, and my wheelchair sitting parked at the bottom of the steps—and the second I told her hoarsely that I had broken up with Tammy at the Norfolk bus station for good, pure sunshine broke across my little sister's face. Propping both of her charcoal crutches cleanly beside my silver pair against the wooden porch railing, Gwen wrapped both long arms fiercely around my neck and hugged me with all her strength.

"You dumped her—you actually dumped her!" Gwen gasped, laughing through happy tears against my hair on Grandpa's porch. "Oh, Nolan, I missed you so much! You're home before junior year even starts!"
