Inside the downstairs family room, seventy-nine-year-old Grandpa Walter sat propped in his worn leather recliner with his dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket folded across one armrest and a thick manila accordion folder tied with string resting across his knees. Though he wasn't on supplemental oxygen yet and still drove his silver-tan sedan on short daytime errands around Emerald Creek, the winter pneumonia and congestive-heart-failure hospitalization had thinned his broad Navy-machinist shoulders noticeably, making it plain why his cardiologist had told him to keep his affairs in order—and why Grandpa was holding on with quiet determination to see Gwen turn eighteen in October free of any city social-services claim.

While Gwen went upstairs to make up the full-sized bed in my old bedroom, Grandpa untied the manila folder in the downstairs family room—explaining he was already planning to move his bed downstairs near the first-floor full bathroom once the stairs up to his master suite got too steep—and quietly walked me through his earthly paperwork ahead of time: his notarized two-page will naming me executor, his honorable Navy discharge from his years stationed at Norfolk and modest burial policy, and the ninety-day payoff terms on the six-figure home-equity medical loan he had been forced to borrow after months of uncovered winter ICU and cardiac care wiped out the comfortable upper-middle-class nest egg he had built before ever taking custody of us—meaning whenever his time came, our big two-story house would have to be sold.

"I'm making October nineteenth, boy," Grandpa Walter said with a steady smile, squeezing my wrist. "I'm seeing your sister turn eighteen. After that, Nolan... make sure she's got a home right beside her brother."

Twenty minutes later, walking four blocks over to the Emerald Creek Blue Tech on my matte-silver forearm crutches to ask Danny in warehouse freight if I could borrow his pickup truck for sheriff civil standby, Mallory Walsh was the very first person outside my family I told. Standing behind the Computers counter with that blue ballpoint pen tucked behind her ear, Mallory listened to me say I had rolled out into the middle of the Norfolk Pathfinder Terminal and ended things with Tammy for good—and broke into the widest, brightest smile I had ever seen behind her thin silver wire-rimmed glasses.

"Good—best news this building's heard all year, Nolan Lennox," Mallory said fiercely, already unclipping her retractable supervisor override key-reel to sign off on covering Danny's afternoon freight window herself. "Get every stick of your furniture out of there today, and don't you dare sign anything letting her rope you back in."

That very afternoon—after a Chesapeake sheriff's deputy stood ten minutes of civil standby outside Pinecrest Court while Danny loaded my full-sized bed, oak dresser, nineteen-inch television, silver VCR and DVD combo unit, and Quantum OS 2000 Professional laptop out of Tammy's rusty, landlord-patched trailer into his pickup before Tammy even straggled back from Norfolk—we swung by the Pinecrest Court park manager's trailer office so I could turn in my brass trailer key and get my name off the lease. Back in November of ninety-nine, when Tammy and I had decided I was moving in, the park's fourteen-day occupant rule had required me to bring two Blue Tech direct-deposit paystubs into that office and sign onto Tammy's lease as a co-tenant—and during both of the earlier breakups Tammy had initiated, she had never taken my name off the lease because she hadn't meant to yell at me either time. Because taking a co-tenant off immediately required Tammy's signature on a roommate release—which Tammy stubbornly refused to sign once she got home, holding out all autumn convinced I would come crawling back—filing my formal ninety-day park lot non-renewal notice on August twenty-first meant my name stayed legally on that trailer lease until midnight on November thirtieth. Paying my final share up front so my credit stayed spotless, Danny and I hauled all of my furniture back to Emerald Creek and unloaded it safely under a tarp in the open second bay of Grandpa Walter's two-car garage.

Even better, when I worked up the nerve at our quiet breakroom corner table the following week to ask Mallory plainly whether she might ever want to grab dinner off the clock sometime—since she spoke my exact literal language and was the easiest friend I had ever talked to—Mallory didn't play a single guessing game. Adjusting her wire-rimmed glasses with a gentle, unmistakable warmth in her blue-gray eyes that told even me she wasn't saying no out of dislike, she tapped the small silver Shift Supervisor pin on her royal-blue collar and laid down a clean, rock-solid rule: as long as we both worked at Blue Tech and her key-reel signed off on floor overrides and schedules, she flat-out refused to date anyone in the store so personal feelings could never get in the way of a business decision—while promising the corner chair opposite hers at lunch stayed mine every single shift. In my rulebook, a clear, honest boundary stated in plain English was ten times better than romance anyway; Mallory and I settled effortlessly into being genuine friends, and come December first, my name would drop off Tammy's lease automatically with zero overlap, leaving me free to sign a lease on Harborview Towers' tiny second-floor Studio 205 over in Norfolk with Gwen right beside me.

### Part 11: Seventy-Five Ohms, Fast-Busy Lines, and Eden Way North

**Date:** 2001-09-11 at 08:48 America/New_York

#### 8:48 AM — Behind the Darkened Wall

Every blister pack of seventy-five-ohm coaxial splitters in Aisle Four hung flush with the front plastic lip of its peg hook under half-lit metal-halide ceiling lamps, ninety minutes into a quiet Tuesday morning that smelled like cardboard freight and floor buffer wax.

Three weeks of sleeping back upstairs in my childhood full-sized bed under Grandpa Walter's roof—after breaking free of Tammy at the Norfolk bus station in late August—had finally begun settling my nervous system back into one piece. Because my calves felt tight from helping Grandpa rake early pine needles off the driveway yesterday evening, I had rolled the four flat blocks over to Blue Tech in my manual wheelchair with both of my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches strapped upright in the holder tubes behind my backrest, clocked in right at eight-thirty for my five-hour opening shift, and rolled back to Home Theater while the front glass doors stayed locked until ten.

Along the darkened back feature wall, all fifty display televisions—curved twenty-seven-inch and thirty-six-inch cathode-ray tubes, hulking rear-projection cabinets, and two brand-new flat plasma screens bolted above the center rack—sat completely black and silent, waiting for nine-thirty when we normally flipped on the muted corporate movie-trailer loop.

Then the heavy double doors from the receiving bay swung inward with a dull metallic thud.

Danny from warehouse freight walked out into the center aisle without his box cutter or clipboard, face the color of drywall dust, and spoke low to my Home Theater supervisor, Ray Halstead.

Ray Halstead was fifty-two, a soft-spoken retired Navy chief electronics technician who kept two ballpoint pens clipped neatly inside his royal-blue polo placket and had never once looked rattled through two Black Fridays or three hurricane warnings. Because his five-year-old granddaughter was autistic, Ray had quietly understood how both Mallory Walsh's brain and mine were wired from the very first week we worked for him—always speaking in plain, literal steps without ever talking down to us. Watching Ray's dark eyebrows pull sharply together before he turned straight toward my wheelchair sent a sudden, nameless chill across my forearms.

"Nolan," Ray said quietly, gesturing toward the narrow black-painted service corridor behind the display cabinetry. "Bring me a brass female-to-female barrel coupler off the third peg. Danny says the warehouse radio just cut in—some kind of plane hit one of the World Trade Center towers up in New York."

At ten minutes to nine, inside a locked electronics store under the clearest, brightest blue September sky Tidewater had seen all month, my literal brain filed the sentence the only way it knew how: a terrible navigation malfunction, maybe a small commuter prop plane losing instruments over the Hudson.

Ducking into the warm, dusty gap behind the plywood display wall while I rolled smoothly up to the black steel distribution rack and handed him a brass coupler, Ray unscrewed the threaded coaxial lead coming off our corporate DVD and satellite promo deck—the upbeat forty-five-minute reel of movie trailers, snowboarding clips, and bouncing blue store logos that played muted on every normal Tuesday—and threaded our live satellite news receiver straight onto the input jack of the sixty-way master RF amplifier.

Except Ray's steady technician fingers slipped twice on the hex nut before the threads caught.

Reaching up, Ray threw the three red rocker switches on the master power strip forty minutes ahead of schedule. Out front across the carpet, fifty cathode-ray tubes energized in a single crackling wave of static degaussing thumps and faint warm ozone, brightening slowly from gray phosphor dots into fifty identical rectangles of cloudless Manhattan sky and dark smoke on CSN. Stepping back around the endcap beside my wheelchair, Ray pointed a remote at a single center-aisle receiver just enough for the anchor's measured, uncertain voice to carry across the empty store. Five other morning associates drifted over from Computers and Front Lanes, holding price-tag guns loosely at their sides while anchors wondered aloud what size aircraft could have strayed off course on an unlimited-visibility morning.

Seven minutes later, at three minutes past nine, nobody in the aisle spoke a single word.

I didn't stare at the fifty screens. The instant a collective intake of breath sucked the air clean out of the carpet around me, my eyes went straight sideways to Ray Halstead's face two feet above my right wheel.

Every trace of color drained out of my supervisor's cheeks in a single heartbeat. His mouth parted half an inch without a sound coming out, the little plastic coaxial dust cap he had been turning between his thumb and forefinger dropped unheeded onto the low-pile gray carpet, and for five eternal seconds a calm grown man who knew how to fix anything with a circuit board looked completely lost—staring forward with wet, unblinking eyes that already understood the world outside our locked glass doors had just split in two.

Over in the front office, our store manager quietly clicked a toggle switch, shutting off the upbeat pop music playing through the ceiling speakers. Into that sudden, cavernous quiet—fifty synchronized screens murmuring in one voice under buzzing warehouse lamps—I knew down to my bones that even if I lived to be ninety, I would never forget the look on Ray Halstead's face.

***

#### 10:25 AM — Unconfirmed Bulletins and Twelve Miles South of the Piers

Unlocking the front entrance at ten o'clock felt unreal, as if following a Thursday-corkboard retail schedule belonged to a planet that no longer existed.

Nobody walked in asking about printer ink or DVD players. Four pale employees from the dry cleaner and shoe store down the strip wandered quietly back to the Home Theater carpet to stand shoulder to shoulder beside our royal-blue polos without saying hello, while a handful of drivers hurried up to Front Lanes buying nothing except four-packs of D-cell lantern batteries and small battery-operated AM/FM weather radios.

For how my autistic brain was wired—relying my whole life on timetables, printed manuals, and broadcast anchors to state verified, dependable truth—watching live coverage unravel over the next hour felt like watching gravity stop working. First came word from Northern Virginia at the Pentagon; then, right around twenty past ten, an urgent wire bulletin flashed onto CSN reporting that a car bomb had just exploded outside the State Department in Washington. Minutes after that came garbled dispatches mentioning a plane possibly down near Camp David, alongside grim, shifting counts warning that even after federal air-traffic control ordered every civilian airliner in the United States onto the ground at once, half a dozen or more commercial flights might still be unaccounted for somewhere overhead.

Even the anchors didn't know what was real and what was panic anymore.

Out beyond our front plate-glass windows, the wide Virginia sky—normally traced every ten minutes by white commercial passenger jets banking toward Norfolk International—sat completely, unnaturally empty until two military fighter jets out of Langley or Oceana tore low across Emerald Creek on hard afterburner, rattling the glass doors hard enough for the vibration to hum straight up through my wheelchair footrests into my shins.

Twice between battery customers I rolled hurriedly into the breakroom and lifted the beige wall telephone receiver—dialing Grandpa Walter's kitchen landline first, then the main attendance number over at Emerald Creek High School where seventeen-year-old Gwen sat in junior-year classes.

Both times, before the last digit barely cleared the keypad, the earpiece barked back a harsh, rapid-fire fast-busy tone—two pulses a second, every trunk line across Hampton Roads slammed solid.

At ten minutes to eleven, rolling back out to Register Two with my chest already tight as an over-wound spring, a middle-aged man in shipyard coveralls set three bricks of alkaline batteries onto my counter with visibly unsteady hands.

"You folks got any crank radios left in the back?" he asked hoarsely, glancing over his shoulder toward the fifty dark-plumed televisions at the back of the store. "My brother-in-law's pinned in four miles of gate lockdown traffic up on five-sixty-four. Said word just came off the pier gate that one of those unaccounted-for planes is heading straight down the bay for Naval Station Norfolk."

Every muscle in my throat locked solid.

Grandpa Walter had served in the United States Navy stationed right at Naval Station Norfolk. Every black-and-white framed ship photograph hanging down our upstairs hallway, every quiet story Grandpa had ever told Gwen and me over soup on rainy nights while teaching us to polish our crutch tips, lived twelve miles north up the interstate along those gray Norfolk piers—with all of Chesapeake sitting right underneath the southern approach. And Grandpa was seventy-nine years old with a weakened heart back at the house, Gwen was locked inside a high school building without a way for me to call either of them, and nobody on television knew how many planes were left in the sky.

When I reached for my handheld laser scanner to ring up the battery pack, my right hand was trembling so violently that the plastic handle slipped straight through my fingers and clattered hard onto the linoleum floor.

"Whoa—easy there, buddy," the shipyard customer murmured softly, bending to pick it up.

I couldn't even answer him. Both of my hands clamped onto the steel push-rims of my manual wheelchair, yet my forearms shook so uncontrollably that my rubber caster wheels chattered faintly against the tile. My breathing came short, ragged, and shallow behind my breastbone while fluorescent lights, fast-busy tones, afterburner roar, and fifty echoing screens all collided inside my skull at once. I couldn't count change. I couldn't read a barcode. I couldn't continue my shift.

Ray Halstead was beside my wheelchair in four quiet strides.

He didn't ask me to pull myself together, didn't glance at the register queue, and didn't stand towering over my head. Dropping onto one knee right beside my left wheel so his tired, red-rimmed eyes met mine on the level, my supervisor rested one steady palm briefly over my shaking wrist, stood up only long enough to tap his manager override code into Register Two to sign my number out for the day, and handed me my backpack from under the counter.

"Go on home, Nolan," Ray said gently, nodding toward the front sidewalk. "You live right down Eden Way, don't you? Leave the timeclock to me. Go be with your grandpa and your sister right now."

"Yes, sir—thank you, Mr. Halstead," I whispered thickly, slinging both backpack straps over my shoulders with trembling fingers before pushing out through the automatic sliding doors into the glaring, silent morning sunlight.

Out past the empty parking bays along Emerald Creek Parkway, traffic sat weirdly clotted at the turn lanes while drivers leaned halfway out rolled-down windows listening to AM news broadcasts. Turning my front casters east onto the concrete sidewalk of Eden Way North—my matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches clinking softly in the backrest tubes behind my shoulders with every hard, rapid stroke—I leaned forward over my knees and drove my wheels homeward as fast as my shaking arms could push.

Overhead, that cloudless blue dome stretched wide and silent except for the distant, circular thunder of combat air patrols banking high toward the bay. Sweat stung my temples inside my royal-blue work polo as I cleared the second driveway apron, chest heaving ragged, counting sidewalk expansion joints just to keep panic from locking my lungs—just four blocks, just get past Merchants Way, just make sure Grandpa's car is in the driveway and Gwenny's okay—right as I rolled up to the corner of Eden Way North and Merchants Way beside the brick facade of Emerald Creek Station.

Thirty feet to my left, behind the shaded walkway pillars, the glass front door of the United States Coast Guard Recruiting Station already stood locked shut from the inside with a white readiness notice taped flat against the pane.

Right as my front casters dipped into the concrete curb cut at Merchants Way, tires crunched briskly to a stop alongside the gutter three feet from my left elbow, and a horn gave two short, unmistakable taps.

Looking sideways through blurred eyes, I stared straight at the front fender of Grandpa Walter's silver-tan four-door sedan—with Gwen already throwing the rear passenger door wide open before the wheels had barely stopped rolling.

### Part 12: Second-Block Latin, the German Classroom, and Holding On at Home

**Date:** 2001-09-11 at 08:55 America/New_York

#### 8:55 AM — Magistra's Doorway and Two Classes in One Room

Right as the second-block tardy bell buzzed down the foreign-language wing of Emerald Creek High School, I propped both of my tall matte-charcoal Lofstrand forearm crutches neatly against the steel leg of my front-row desk, opened my green spiral Latin III notebook to Tuesday's subjunctive lines, and uncapped my pen.

Most mornings of junior year, sitting six-foot-one in a desk row full of sixteen-year-olds still sparked a quiet flare of irritation whenever I remembered the Chesapeake school district holding me back an entire grade in early elementary school over hospital-cast attendance days after childhood leg surgery—a rigid, paper-pushing decision I still hated the district for, even if Nolan liked reminding me that straight A's were the best revenge. Second-block Latin III, at least, never treated anybody like a slow child. Up at the green slate chalkboard, Magistra was already chalking the date in Roman numerals while crisp, bright September sunlight slanted across twenty wooden desktops.

She hadn't finished the last numeral when hurried adult footsteps and low, urgent voices echoed down the waxed linoleum hallway outside our closed oak door.

A sharp knock tapped twice against the frosted glass pane. Magistra paused mid-stroke, chalk poised in her fingers, frowned slightly at the interruption, and stepped out into the corridor, pulling the door mostly shut behind her.

Across the room, half a dozen juniors traded perplexed looks—wondering if somebody had pulled a fire alarm in the science wing or if the A-day bell clock had misfired.

Less than ninety seconds later, Magistra pushed the door back open.

Every trace of brisk classroom poise had vanished from her face. Standing just inside the threshold with one hand gripping the brass doorknob hard enough to whiten her knuckles, she didn't even glance back toward her chalkboard or her lesson binder.

"Leave your books and notebooks right where they are," Magistra said, her voice tight and faintly unsteady in the sudden quiet. "Everyone stand up quietly and come down the hall with me to the German classroom. Something terrible has happened—they're saying the World Trade Center up in New York has been blown up."

Chairs scraped softly across tile with zero talking or joking. Clicking my charcoal cuffs around my forearms, I rose smoothly beside my desk and matched pace along the cinderblock wall as all twenty-two of us filed three doors down into Herr Weber's German classroom.

Inside, beneath laminated maps of Bavaria and verb-declension posters, twenty-odd German students had already shoved desks together to clear space, every single pair of eyes tilted upward toward the twenty-five-inch black CRT television strapped onto a steel wall bracket in the upper front corner—one of the few rooms on our hall wired with a live coaxial cable drop. Easing onto an open wooden chair near the side table while two sophomores perched side by side on a radiator sill behind me, I tilted my head up toward the glowing glass tuned to CSN.

High against a cloudless, brilliant blue sky that looked identical to the morning sky outside our Chesapeake windows, thick dark smoke poured from the upper floors of one tall silver tower while network anchors spoke rapidly over live helicopter video—still sounding half-convinced some terrible guidance failure had sent an aircraft off course.

Nobody in those two combined language classes whispered. Nobody shifted a sneaker on the linoleum.

Then, at three minutes past nine, right before forty-five teenagers' eyes on CSN, a second shadow crossed the blue corner of the screen—and every single person crammed inside that German classroom realized in the exact same frozen breath that nothing we were watching had been an accident.

Somebody in the back row let out a small, choked gasp; beside the light switch, Herr Weber reached up and covered his mouth with one hand while Magistra stood rigid as stone beside his desk.

We didn't go back to Latin III—not ten minutes later, and not an hour later. Both classes stayed crowded together inside that German classroom for the entire ninety-minute second block. Within half an hour, word broke from up north at the Pentagon—and inside a Tidewater Virginia public school where half the kids in every row had a mother, father, or older sibling wearing Navy khakis at Norfolk, working cranes at the shipyard, or flying out of Oceana, quiet dread turned acute and personal. Two girls in the middle row began crying silently into their sleeves, terrified which pier or Pentagon corridor a parent had reported to that morning; main-office student runners started cracking the hallway door every three minutes with handfuls of pink early-dismissal slips as shaken parents lined up outside attendance; and right near the end of the block around twenty past ten, the CSN anchor read an urgent wire bulletin reporting that a car bomb had just exploded outside the State Department.

My stomach knotted hard around one immediate thought: four blocks past the mall, Nolan was clocked in at Blue Tech with his literal, routine-loving brain surrounded by fifty televisions, and neither of us carried a cell phone.

***

#### 10:32 AM — Overhead Projectors, Attendance Slips, and Emerald Creek Station

When the ten-twenty-five passing bell rang—sounding surreal and almost obscene echoing down hushed hallways where nobody slammed a locker—word had already come down from administration that classroom televisions were to stay switched off for third block.

Ten minutes later inside third-block Social Studies, the corner television hung completely black and dead above the flag.

Standing beside a humming glass overhead projector at the front cart, Mr. Hargrove clicked its blinding white bulb on, laid a clear plastic outline sheet across the lens, and desperately attempted to conduct a normal class session—reading nineteenth-century tariff bullet points aloud to half-empty rows as if sticking to Tuesday's syllabus by sheer stubbornness could hold the roof up over seventeen-year-olds' heads. Beneath the projector's cooling fan, the blue dry-erase marker trembled slightly between his fingers every time he underlined a date, and outside the tall courtyard windows, the low, unmistakable thunder of military jet engines rolled across Chesapeake air. Nobody wrote a single note in a binder.

Twenty minutes into the period, the hallway door clicked open again.

An office runner stepped quietly to the projector cart and handed Mr. Hargrove a pink attendance slip. Mr. Hargrove glanced down at the handwritten line, exhaled once with visible relief on my behalf, and looked straight toward my desk.

"Gwen Lennox," he said softly. "Pack your bag—your grandfather's up at attendance signing you out."

I had my spiral notebooks zipped into my backpack and both charcoal Lofstrand crutches locked onto my forearms in five seconds flat.

Up at the crowded main-office counter—where twenty anxious mothers and fathers in work clothes, scrubs, and Navy uniforms stood signing clipboards shoulder to shoulder—seventy-nine-year-old Grandpa Walter stood straight-backed in his tan windbreaker and faded dark-blue Navy cap. Ever since his winter hospital stay for congestive heart failure, Grandpa walked noticeably slower and watched stairs carefully, yet the second I crutched through the double hallway doors, his steady gray eyes locked onto mine and his whole weathered face eased.

"Got your bag, kiddo?" Grandpa asked calmly, resting a warm, steadying hand between my shoulder blades as we walked out into the bright September glare toward his silver-tan four-door sedan parked near the curb ramp.

"Yeah, Grandpa—have you talked to Nolan?" I asked immediately as I slid into the front passenger seat and laid my charcoal crutches beside the console.

"Tried Blue Tech three times from the kitchen wall phone before driving over here, and tried the payphone outside the office," Grandpa said plainly, turning the ignition key and easing into the jammed school exit lane. "Fast-busy tone every single dial—every phone circuit in Tidewater's locked solid. We're swinging right by Blue Tech on the way home to lay eyes on your brother."

Driving over to Emerald Creek Parkway felt like crossing a town holding its breath—cars backed up around gas-station pumps, flags already drawing stares against an empty blue sky stripped completely bare of passenger planes, AM news chatter spilling from neighboring sedans at every red light.

When Grandpa pulled up against the yellow fire-lane curb outside Blue Tech and kept the engine idling, I clicked my charcoal crutches out onto the concrete and hurried through the automatic glass doors. Inside, the overhead music was completely off. Spotting me right away by Customer Service, Nolan's supervisor Mr. Halstead walked straight up to meet me before I even had to ask.

"You're Nolan's sister Gwen, right?" Mr. Halstead said gently, wiping tired eyes. "You just missed him by maybe eight or ten minutes. Somebody came in repeating a rumor that an unaccounted-for plane was heading toward Naval Station Norfolk—Nolan started shaking so badly behind the register I signed him out early and sent him home down Eden Way in his wheelchair."

My heart jumped straight into my throat—knowing better than anyone on Earth how deeply Grandpa's old Norfolk Navy years lived inside Nolan's head, and picturing my brother all alone on a sidewalk convinced Norfolk was next.

"Thank you so much," I blurted, turning cleanly on my crutch tips and hurrying straight back out to the silver-tan sedan. "Grandpa—his boss sent him home in his chair ten minutes ago after somebody started a rumor about Norfolk! Turn east down Eden Way North—he has to be right on the sidewalk between here and our street!"

Grandpa Walter didn't waste half a breath. Signaling cleanly out of the shopping-center apron, he turned east onto Eden Way North while I leaned forward against my seatbelt, scanning every yard of concrete sidewalk past the trimmed hedges and commercial driveways.

Barely three hundred yards ahead—right where Eden Way North met Merchants Way in front of the brick pillars of Emerald Creek Station and the locked glass storefront of the United States Coast Guard Recruiting Station—a stocky figure in a royal-blue Blue Tech work polo leaned low over the wheels of a manual wheelchair, silver forearm crutches rattling upright behind his shoulders as he pushed toward the curb cut with everything he had left.

"There he is!" I breathed, pointing straight past the windshield. "Right by the Coast Guard recruiting office!"

Grandpa eased the silver-tan sedan smoothly against the Merchants Way curb cut and tapped the horn twice.

Before Grandpa had barely shifted into park and popped the trunk latch, I was already out the passenger door on my charcoal crutches. Nolan looked up with red-rimmed, terrified hazel eyes, his hands still trembling on his steel push-rims—and the second I reached his chair, set one crutch against the fender, and wrapped my arm fiercely around his neck right there on the sidewalk outside Emerald Creek Station, a ragged breath broke out of my big brother's chest as he locked both arms around my waist and held on tight.

"Got you, Nolan—we've got you," I murmured fiercely against his hair while Grandpa stepped steadily around the rear bumper to fold Nolan's wheelchair into the trunk. "Grandpa's fine, I'm fine, and nobody hit Norfolk. Let's go home."

***

#### 11:45 AM — Clinging Together While the Recliner Creaks

Back inside our quiet two-story house off Eden Way North, with the front deadbolt turned and all four of our matte-silver and matte-charcoal Lofstrand forearm crutches lined up side by side against the family-room wall, the afternoon stretched long, heavy, and hushed.

Out on CSN, anchors had already walked back the earlier State Department car-bomb report as a false alarm, though nothing else on the screen offered an ounce of comfort—not the gray dust hanging over Lower Manhattan where two towers had stood when I walked into Latin III, not the dark gash in the Pentagon, not the confirmation from a field in Pennsylvania after earlier Camp David rumors cleared, and not the low, periodic rumble of armed patrols circling high above Hampton Roads.

Across the carpet in his worn brown leather recliner, Grandpa Walter lowered himself slowly back against the cushion with a long, quiet creak of springs—setting his faded Navy cap onto the end table right beside his afternoon pill bottle and a glass of tap water. He didn't say much at all. Resting both veined machinist hands flat along the leather armrests, seventy-nine-year-old Walter Lennox watched the news in solemn, unblinking silence, looking older in that gray television light than he had at breakfast—an old Norfolk sailor quietly absorbing an ugly morning he couldn't shield his grandchildren from.

Six feet away on the family-room sofa bed—the exact cushions we had shared since we were little kids visiting from our parents' one-story house—twenty-three-year-old Nolan and I sat tucked tight beneath Grandpa's dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket, clinging to each other without an inch of daylight between our shoulders. Every time another pair of military jets rumbled faintly over Chesapeake and a tremor tried to start up again in Nolan's fingers, I tightened both arms around my brother, rested my cheek firmly on top of his tousled hair, and stayed right there with him while the three of us watched the afternoon fade together.

### Part 13: Eighteen Candles, Always There for Big Brother, and Ten Raised Seals

**Date:** 2001-10-19 at 18:30 America/New_York

#### 6:30 PM — Eighteen Candles and Always There for Big Brother

Yellow wax dripped down eighteen crooked birthday candles stuck into a homemade chocolate sheet cake at the center of Grandpa Walter's kitchen table while Gwen beamed bright enough to light the whole Emerald Creek neighborhood by herself.

Grandpa Walter had kept his word. Even after his tired heart finally slipped onto home supplemental oxygen in late September—a few hard weeks after the three of us sat together watching the world change on September eleventh—he sat right at the head of the table in his pressed flannel shirt with his dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket tucked over his knees and his nasal oxygen tubing tucked neatly behind his ears, clapping slowly along as I finished singing off-key across the laminate tabletop. Gwen—officially eighteen years old since breakfast, six-foot-one even seated tall in her manual wheelchair—blew all eighteen flames out in a single gust before reaching sideways to pull me into a rib-crushing one-armed hug.

"Best birthday since I was ten," Gwen declared, eyes shining as she cut Grandpa the corner piece with extra frosting first. "Grandpa's at the table, my big brother's free of that trailer park off Route 13 for good, and nobody from city social services can ever put my name on a minor ward list."

Across the table, Grandpa Walter gave me one small, unmistakable nod.

My palms felt damp where I wiped them against my jeans knees. Because Tammy over at Pinecrest Court was stubbornly refusing to sign a roommate release—convinced I was going to come crawling back—my written non-renewal notice wouldn't clear my name off her trailer lease until midnight on November thirtieth, meaning I couldn't sign a new apartment lease until December first. Worse, spending the past month calling every modern apartment complex around Emerald Creek had taught me a cold math lesson: every suburban one- and two-bedroom near Blue Tech demanded paystubs showing three times monthly rent—over seven hundred and fifty dollars a month—leaving zero chance of qualifying on part-time retail hours. The one place in Hampton Roads where my paystubs actually cleared was right across the river in Norfolk: Studio 205, a three-hundred-ninety-five-dollar utilities-included second-floor efficiency inside Harborview Towers, a twenty-story nineteen-sixty-five high-rise overlooking The Hague inlet off West Brambleton Avenue right on CVTA Routes 2 and 11 and six blocks from Ghent High School. All afternoon a quiet knot of nervousness had sat under my ribs—wondering if six-foot-one Gwen would hear *rectangular one-person Norfolk studio with one bed and transferring schools mid-junior year* and wish she could go find roommates instead.

"Hey, Gwenny... my written notice at Pinecrest doesn't clear until the end of November, so I can't sign a lease on my own place until December first," I said, rubbing the back of my neck. "Every apartment around Emerald Creek wanted way more income than Blue Tech pays part-timers, so the one place I qualified for is across the river in Norfolk—Studio 205 on the second floor of Harborview Towers off West Brambleton Avenue, right on the two and eleven buses and six blocks from Ghent High School. It's tiny—literally an eleven-by-eighteen-foot box built for one person, only big enough for my one full-sized bed in the corner and a dorm fridge under the counter. Still, come December first... do you want to come live with me?"

Gwen stared at me across the frosted cake for half a second—and then let out a joyous gasp that made the forks jump on the placemats.

Without hesitating for a split second about waiting six weeks, switching out of the Chesapeake school district she had hated ever since elementary school, or squeezing into a tiny room, my newly eighteen-year-old sister unlocked her wheelchair brakes, rolled two feet around the table corner, and threw both arms around my neck so exuberantly she nearly tipped me backward off my kitchen chair.

"Are you serious?! Yes! A million times yes!" Gwen laughed, kissing the top of my head and hugging me with all her might, practically bouncing with excitement. "First you finally dump Tammy, and now you're asking me to live with you?! Nolan, I don't care if it's the size of a shoebox—we're living together, and I promise I'm always going to be there for my big brother!"

At the head of the table, Grandpa Walter reached both thin hands across the tablecloth to cover Gwen's clasped hand and mine. Every ounce of worry that had etched deep lines around his mouth for months seemed to smooth completely away in the warm kitchen light.

"Two grown Lennoxes under one roof come December," Grandpa whispered, his smile peaceful and whole. "Look after each other now."

***

#### 7:15 AM, The Next Morning — The Quiet Room and Ten Raised Seals

Grandpa Walter slipped away peacefully in his sleep sometime before six o'clock the next morning, his hands folded neat and calm atop his green-and-navy plaid wool blanket.

Knowing someone is dying prepares you for the grief, maybe ten percent. Nothing on Earth prepares a twenty-three-year-old autistic retail sales associate on forearm crutches—whose brain thrives on one clear task at a time—for the avalanche of vague, overlapping, emotionally loaded bureaucracy that crashes down on a house the hour the grandfather who gained custody of you and raised you stops breathing.

First came the morning itself: holding tight to Gwen's shaking hand at the living-room window while the visiting hospice nurse listened with her stethoscope, recorded seven-twelve in the morning on a carbon clipboard, called the city non-emergency desk so patrol cars wouldn't run sirens onto Grandpa's quiet street, and waited with us until two soft-spoken men in dark suits arrived from the Chesapeake funeral chapel. Before they wheeled Grandpa out the front door, Gwen gently lifted his dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket off the bedframe and held it folded tight against her chest like armor; she didn't put it down all morning.

By two o'clock that afternoon—burning the first of the measly three paid bereavement shifts Blue Tech corporate policy allowed—I was sitting on a stiff upholstered chair inside a rose-carpeted funeral home arrangement office with my silver forearm crutches propped against my thighs while an arrangement director slid glossy binders across a mahogany desk. Everything had a price tag designed to make exhausted families feel cheap if they blinked: bronze-trimmed caskets that cost more than half a year of my pre-tax Blue Tech register wages, memorial booklets, vault liners. Remembering Grandpa's manila folder word for word, I swallowed my dry throat, slid his modest burial-policy certificate and honorable veterans discharge across the blotter, and held firm on the simple, dignified cremation and graveside military honors Grandpa had written down—plus paying ten dollars apiece for ten official raised-seal copies of the Virginia death certificate.

"Ten copies?" Gwen whispered softly beside me in the hallway afterward, leaning close against my shoulder on her charcoal crutches. "Why so many?"

"Nurse warned me," I murmured tiredly, rubbing my forehead. "Nobody—not the bank, not the court, not the electric company—lets you close a single account without a raised seal."

She wasn't exaggerating. Over the next six weeks through Thanksgiving—while grieving Grandpa every night, rushing back onto the Blue Tech computer aisles in my royal-blue polo to save up December's security deposit and first month's rent, and knowing Tammy over at Pinecrest Court was still holding out refusing to sign an early release convinced grief would drive me back to her trailer—every free daylight hour turned into a mountain of clipboards, long hold queues, and brick courthouse counters.

Down at the Chesapeake Circuit Court clerk's office, I had to stand on my silver crutches at a chest-high marble probate window with Grandpa's original will, swear a formal executor's oath, and open an estate checking account just to pay bills legally. When I called the Social Security office with a death certificate number, a flat voice informed me that because Grandpa had died on the twentieth rather than living through the entire calendar month, the Treasury would automatically claw back his final direct-deposit check straight out of the bank—wiping out seven hundred dollars I had counted on to pay the final electric and natural-gas bills. Then came returning the leased medical oxygen concentrator before another monthly rental fee hit, canceling landline telephone and city water deadlines, filing final medical claim forms, and sitting across Grandpa's worn kitchen table with an estate real-estate agent as she laid out the payoff sheet on Grandpa's home-equity medical loan.

Just like Grandpa had warned me, the bank's ninety-day payoff clock was already ticking. On an hourly electronics-store wage, no mortgage underwriter in Virginia would let me refinance a six-figure medical home-equity balance or carry the taxes on a nearly three-thousand-square-foot upper-middle-class home. The big two-story house Grandpa Walter had bought years before ever taking custody of us had to go up for sale immediately—and forty years of his dining-room hutch, king bedroom suite, upstairs full beds, woodworking clamps in the two-car garage, dishes, wool coats, and bookshelves had to be priced, tagged, and cleared out in a weekend estate sale because none of a three-bedroom, three-bath two-story house could fit inside the eleven-by-eighteen-foot studio floorplan waiting for us on December first.

***

#### 10:40 PM, Mid-November — Not Carrying It Alone

Three weeks after the funeral, surrounded by taped cardboard boxes and bare picture-hook nails on Grandpa's living-room carpet while we counted down the final two weeks until December first, I finally hit a wall.

My wrists throbbed from six hours of standing on my silver forearm crutches at Blue Tech followed by four hours of trying to decode vague realtor seller disclosures and overlapping utility shutoff rules on the floor. Being neurotypical like Grandpa Walter had been, Gwen could hear what polite strangers on the phone actually meant between the lines; for me, juggling ten unfamiliar agencies with zero written checklist felt like static roaring louder and louder inside my skull. Staring at a final hospital billing envelope beside Grandpa's empty recliner spot, my vision blurred hot and sudden. I buried my face in both palms, shoulders hunching tight as that ugly voice from Tammy's trailer whispered in the back of my head that I wasn't built to handle real adult life.

"Hey—none of that," Gwen's warm, calm voice said instantly—never loud, never sharp, always steady.

Before I could even pretend I was just rubbing my eyes, six-foot-one Gwen lowered herself onto the carpet right beside me, wrapped Grandpa Walter's green-and-navy plaid wool blanket firmly across both of our shoulders with the exact gentle deep-pressure squeeze she knew quieted the static in my chest, and pulled me sideways against her in a fierce, steady two-armed hug.

"It's just too many moving pieces at once, Gwenny," I admitted thickly against her collarbone, ashamed my voice cracked at all. "Selling Grandpa's house out from under you, shutting off his phone number, packing your whole childhood down to four boxes for a tiny studio—you deserved way better than this for turning eighteen."

Gwen pulled back just enough to cup both sides of my face in her palms, looking straight into my eyes with clear, literal honesty and unmistakable joy shining right underneath our shared grief.

"Listen to me, Nolan Lennox," my eighteen-year-old sister said gently. "All two years while Tammy had you stuck over in that awful trailer off Route 13, I used to lie awake in my bedroom upstairs terrified because I know how your brain works—you take people at their word, you see the world honestly, and out in a neurotypical world full of double-talk and users, Grandpa Walter was always the one helping translate things for you. The morning Grandpa passed away, I made up my mind that job belongs to me now. Selling brick and drywall doesn't scare me one bit—I get my brother back, nobody is ever going to yell at you under our roof, and just like I promised you on my birthday, I'm always going to be there for my big brother. You and I are staying together for the rest of our lives. Now hand me the utility stack and open my laptop; I'll translate the phone scripts and put every single date in one clean list so we knock them out side by side."

True to her word, Gwen booted up her clunky gray laptop running Quantum OS 98 right there on an upturned packing crate beside me, drafted a neat checklist of final meter-read dates while leaning warm against my shoulder, and refused to go to sleep until every single envelope was stacked, stamped, and conquered together.

### Part 14: Moving Into Studio 205 and Quantum OS 2000

**Date:** 2001-12-01 at 16:15 America/New_York

![Inside tiny rectangular Studio 205 on the second floor of a 1965 Norfolk high-rise featuring Nolan's corner full-sized bed draped in Grandpa Walter's green-and-navy plaid blanket, a wooden dresser with a swiveled rabbit-ear CRT television and silver VCR/DVD combo aimed toward the bed, an unobstructed tiny kitchen with an under-counter dorm fridge, narrow natural-gas oven, and rolling microwave pantry cart, two early-2000s laptops, and two parked manual wheelchairs, barefoot twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox (5-foot-7) and his taller eighteen-year-old sister Gwen Lennox (6-foot-1) share a warm, joyful hug beside all four of their single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p002_two_names_studio_lease.jpg)

#### 4:15 PM — Zero Lease Overlap and Eleven Feet by Eighteen Feet of Freedom

At midnight on November thirtieth—after more than three straight months in which Tammy over at Pinecrest Court had stubbornly refused to sign the early roommate release, holding out past Thanksgiving convinced that burying Grandpa Walter and selling our house would send a lonely Nolan running back to her trailer—my brother's written non-renewal notice expired right on schedule and severed his name off Tammy's lease forever without Nolan dialing her number once.

By quarter past four on Saturday afternoon, December first, with the estate realtor's fresh *UNDER CONTRACT* rider hanging out on Grandpa Walter's front lawn in Chesapeake and zero lease conflicts left anywhere in Virginia, we walked out of the ground-floor leasing office of Harborview Towers—a twenty-story nineteen-sixty-five brick-and-concrete high-rise overlooking The Hague inlet off West Brambleton Avenue in Norfolk, right on CVTA Routes 2 and 11 and six blocks from Ghent High School where I was starting Monday morning to finish eleventh grade—holding two brand-new brass keys, unloaded Nolan's furniture out of a borrowed pickup from the second bay of Grandpa's garage, and unlocked Studio 205 together—where I took one look inside our tiny rectangular apartment, burst out laughing with pure happiness, and hugged my brother right in the centered entry doorway.

Nolan hadn't exaggerated by half an inch: Studio 205 was an eleven-by-eighteen-foot rectangular shoebox designed strictly for one solitary tenant who didn't own two manual wheelchairs.

Once we rode the groaning nineteen-sixty-five lobby elevator up to the second floor—the very last month that ancient elevator worked reliably before the landlord taped a yellow out-of-order notice across the call button—and stepped past the short left-hand entry closet return into our room, you could see our entire new life in a single sweep. Flush in the far left corner against two perpendicular walls beneath a small beige window air-conditioner sat Nolan's fifty-four-inch full-sized bed, draped immediately in Grandpa Walter's dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket to double as our bed by night and our only couch by day. Along the stepped-in front-right wall right inside the doorway stood Nolan's scuffed oak dresser—now split evenly, top two drawers mine, bottom two drawers his—topped by a chunky nineteen-inch television with V-shaped metal rabbit ears and a brushed-silver dual-deck VCR and DVD combo unit swiveled cleanly toward the corner bed so Nolan and I could watch movies together propped against the pillows or seated in our wheelchairs. Just past the dresser, where the right wall recessed two feet outward, both of our manual wheelchairs parked neatly beside a narrow three-foot pine bathroom door, while along the unobstructed back-right wall ran the tiniest functional kitchen in Virginia: a dorm-style mini-fridge tucked underneath the counter with clear floor space to swing the door wide, a narrow single sink, a compact natural-gas oven, and Nolan's white microwave perched on a rolling wire cart whose lower wire shelves doubled as our entire pantry of canned soup, rolled oats, peanut butter, and macaroni boxes.

"Okay, it's TINY," Nolan winced sheepishly on his matte-silver forearm crutches, glancing from the rolling microwave pantry cart to the single corner bed as old reflexes from Tammy's put-downs tried to hunch his shoulders inward. "I know you're six-foot-one, Gwenny, and squeezing down from Grandpa's two-story house and having your own full-sized bed upstairs to sharing one full bed and two dresser drawers is—"

I didn't even let him finish the apology. Stepping across the narrow strip of floor on my matte-charcoal crutches, I wrapped both arms around my five-foot-seven brother and rested my chin happily on top of his tousled brown head.

"Nolan, stop," I smiled, giving him a tight squeeze. "First off, you and I have been sharing Grandpa's queen pull-out sofa bed downstairs since we were little kids visiting from Mom and Dad's one-story house—and every single night our legs weren't feeling the stairs! Tammy isn't here anymore, Grandpa's mortgage bank is paid off once closing clears, and I'm living with my favorite person in the universe! Show me where the lease addendum is so I can put my name next to yours right now!"

Two minutes later, sitting cross-legged side by side on top of Grandpa Walter's plaid blanket on the corner full-sized bed, Nolan handed me a blue ballpoint pen off the microwave cart and watched proudly as I printed *Gwendolyn M. Lennox* in the co-tenant box right beside *Nolan W. Lennox*.

Nolan still thought I was doing him a favor by squeezing my six-foot-one frame into a one-room studio instead of hunting for college roommates my own age. What my sweet, literal-minded brother didn't realize yet was that staying right beside him was the biggest reason I had counted down the days to turning eighteen in the first place. Growing up neurotypical five years behind a verbal autistic big brother, I had watched Grandpa Walter spend our whole childhood quietly translating a loud, sharp-edged neurotypical world for Nolan—decoding hidden sales pitches, unspoken social traps, and people whose smiles didn't match their motives—and I had watched in heartbreak for two long years what happened when a woman like Tammy pulled Nolan out beyond Grandpa's reach. Standing beside Grandpa Walter's quiet bed the morning after my eighteenth birthday, I had decided in the deepest part of my heart that the task of helping Nolan navigate the world had passed from Grandpa's hands straight down into mine. This eleven-by-eighteen-foot room wasn't a temporary stopgap until one of us moved on. Nolan and I were staying together for the rest of our lives—sharing one roof, sharing one bed, cuddling through every storm, and never letting anyone come between us again.

***

#### 8:45 PM — Three-Foot Bathrooms, Learning Not to Hide, and Quantum OS 2000 Professional

Moving in together as two grown siblings inside a one-room rectangle came with exactly two immediate engineering puzzles—and we solved both before midnight.

The first puzzle announced itself right after supper when I carried my sleep shirt into the three-foot bathroom to change out of my jeans. At six-foot-one on forearm crutches, one elbow bumped the shower curtain rod, the other hit the towel hook, and the rubber ferrule of my left charcoal crutch wedged straight behind the radiator pipe—dropping me with a loud clatter onto the closed toilet lid.

"Gwenny? You okay in there?" Nolan called anxiously from beside the dorm fridge without barging in.

"Didn't fall," I laughed, opening the narrow door after pulling my lounge pants up seated. "Just confirmed nobody over five feet tall can balance on one foot inside that closet without breaking a collarbone. We're both adults now, Nolan, and if we hang a divider curtain across this tiny room neither wheelchair will ever turn. We're just going to have to get changed out here."

Nolan agreed quietly—yet ten minutes later, when he unbuttoned his royal-blue Blue Tech work polo beside his oak dresser under the swiveled rabbit-ear television, my heart pinched hard. Instantly my brother pulled his shoulders inward and angled his stocky five-foot-seven chest hurriedly toward the plaster wall, bracing himself as if waiting for Tammy's cruel voice from that trailer off Route 13 to sneer at his soft waist or the old childhood brace marks above his ankles.

Making a sad fuss would have made him feel stared at all over again. Instead, sitting comfortably on the corner bed unclipping my charcoal cuffs and reaching for my own sleep tee with zero rush or awkwardness, I kept my gaze warm, matter-of-fact, and completely unbothered on my brother's face.

"Hey, shortstop," I asked lightly, nodding toward the silver VCR and DVD combo deck glowing green on the dresser. "Want to put a VHS space tape or that synth concert DVD in while we unpack your cable box?"

Nolan paused halfway into his clean cotton t-shirt, blinking back over his shoulder when he realized I wasn't flinching, wasn't averting my eyes like something was wrong with him, and wasn't judging an inch of him. Right in front of me, that defensive hunch melted clean out of his shoulders. A quiet, genuine smile warmed his hazel eyes as he turned back into our room without hiding at all. If we were going to spend the rest of our lives side by side under one roof—sharing beds, cuddling close every night, and looking out for each other the way Grandpa Walter taught us—getting dressed together in our own room belonged right alongside sharing the kitchen sink or passing crutches grip-first, completely natural and unselfconscious from night one.

Ten minutes after that came puzzle number two: sitting propped beside Nolan on our shared corner bed, my chunky gray laptop let out a harsh speaker beep and froze dead on a bright blue fatal-exception screen right in the middle of my twin-moon orbital simulation.

"Stupid Quantum OS 98," I groaned as the Version 4.10.1998 splash screen crawled back up. "Thirty-two megs of memory and the whole kernel folds every night."

Nolan grinned from ear to ear, reached down to his zippered CD wallet beside his own smooth-running charcoal laptop on the milk-crate nightstand, and held up a gleaming silver retail disc straight from the Emerald Creek Blue Tech software cage on his employee discount: *Quantum OS 2000 Professional*.

"Scoot that dinosaur over here, astronomer," Nolan said happily, backing up my star-chart math onto a blank CD first before popping the *Quantum OS 2000 Professional* upgrade disc into my spinning drive. "Protected memory, rock-solid kernel, and zero nine-eight crashes all winter."

While blue setup bars marched steadily across my screen and Nolan—happily back in his element of clean technical routines—lined all four of our forearm crutches in exact height order beside the footboard before fine-tuning the V-shaped rabbit ears atop our swiveled television until snowy local broadcast cleared into a late-night nineteen-eighties fantasy movie, I curled right up against my big brother's side under Grandpa Walter's plaid wool blanket. Being neurotypical while growing up five years behind a verbal autistic big brother meant I had known my whole life how deeply Nolan thrived on plain truth, zero yelling, and quiet, predictable affection—and even Grandpa Walter and I had genuinely approved of polite, soft-spoken Tammy that first evening we drove out to pick Nolan up from her trailer when I was sixteen, long before her temper began to turn. Exhausted from weeks of funeral and house-sale chaos, broke as church mice, and crammed into a rectangular one-person box where nobody would ever play mind games with my brother again, I felt happier than I had been in two whole years just having Nolan safe beside me.

### Part 15: Clocking Out Together, One Car Ride Home, and Crimson Red Outside the Lobby

**Date:** 2002-02-12 at 19:45 America/New_York

![Outside the glass-walled lobby entrance of Harborview Towers in Norfolk on an overcast March afternoon in 2002, eighteen-year-old Gwen Lennox (6-foot-1, burgundy knit sweater and jeans, seated in her manual wheelchair with her matte-charcoal single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches stowed behind her backrest) warmly shakes hands with off-duty Mallory Walsh (5-foot-5, copper-auburn ponytail, thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, forest-green jacket over cream sweater) on the red-brick walkway while twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox sits beside Mallory's silver compact car in his manual wheelchair (matte-silver single-tube Lofstrand crutches in the rear pocket) blushing visibly crimson red across his cheeks and ears.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p015_crimson_red_harborview_lobby.jpg)

#### 7:45 PM — Half a Mile to Mall Ring Road and Three Buses in the Sleet

Freezing February sleet ticked hard against the silent window air-conditioner above our corner headboard while I wrapped both stiff, red-knuckled hands around a steaming mug of canned chicken broth to get the feeling back into my thumbs.

Ten weeks after unlocking Studio 205 on the second floor of Harborview Towers—a twenty-story nineteen-sixty-five brick-and-concrete high-rise overlooking The Hague inlet off West Brambleton Avenue at the edge of Ghent—living under our own Norfolk roof with Gwen was everything I had hoped for, and commuting back across the city line to the Emerald Creek Blue Tech was grinding my wrists and knees into dust.

On paper back in October, riding the bus from West Brambleton Avenue down to Chesapeake had looked manageable because I already knew the three-bus chain by heart: catch CVTA Route 2 or Route 11 five minutes into Downtown Norfolk, transfer to eastbound Route 20 out Virginia Beach Boulevard to Glenrock, and catch southbound Route 15 down Military Highway to Emerald Creek Mall. What looked way shorter on a folded paper map was the sprawling half-mile trek *after* stepping off the bus: rolling or hiking along Mall Ring Road from the unnumbered Emerald Creek Mall bus stop—planted directly across the ring drive from the weekday commuter Park and Ride shelter—out to the multi-lane traffic lights at Emerald Creek Parkway and Eden Way North, waiting through two long turn-arrow cycles just to cross six lanes of wet asphalt, and pushing all the way through the Marketcenter lot to the Blue Tech entrance. In January and February's post-holiday retail payroll drought—with corporate slashing every part-timer to fifteen hours a week across four-hour shifts—that meant nearly four hours of sleet, slow hydraulic wheelchair lifts, and shopping-center crosswalks just to bring home thirty-four dollars before taxes.

"Your wrists are shaking, Nolan," eighteen-year-old Gwen said quietly from our shared fifty-four-inch bed, tucking Grandpa Walter's dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket snug around my shoulders the second I sat down beside her in dry socks. Six blocks up through Ghent, Gwen had settled smoothly into finishing her eleventh-grade junior year at Ghent High School—thrilled to be out of the Chesapeake school district she still resented for holding her back a grade over childhood leg-surgery casts—yet watching me come home soaked to the shins was putting a deep crease between her eyebrows. "You spent almost four hours commuting in freezing rain today for a four-hour shift."

"Spring hours pick back up around Easter," I murmured automatically, resting my sore forearms across my lap.

"Forget Easter—look at the math on my screen right now, shortstop," Gwen said firmly, pivoting her upgraded Quantum OS 2000 Professional laptop on our milk-crate nightstand toward my eyes. "My computer-science teacher at Ghent High pinned this on the classroom corkboard this afternoon."

Across the glowing white browser window sat a staff-vacancy notice from the Community College of Hampton Roads—downtown Norfolk campus, inside the Martin Building.

*Part-Time Campus Computer Lab and Helpdesk Technician. Twenty-eight hours per week, Monday through Friday plus alternating Saturday morning open lab. Seated technical bench: imaging Quantum OS 2000 Professional workstations, maintaining NTFS student profiles, clearing network print queues, and assisting students at the third-floor counter.*

My breath caught halfway down the paragraph.

"Wait... the Martin Building downtown?" I blinked, running the transit map in my head instantly. "That's straight down Route 2 or Route 11 from right outside our lobby. One bus. Six minutes. Zero transfers."

"Seated workbench whenever your legs aren't feeling concrete, nearly double your winter hours at two dollars more an hour, and zero crossing six lanes of Emerald Creek Parkway in sleet," Gwen grinned, nudging my shoulder warmly with hers. "You know Quantum OS 2000 better than anybody outside Mallory Walsh, Nolan. Print your resume before supper gets cold."

***

#### 2:30 PM, Friday, March 1 — Both Off the Clock

Seventeen days later—after fixing a broken TCP/IP gateway and a hung network print spooler in four minutes flat during a hands-on practical interview at the CCHR Norfolk Campus, Ray Halstead happily giving the campus IT director a glowing ten-minute telephone reference praising two years of spotless diagnostics, accepting the job on the spot, and working every scheduled minute of a clean two-week notice at the Emerald Creek Blue Tech—my final Friday shift ended at two-thirty in the afternoon.

My legs had been aching stiffly since noon, so I was using my manual wheelchair with my two matte-silver Lofstrand forearm crutches stowed upright inside the black canvas pocket behind my backrest. Rolling back into the employee breakroom after turning my laminated register card in to Ray Halstead, I got the nicest surprise of my whole final week: Mallory Walsh was standing right beside the gray wall timeclock, sliding her own badge through the slot at the exact same minute.

*2:30 PM — OUT.*

Ever since last spring, talking with Mallory in that back breakroom corner when we were both off the clock had been my favorite half-hour of any workday. With both of us officially punched out—and me permanently off Store Four-Twenty-One's payroll—neither of us was in a hurry to stop talking. For ten easy minutes by the coat hooks while I pulled my navy windbreaker on over a plain sweater and Mallory slung her canvas tote over her shoulder, we traded jokes about how CCHR's thirty domain-joined workstations were going to feel like a library vacation compared to home-theater wall TVs.

Then Mallory glanced down at the wheels of my chair and out toward the gray March wind whipping across the Marketcenter parking lot.

"Hold on—you're about to push all the way out to the Eden Way North light, cross Emerald Creek Parkway, and cut around Mall Ring Road to the mall stop across from the Park and Ride just to ride three buses back to Norfolk, aren't you?" Mallory asked plainly, adjusting her thin silver wire-rimmed glasses.

"Just one last time," I smiled sheepishly. "Starting Monday morning it's six minutes on the two bus."

"How about zero last times?" Mallory said, twirling a small brass car key on her ring with a warm, unhurried smile. "We're both off the clock, you aren't in my department anymore, and my car's parked forty feet outside the side doors. Let me drive you home to Norfolk."

My ears warmed instantly. "Are you sure? Norfolk's completely opposite Kempsville in Friday afternoon traffic—"

"Positive," Mallory said easily. "Twenty-five minutes on the interstate with good company beats watching you cross six lanes of Parkway traffic in March wind by a mile. Come on."

Out by her clean silver four-door compact in the employee lot, I braced one hand cleanly on the roof frame to ease myself onto the front passenger seat while Mallory popped her trunk, clicked the cross-brace release on my manual wheelchair like she had studied mechanical diagrams of it, and lifted the folded frame—crutches still tucked snug in the rear backrest pocket—smoothly into the trunk without a single awkward fuss.

All twenty-five minutes up Interstate 64 and across Downtown Norfolk toward West Brambleton Avenue, the conversation never stalled once. Without store walkie-talkies or timeclock bells cutting us off every fifteen minutes, Mallory laughed freely at my CCHR interview stories, asked genuine questions about how Gwen liked Ghent High, and kept glancing over at road lights with a quiet, unmistakable warmth behind her glasses that made my pulse hum steady and bright all the way to The Hague.

***

#### 3:08 PM — Investigating From the Lobby and One Corner Bed

Inside the glass-walled ground-floor lobby of Harborview Towers, eighteen-year-old Gwen had gotten off her Ghent High accessible school bus two minutes earlier and was waiting in her manual wheelchair beside the brass mailbox bank for the slow nineteen-sixty-five lobby elevator to groan its way down from the twelfth floor.

Glancing idly out through the front lobby glass toward West Brambleton Avenue, my sister froze mid-blink.

Instead of rolling down the sidewalk from the Route 2 and Route 11 bus stop on the corner, I was sitting in the front passenger seat of an unfamiliar silver compact car pulled up right to our curb—and a five-foot-five young woman with a copper-auburn ponytail and wire-rimmed glasses had just popped the trunk, lifted my wheelchair onto the brick walkway, and locked both rear wheel brakes steady so I could transfer smoothly out of the passenger door into my seat.

Remembering Tammy all too vividly, Gwen didn't wait half a second for the elevator. Unlocking her wheelchair brakes, my six-foot-one little sister spun straight around from the elevator alcove, pushed through the automatic lobby doors, and rolled briskly out onto the front walkway to investigate who on Earth had driven her brother home.

"Nolan?" Gwen called out, green-hazel eyes scanning from the silver car to Mallory's hands steadying my armrest before landing squarely on my face.

Without an ounce of warning, heat surged straight up my neck until my entire face burned crimson red from collar to ears.

"Uh—hi, Gwenny!" I stammered, my voice jumping half an octave while my cheeks glowed like a stop sign. "We—uh—clocked out at the same time on my last shift, so I didn't have to walk over to the mall stop, and—Gwen, this is Mallory Walsh. Mallory, this is my sister Gwen."

Watching her normally calm twenty-three-year-old brother turn bright crimson red while looking at the exact copper-auburn ponytail and wire-rimmed glasses he had quoted at the supper table for ten straight months, Gwen's protective frown vanished into sheer, delighted comprehension in under two seconds flat.

"Wait—*you're* Mallory Walsh?! Protected-memory Quantum OS 2000 Mallory?!" Gwen lit up, rolling forward to shake Mallory's hand warmly while shooting me a sideways grin that only made my ears burn hotter. "Nolan talks about your breakroom kernel lectures practically every week! Thank you for saving him from that awful three-bus haul—do you have fifteen minutes to come upstairs and grab a soda or some water before fighting Friday tunnel traffic?"

"Gwenny, she probably needs to beat rush hour—" I started quickly, still glowing crimson red as every anxious thought about our tiny one-room apartment hit me at once.

"Fifteen minutes sounds great, actually," Mallory smiled warmly at Gwen, locking her car doors with a chirp. "And your elevator finally just dinged in the lobby."

All two floors up inside Harborview Towers' creaking wood-paneled elevator cab, both Gwen and I traded one silent, telepathic sibling look—because both of us knew what happened the second a stranger walked past the short entry closet return of Studio 205 and realized there was no hallway leading to a bedroom.

When I unlocked brass door *205* and held the centered white door open so Gwen could roll inside ahead of Mallory, you could take in our entire eleven-by-eighteen-foot life in one glance: my oak dresser on the immediate right with the nineteen-inch rabbit-ear television and brushed-silver VCR and DVD combo angled across the narrow hardwood strip, our tiny under-counter dorm fridge, single sink, compact gas stove, and rolling wire microwave-cart pantry in the back right, and tucked flush into the far left corner under the window air-conditioner, one single fifty-four-inch full-sized bed draped neatly in Grandpa Walter's dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket.

My face went right back to crimson red, and even confident six-foot-one Gwen sat up a fraction straighter in her chair—both of us bracing out of lifelong experience for a visitor to stare weirdly or get uncomfortable.

"It's definitely a one-person nineteen-sixties floorplan," Gwen explained upfront in a clear, matter-of-fact voice before any awkward silence could form, gesturing lightly toward the plaid-covered mattress in the corner. "Two wheelchairs and eleven feet wide means zero room for a sofa or a twin frame, so Nolan and I share the full bed—same way we grew up sharing Grandpa Walter's pull-out sofa downstairs."

"Yeah," I added honestly, rubbing the back of my warm neck beside the oak dresser. "Didn't want to catch you off guard."

Mallory stood in the center of our scuffed hardwood floor, looked once from Grandpa Walter's framed photograph beside the VCR clock to the two laptop crates by the footboard and the single plaid-covered corner bed—and didn't flinch, blink weirdly, or look uncomfortable for even half a second.

Behind her thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, her perceptive blue-gray eyes connected the whole picture effortlessly: Grandpa Walter's winter hospital lien forcing the sale of our childhood house, part-time electronics-store paystubs barely clearing a three-hundred-ninety-five-dollar Norfolk efficiency, and an eighteen-year-old sister in the middle of eleventh grade. Either brother and sister shared one bed under one safe roof, or Gwen was out on the street.

"Makes complete sense to me," Mallory said simply, her tone so natural and unbothered that both Gwen and I actually blinked in surprise. "If you hadn't shared the one bed that fit in here after your grandpa's house sold, Gwen wouldn't have had anywhere safe to live while finishing high school. Honestly, that's smart engineering—and your TV swivel angle to the pillows is spot-on."

Gwen's jaw dropped half an inch before breaking into the widest, most genuine smile I had seen on her face since Christmas. My own shoulders dropped two full inches in pure, stunned relief. Neither of us had ever met a single person outside Grandpa Walter who heard *grown brother and sister share one studio bed* and understood the real-world math immediately without making it weird.

Over cold glasses of tap water by our microwave pantry cart before Mallory headed down to beat Friday bridge traffic back to Virginia Beach, Mallory didn't rush toward the door once. In fact, right at the threshold of Studio 205—with Gwen grinning shamelessly behind my shoulder—Mallory paused, tapped the pocket spiral pad in her canvas bag where zero blue supervisor polo or collar pin stood between us anymore, and looked straight up into my hazel eyes.

"Hey, Nolan—I'm off tomorrow too," Mallory said plainly. "Would it be okay if I drove back up here tomorrow afternoon when neither of us has worked six hours first?"

Even through a fresh wave of warm pink climbing my cheeks, I didn't hesitate for a millisecond.

"Yeah," I beamed. "Tomorrow afternoon sounds great."

### Part 16: Coming Back the Next Day, Hot Cocoa in Studio 205, and Zero Supervisor Pin

**Date:** 2002-03-02 at 14:20 America/New_York

![Inside tiny rectangular Studio 205 on a rainy Saturday afternoon in March 2002 in Norfolk, twenty-three-year-old Nolan Lennox (5-foot-7, heather-navy tee and jeans) and Mallory Walsh (5-foot-5, copper-auburn ponytail, thin silver wire-rimmed glasses, off-duty forest-green zip cardigan over a cream tee, jeans, and sneakers) sit warmly side by side on the edge of the corner full-sized bed draped in Grandpa Walter's green-and-navy plaid wool blanket beside two wooden crates and both early-2000s laptops while eighteen-year-old Gwen Lennox (6-foot-1, heather-burgundy tee and jeans) smiles approvingly from her manual wheelchair with a mug of hot cocoa, four single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches leaning neatly against the footboard, the unobstructed dorm-fridge kitchen and rolling microwave pantry cart in the back, and the swiveled rabbit-ear CRT television and silver VCR/DVD combo on the oak dresser.]({{CDN}}/raggiesoft-books/images/scenes/waystation/e001/e001_c001_p015_mallory_visits_studio_205.jpg)

#### 2:20 PM — She Actually Came Back

Steady Saturday afternoon rain pattered against the window glass above our corner headboard when the downstairs lobby buzzer crackled on the wall beside our entry closet at twenty past two—right on the exact minute Mallory Walsh had promised on the phone that morning.

"Told you she wasn't just being polite yesterday!" Gwen grinned triumphantly from her manual wheelchair beside our oak dresser, tossing me a clean kitchen towel after I finished wiping down the rolling wire microwave-cart counter on my forearm crutches.

Up until that buzz sounded, a tiny, cautious part of my brain had half-wondered overnight if Friday afternoon had been too good to be true. Plenty of polite people in Tidewater could smile nicely for ten minutes inside a cramped three-hundred-ninety-five-dollar efficiency, nod politely at two wheelchairs and one shared corner bed, and then quietly invent busy weekend excuses forever afterward. Instead—even after Harborview Towers' temperamental nineteen-sixty-five lobby elevator blew a relay again at ten o'clock Saturday morning, bringing the landlord's yellow out-of-order tape right back onto the brass doors—Mallory had driven all the way back across the Elizabeth River through March rain and climbed the twenty-two terrazzo stairs up to our second-floor landing.

When I opened door *205*, seeing Mallory standing there completely off-duty—wearing a cozy forest-green zip cardigan over a plain cream t-shirt, blue jeans, and dry sneakers, rain specks dotting her thin silver wire-rimmed glasses and zero royal-blue supervisor polo or store lanyard anywhere in sight—made my chest feel lighter than helium.

"Twenty-two stairs confirmed—counted every riser on the way up," Mallory smiled warmly, stepping inside out of the hallway draft as naturally as if she had visited our little Norfolk rectangle a dozen times already. "Smells like hot cocoa in here."

"Fresh off the microwave cart—and zero fish sticks in sight," Gwen laughed, handing Mallory a steaming ceramic mug before settling comfortably back in her wheelchair beside the dresser while my own manual wheelchair rested neatly near the white bathroom door.

Just like Friday afternoon, Mallory didn't treat our tiny space like something fragile or embarrassing. Once I leaned all four of our single-tube Lofstrand forearm crutches—my shorter matte-silver pair and Gwen's taller matte-charcoal pair—neatly in height order against the wooden footboard, Mallory sat right down beside me on the edge of our shared full-sized bed atop Grandpa Walter's dark-green and navy-blue plaid wool blanket, both sneakers planted comfortably on the honey-oak floorboards right next to our two wooden milk crates and both closed laptops.

Within three minutes, Gwen popped open the lid of her upgraded laptop on the crate beside Mallory's knee, and the two of them were deep into pure astronomy-and-kernel heaven.

"Wait—is that a three-to-two binary orbital resonance loop running live on Two-Thousand Professional without spiking your processor?" Mallory asked, leaning six inches closer as her blue-gray eyes lit up behind her lenses with genuine craft delight. "Did you code the gravity time-step yourself, Gwen?"

"Wrote it in December the week Nolan upgraded me off ninety-eight!" Gwen beamed, holding her cocoa mug in both hands while angling the LCD screen toward Mallory and me so we could watch the large pale moon and its smaller amber companion trace smooth, unbroken ellipses. "Before Nolan brought home that disc you recommended in the breakroom, the hybrid sixteen-bit kernel blue-screened every twenty minutes whenever my floating-point table rolled over."

"Protected ring-zero memory saves astrophysics," Mallory declared happily, bumping her shoulder lightly and warmly against mine on the plaid blanket.

For over an hour while cold March rain drummed steadily outside our window air-conditioner and the table lamps cast a warm golden glow across our eleven-by-eighteen-foot lifeboat, the three of us talked as effortlessly as old friends—covering Gwen's Ghent High Latin translations, my upcoming Monday morning start down Route 2 at the CCHR Norfolk Campus computer lab, and every terrible Y2K panic story Mallory had survived back at Store Four-Twenty on Independence Boulevard. Halfway through Mallory laughing at Gwen's impression of our building superintendent blaming nineteen-sixties elevator relays on humid weather, Gwen caught my eye from her wheelchair, smiled from ear to ear, and gave me the most unmistakable sisterly nod of approval on Earth.

***

#### 4:05 PM — Plain Script on the Second-Floor Landing

When the afternoon rain finally eased to a soft drizzle over The Hague inlet and Mallory stood up to head home before dusk, I clicked my forearms into my matte-silver crutches to walk her out onto the second-floor corridor landing by the top of the stairwell.

Out in the quiet hallway with door *205* pulled half-shut behind us, Mallory turned around, tucked a loose strand of copper-auburn hair behind her right ear where her blue work pen usually rode, and looked straight up into my hazel eyes with zero riddles or subtext between us.

"Thank you for inviting me back today, Nolan—and tell Gwen her cocoa and orbital math are both ten out of ten," Mallory said softly. Then faint pink warmed the freckles across the bridge of her nose, though her voice stayed steady and crystal-clear. "Also... now that neither of us has clocked into Blue Tech for twenty-five hours and thirty-five minutes, I wanted to tell you something plainly so you never have to guess."

My pulse gave a quick, hopeful jump under my heather-navy t-shirt. "Plainly is definitely my best language."
